# Opioids

<!-- PDF page 1 -->


KAZUHIKO FUKUDA

|**KEY POINTS**|ᑏOpioids are a vital part of the analgesic component of anesthesia and often form the<br>foundation for postoperative pain management.<br>ᑏOpioids suppress pain by targeting multiple sites throughout the nervous system including<br>action in brain, spinal cord, and peripheral nervous systems.|
|---|---|
||ᑏAn increased understanding of the molecular pharmacology of opioid receptors and opioid-<br>induced cellular responses allows utilization of innovative techniques for analgesia and<br>development of novel opioid analgesics with fewer side effects.|
||ᑏOpioids also affect multiple organ systems, including the respiratory and cardiovascular<br>systems, and can cause a variety of adverse effects. Proper dosing and monitoring may allow<br>these adverse effects to be minimized. Understanding of the mechanism of the adverse effects<br>will contribute to better management of patients.|
||ᑏPharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic properties of opioids are affected by a variety of<br>factors, such as age, body weight, organ failure, shock, and drug interactions. To properly<br>administer opioids, these factors should be taken into consideration.<br>ᑏAlthough new opioid delivery systems such as transdermal patches provide certain clinical<br>advantages, they can also pose additional risks such as respiratory depression.<br>ᑏOpioid analgesics serve a critical role in acute pain management but their role in the long- term<br>treatment of chronic non- cancer pain has been called into question due to increased risks of<br>overdose, addiction, and opioid use disorders.|


## Introduction

The remarkable beneficial effects of opioids, as well as their toxic side effects and addictive potential, have been known for centuries. The term opioid refers broadly to all compounds related to opium. The word opium originates from _opos_ , the Greek word for juice, the drug being derived from the juice of the opium poppy, _Papaver somniferum_ . In contrast, the term opiate refers to natural products derived from the opium poppy and includes morphine, codeine, and thebaine.

The first undisputed reference to opium is found in the writings of Theophrastus in the third century BCE. During the Middle Ages, many of the uses of opium were appreciated. Opium contains more than 20 distinct alkaloids. In 1806, Sertürner reported the isolation of a pure substance in opium that he named morphine, after Morpheus, the Greek god of dreams. By the middle of the 19th century, the use of pure alkaloids rather than crude opium preparations began to spread throughout the medical world. Since then, there have been ongoing efforts to develop semisynthetic and synthetic opioid analgesics without the adverse side effects. Unfortunately, many of the synthetic opioids share side effects of natural opioids. The search for new opioid agonists led to the synthesis of opioid antagonists and compounds with mixed agonist/antagonist properties, which further expanded therapeutic options and provided important tools for exploring mechanisms of opioid actions.

Although new methods of opioid administration, including patient- controlled analgesia (PCA), computer- based infusion techniques, and transdermal patches, have been developed, opioids continue to act on common binding sites throughout the nervous system.

## Pharmacology of Opioids

### CLASSIFICATION OF OPIOID COMPOUNDS

From the point of view of chemistry, opioids can be classified as naturally occurring, semisynthetic, and synthetic. The naturally occurring opioids can be divided into two chemical classes, phenanthrenes (morphine and codeine), and benzylisoquinolines (papaverine). The semisynthetic opioids are morphine derivatives in which one of several changes has been made. Synthetic opioids are classified into four groups, the morphinan derivatives (levorphanol), the diphenyl or methadone derivatives (methadone, _d_ - propoxyphene), the benzomorphans (phenazocine, pentazocine), and the phenylpiperidine-related opioids (meperidine, fentanyl, sufentanil, alfentanil, and remifentanil). Structures of opioid compounds are shown in Fig. 22.1 and Table 22.1.<sup>1</sup>

Pharmacologically, opioids can be classified as agonists, partial agonists, mixed agonist–antagonist, and antagonists based on their interaction with the opioid receptors.

### BASIC STUDIES ON OPIOID RECEPTORS

In 1973, three independent teams of investigators described the presence of opioid binding sites in the nervous system from the radioligand binding assays. From pharmacologic experiments, three types of opioid receptors were postulated. They were named μ for the morphine type, κ for the ketocyclazocine type, and σ for the SKF10047 ( _N_ - allylnormetazocine) type. In addition, a high-affinity receptor for enkephalins was found in the mouse vas deferens and was named the δ- receptor. Furthermore, the ε- receptor was proposed as the binding site for β- endorphin in the rat vas deferens.

Since the early 1990s, molecular biologic studies have elucidated the molecular structures and signal transduction mechanisms of the opioid receptors. Four different complementary DNAs (cDNAs) have been isolated as members of the opioid receptor family.<sup>2</sup> Investigators demonstrated that three of them correspond to the pharmacologically defined μ- , δ- , and κ- opioid receptors, but the fourth receptor did

not bind with opioid ligands with high affinities. Later, a novel peptide, nociceptin, was identified as an endogenous agonist of the fourth member of the opioid receptor family.<sup>3,4</sup> The μ- , δ- and κ- opioid receptors and the nociceptin receptor share approximately 50% amino acid sequence homology with each other. Characteristics of three opioid receptors and the nociceptin receptor are listed in Table 22.2. Hydropathy analysis of the primary structures of the opioid receptors predicted that the opioid receptors possess seven transmembrane domains (Fig. 22.2), which is a characteristic structural feature of G- protein- coupled receptors including the adrenergic receptor and the muscarinic acetylcholine receptor. Crystallographic analysis revealed that the μ- , δ- and κ- opioid receptors and the nociceptin receptor actually possess seven transmembrane domains and the ligand binds deeply within a large pocket<sup>5</sup> (Fig. 22.3). Furthermore, analysis of the crystal structure of the opioid receptor revealed agonist- induced conformational changes

in detail, providing the basis for the development of new ligands.<sup>6</sup>

The σ (sigma) receptor, which was previously classified as a member of the opioid receptors, was shown to be an endoplasmic reticulum- resident protein, and has been implicated in many diseases, ranging from cocaine or alcohol addiction to familial adult or juvenile amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. The amino acid sequence of the σ1- receptor does not resemble that of any other mammalian proteins.<sup>7</sup>

Further pharmacologic classification of the μ- receptor as the μ1, μ2, and μ3 subtypes has been proposed, but the molecular identity of these receptor subtypes has not been clarified. Several possible molecular models of opioid receptor subtypes exist, including alternate splicing of a common gene product, receptor dimerization, and interaction of a

common gene product with other receptor or signaling molecules.<sup>8</sup> Multiple μ- opioid receptor species can be produced from a gene product of the single μ- opioid receptor gene by alternate splicing (Fig. 22.4).<sup>9,10</sup> Analysis of the alternate splicing products reveals differences in ligand binding and G- protein activation. However, their physiologic, pharmacologic, or clinical implications remain to be elucidated. Interestingly, it was reported that the analgesic action of 3- iodobenzoyl- 6β- naltrexamide, a potent analgesic against thermal, inflammatory, and neuropathic pain, is mediated by its interaction with mMOR- 1G, a truncated splice variant possessing only six transmembrane segments, in mice.<sup>11</sup>

G- protein- coupled receptors can form dimers – that is, homodimers (same receptor molecules) or heterodimers (different receptor molecules). The existence of these homodimers

and heterodimers has been shown in cultured cells and _in vivo._ Among opioid receptors (μ, δ, κ, and nociception receptors), various combinations of the molecules have been shown to form dimers, and the dimer formation has been shown to affect the ligand-binding properties and signal transduction mechanisms.<sup>12</sup> Physiologic and clinical implications of dimer formation of opioid receptors has been analyzed. For example, it is interesting that a bivalent ligand containing μ- agonist and δ- antagonist pharmacophores effectively bridges μ- δ opioid receptor heterodimers and exhibits enhanced efficacy and

a reduced tendency for tolerance in mice.<sup>13</sup> However, in the peripheral nervous system, μ- and δ- receptors are apparently expressed in different sensory neuronal subpopulations and do not form heterodimers.<sup>14,15</sup>

### GENETIC VARIATIONS WHICH INFLUENCE OPIOID EFFECTS

A lot of single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) have been identified in the human μ- opioid receptor gene<sup>16</sup> (Fig. 22.5).

The A118G mutation, the most common of these SNPs, leads to a change in the gene product in the human μ- opioid receptor molecule, which is an A- to- G substitution in exon 1 that results in an amino acid exchange at position 40 from asparagine to aspartate (N40D). Various clinical impacts of the A118G mutation have been reported. Patients with cancer who are homozygous for the A118G variant required higher doses of oral morphine for long- term treatment of their pain.<sup>17</sup> A118G mutation of the human μ- opioid receptor gene reduces analgesic responses to morphine- 6- glucuronide (M6G), but it does not significantly affect the respiratory depression induced by M6G.<sup>18</sup> Furthermore, morphine consumption with intravenous PCA after total abdominal hysterectomy is significantly greater in women homozygous for the A118G variant than in other patients.<sup>19</sup> In a meta- analysis involving 18 studies and more than 4600 patients, carriers of A118G were observed to exhibit higher opioid analgesic requirements.<sup>20</sup> Another metaanalysis showed significant association between A118G polymorphism and susceptibility to opioid dependence or addiction in Asian populations.<sup>21</sup>

_In vitro_ experiments show that the variant receptors with A118G mutation are associated with higher binding affinity to and potency of β- endorphin, but lower potency of morphine.<sup>22</sup> Studies in mouse models with analogous substitution of human A118G showed reduced analgesic response to morphine in some regions of the mouse brain with the GG genotype when compared with the AA

<!-- PDF page 6 -->

genotype.<sup>23</sup> A study also showed that 118A messenger RNA was 1.5- to 2.5- fold more abundant than the 118G messenger RNA in heterozygous human brain autopsy tissues.<sup>24</sup>

Overall, these findings suggest that the 118G allele may result in changes in abundance and/or function of the μ- opioid receptor, leading to differences in antinociceptive potency of opioid analgesics. The genetic difference may affect the dose of opioids necessary for analgesia, and in turn could misdirect health care providers in providing the most effective and safe opioid- based analgesic plan. The C17T mutation of the human μ- opioid receptor, located in exon 1, causes an amino acid exchange from alanine to valine at receptor protein position 6 of the extracellular receptor terminal. This mutation has been reported to occur in a higher overall proportion of opioid- dependent persons, but the effects of the C17T polymorphism on analgesic responses are unknown. A study using cultured cells reported that adenylate inhibition via the C17T mutant receptor was decreased for many opioids, including the clinically significant drugs morphine, buprenorphine, and

fentanyl, as well endogenous opioids,<sup>25</sup> suggesting that this polymorphism may affect individual responses to opioid therapy.

It is known that genetic variation of genes other than opioid receptor genes can affect sensitivity to opioids. It has been demonstrated that the Val158Met polymorphism of the human catechol- _O_ - methyltransferase (COMT) gene has contributed to differences in opioid consumption in patients treated for chronic cancer pain,<sup>26</sup> and is associated with variability in opioid consumption in postoperative nephrectomy patients.<sup>27</sup> COMT metabolizes biogenic amines including catecholamines such as dopamine, epinephrine, and norepinephrine (NE), thereby acting as a key modulator of dopaminergic and adrenergic neurotransmission, and can affect the pharmacologic effects of opioids. It was shown that the polymorphism of COMT can affect the phenotype of the polymorphism of the μ- opioid receptor. Kolesnikov and associates have demonstrated that the heterozygous patients with μ- opioid receptor A118G and COMT G1974A mutation consumed significantly less morphine for postoperative analgesia compared with homozygous patients of A118.<sup>28</sup>

### ENDOGENOUS OPIOID PEPTIDES

Enkephalin, β- endorphin, and dynorphin were identified as endogenous agonists for the δ- , μ- , and κ- opioid receptors, respectively. Following purification of these peptides from mammalian tissues, cDNAs for the precursors of these peptides were cloned and their primary structures were elucidated (Fig. 22.6).<sup>29</sup> It was suggested that the cleavage of preproopiomelanocortin produces not only β- endorphin but also several other neuropeptides including methionineenkephalin, adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), and α- melanocyte stimulating hormone. Amino acid sequence of preproenkephalin indicates that six methionine- enkephalins and one leucine- enkephalin are cleaved from this precursor molecule. Furthermore, the primary structure of preprodynorphin, the precursor of dynorphin, gives rise to dynorphins and neoendorphins, which are ligands for the κ- opioid receptor.

Nociceptin, a novel endogenous opioid peptide with a significant sequence homology to dynorphin, was isolated in 1995<sup>3,4</sup> and so named because it lowered the pain threshold under certain conditions in contrast to other endogenous opioid peptides. Pharmacologic and physiologic studies have demonstrated that nociceptin has behavioral and pain modulatory properties distinct from those of the three classic opioid peptides.<sup>30</sup> Prepronociceptin, the precursor of nociceptin, was cloned, and its amino acid sequence suggested the existence of prepronociceptin- derived neuropeptides other than nociceptin.<sup>31</sup>

The search for endogenous ligand binding with the μ- receptor with high affinity and high selectivity led to the discovery of a class of novel endogenous opioids termed endomorphin- 1 and endomorphin- 2.<sup>32</sup> These peptides are tetrapeptides with the sequence Tyr- Pro- Trp- Phe and Tyr- ProPhe- Phe, respectively. The endomorphin gene has yet to be cloned, and much remains to be learned about the anatomic distribution, mode of interaction with the opioid receptors, function _in vivo_ , and potential existence of other related peptides that are highly selective for each of the opioid receptors. It was recently demonstrated that both endomorphin- 1 and endomorphin- 2 given centrally and peripherally produced potent antiallodynic activities in a mouse model of neuropathic pain,<sup>33</sup> and downregulation of endomorphin- 2 correlates with mechanical allodynia in a rat bone cancer pain model,<sup>34</sup> suggesting the analgesic function of these peptides _in vivo_ .

### INTRACELLULAR SIGNAL TRANSDUCTION MECHANISM OF OPIOID RECEPTORS

The opioid receptors belong to the G- protein- coupled receptor family. It has been demonstrated that activation of the opioid receptors leads to activation of the pertussis toxinsensitive G proteins (Gi or Go or both). Expression of the cloned opioid receptors in cultured cells by transfection of the cloned cDNAs has facilitated analysis of the intracellular signal transduction mechanisms activated by the opioid receptors<sup>2</sup> (Fig. 22.7). **Fig. 22.7 Intracellular signal transduction mechanisms linked with the opioid receptors.** Opioid agonists bind with the opioid receptors, leading to activation of the G protein. Activity of adenylate cyclase and the voltage- dependent calcium (Ca<sup>2+</sup> ) channels is suppressed. On the other hand, inward rectifier potassium (K<sup>+</sup> ) channels and mitogen- activated protein kinase (MAPK) cascade are activated. _AMP_ , adenosine monophosphate; _ATP_ , adenosine triphosphate.

by opioid receptor activation, with a resulting reduction of the cellular cyclic adenosine monophosphate (AMP) content. Long- term tolerance to opioids has been thought to be associated with superactivation of adenylyl cyclase activity, which is a counterregulatory response to the decrease in cyclic AMP levels seen after acute opioid administration. Electrophysiologically, the voltage-dependent Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel is inhibited and the inwardly rectifying potassium (K<sup>+</sup> ) channels are activated by the opioid receptors. As a result, neuronal excitability is reduced by activation of the opioid receptors. The other regulatory components also participate in the coupling of opioid receptor binding and the cellular response. It was shown that extracellular signal- related kinase, a class of mitogen- activated protein kinases, is activated by the opioid receptors.<sup>35</sup> Opioid- induced activation of extracellular signal- related kinase can lead to increase in arachidonate release<sup>35</sup> and expression of immediate early genes, c- fos and junB.<sup>36</sup>

Chronic exposure of the opioid receptors to agonists induces cellular adaptation mechanisms, which may be involved in opioid tolerance, dependence, and withdrawal symptoms. A number of kinases, including protein kinase A and β- adrenergic receptor kinase (BARK), a member of the G- protein- coupled receptor kinase (GRK), have been shown to affect the function of the opioid receptors after activation by the agonist.<sup>37</sup> BARKs selectively phosphorylate agonist- bound opioid receptors and thereby promote interactions with β- arrestins, which interfere with G- protein coupling and promote receptor internalization. β- Arrestin 2 functions as a scaffolding protein that interacts with signal transducers, and the recruitment of β- arrestin 2 induced by opioid receptor activation is involved in the regulation of activity of c- Src, Akt, and mitogen- activated protein kinases<sup>38</sup> (Fig. 22.8). The c- Src inhibitor, dasatinib,

attenuated and reversed morphine- induced tolerance in mice, suggesting that c- Src recruited by β- arrestin 2 is involved in the morphine- induced tolerance.<sup>39</sup> Acute morphine- induced analgesia was enhanced in mice lacking β- arrestin 2, suggesting that this protein contributes to regulation of responsivity to opioids _in vivo._<sup>40</sup> Therefore, recruitment of β- arrestin serves a critical role in the regulation of the coupling between agonist binding to opioid receptors and their ability to develop and sustain an analgesic response. Like other G- protein- coupled receptors, the opioid receptors can undergo rapid agonist- mediated internalization by a classic endocytic pathway.<sup>41,42</sup> These processes may be induced differentially as a function of the class of the ligand. For example, certain agonists, such as etorphine and enkephalins, cause rapid internalization of the μ- receptor, whereas morphine, which decreases adenylyl cyclase activity equally well, does not cause μ- receptor internalization in all cell types such as HEK293 cells.<sup>43</sup> These findings may suggest that different ligands induce different conformational changes in the receptor molecule that lead to divergent intracellular events, and may provide an explanation for differences in the efficacy and abuse potential of various opioids.<sup>44</sup>

### BIASED AGONISM

Chemically distinct ligands binding to the same G- proteincoupled receptor can stabilize the receptor in multiple active conformations. In this way, differential activation of G- protein- coupled cell signaling pathways may produce divergent physiologic outcomes, a phenomenon known as biased agonism. Biased agonism can be exploited to design drugs that selectively activate desired signaling pathways, while minimizing other signaling pathways

via the same receptor subtype and associated side effects. Morphine binds to the μ- opioid receptor protein to form an active complex with signaling proteins, including Gi/o and β- arrestin. The Gi/o signaling pathway was thought to mediate analgesic action of morphine, whereas β- arrestin signaling results in unwanted side effects including euphoria, which can lead to addiction, respiratory depression, and gastrointestinal effects (Fig. 22.9). Consistently, it was reported that the analgesic properties of morphine were enhanced but morphine- induced respiratory depression and constipation were attenuated in β- arrestin 2 knockout mice.<sup>45</sup> TRV130, a “G-protein- biased” μ- opioid ligand with G- protein- coupling efficacy similar to that of morphine, but markedly reduced receptor phosphorylation, recruitment of β- arrestin 2 and internalization, was tested in a randomized, double- blind, placebo- controlled crossover study in healthy volunteers.<sup>46</sup> As expected, TRV130 produced greater analgesia than morphine, but less respiratory depression and less severe nausea. Manglik and associates computationally screened 3 million molecules, and found that the compound PZM21 with high Gi/o- biased signaling suppressed pain in mice without constipation, respiratory depression, hyperlocomotion, and addiction- related behaviors.<sup>47</sup> However, Hill _et al._ reported that the adverse-effect profile was not significantly different between morphine and PZM21 in mice.<sup>48</sup> Furthermore, contrary to the previous report,<sup>45</sup> it was recently reported that the morphine- induced

<!-- PDF page 10 -->

### MECHANISM OF OPIOID ANALGESIA

Opioid receptors are widely expressed in the central nervous system (CNS), including medulla, locus coeruleus, periaqueductal gray (PAG), limbic system, cerebral cortex, and spinal cord. They are also distributed in the peripheral tissues. Mechanism of opioid analgesia can be classified into brain, spinal cord, and peripheral mechanisms<sup>50</sup> (Fig. 22.10).

### Brain

Pain control by opioids needs to be considered in the context of brain circuits modulating analgesia and the functions of the various types of receptors in these circuits.<sup>51</sup> Nociceptive information ascending from the spinal cord activates a large interconnected neural network in the brain, and induces a variety of responses including pain perception, pain modulation, and affective response. Opioid receptors and peptides are expressed in these neural networks and affect these various aspects of pain responses.<sup>51</sup> It is well established that the analgesic effects of opioids arise from their ability to directly inhibit the ascending transmission of nociceptive information from the spinal cord dorsal horn and to activate pain control circuits. Opioid actions at PAG block release of the inhibitory transmitter γ-aminobutyric acid (GABA) from tonically active PAG system that regulate activity in projections to the medulla, which in turn releases

(From Al- Hasani R, Bruchas MR. Molecular mechanisms of opioid receptor- dependent signaling and behavior. _Anesthesiology._ 2011;115:1363–1381.)

norepinephrine and serotonin and modulates nociceptive transmission at the dorsal horn of the spinal cord through the action of the descending inhibition pathway.

Petrovic and colleagues used an experimental pain model and positron emission tomography (PET) to study mechanisms of action of the short- acting μ- opioid agonist remifentanil and found drug- induced activation of the rostral anterior cingulate cortex, insula, orbitofrontal cortex, and brainstem areas.<sup>52</sup> The brainstem activated by the opioid overlapped with brain areas that have been implicated in pain modulation, such as PAG. Interestingly, placebo analgesia acts similarly on the activity of these brain areas, presumably by endogenous opioid release.<sup>53</sup>

It is well known that opioid actions in the forebrain contribute to analgesia, because decerebration prevents analgesia when rats are tested for pain sensitivity using the formalin test,<sup>54</sup> and microinjection of opioids into several forebrain regions are analgesic.<sup>55</sup> Analgesia induced by systemic administration of morphine in the formalin test was disrupted either by lesioning or reversibly inactivating the central nucleus of the amygdala, thereby demonstrating that the opioid action in the central nucleus of the amygdala contributes to analgesia after tissue damage.<sup>56</sup>

### Spinal Cord

Analgesic actions of systemic morphine are in part mediated by a net inhibitory effect of the descending inhibition pathway on nociceptive processing in the spinal dorsal horn. It was demonstrated that morphine increased 5-hydroxytryptamine (5-HT; serotonin) and NE release in the spinal dorsal horn by activation of serotonergic and noradrenergic neurons in the medulla. Intrathecal pretreatment with ondansetron attenuated the analgesic effect of morphine in normal rats, suggesting the involvement of the 5- HT3 serotonergic receptor in morphine analgesia.<sup>57</sup> It was also reported that the acute analgesic effect of morphine in the tail- flick test of rats was reduced significantly by pretreatment with a single dose of yohimbine, an α2- adrenergic antagonist, suggesting the involvement of α2- adrenergic receptor in the analgesic effect of morphine.<sup>58</sup> Furthermore, it was reported that an adenosine receptor antagonist blocks the analgesic effect of intrathecally administered morphine,<sup>59</sup> suggesting the involvement of the adenosine receptor in the analgesic action of opioids,<sup>60</sup> and was postulated that endogenous opioid peptide released by the descending inhibitory system directly stimulates the opioid receptor or indirectly stimulates adenosine release in the spinal cord to produce an analgesic effect.<sup>61</sup>

Local spinal mechanisms, in addition to descending inhibition, also underlie the analgesic action of opioids. Opioid receptors are abundantly expressed in the substantia gelatinosa, where glutamate and substance P released from the primary sensory neuron is inhibited by opioids. In the spinal cord, opioids are known to act at synapses either presynaptically or postsynaptically. The presynaptic action corresponds to the inhibitory effect of opioids on the voltage-dependent Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel, leading to reduction in the neurotransmitter release. The postsynaptic action is mediated by opioid- induced K<sup>+</sup> channel activation and hyperpolarization, which inhibits excitation of the dorsal horn projection neuron by glutamate.

Histamine receptors are known to participate in spinal cord nociceptive transmission, and previous studies have

<!-- PDF page 11 -->

reported that the antinociceptive effects of morphine and β- endorphin injected intracerebroventricularly were dosedependently suppressed by intrathecally injected histamine H1 and H3 antagonists, and suggested the involvement of spinal H1 and H3 histaminergic receptors in the analgesic effects of opioids.<sup>62</sup>

### Peripheral Mechanism

Opioids may also produce analgesia through the peripheral mechanism. Cell bodies of the dorsal root ganglion neurons express mRNA and proteins of the μ- , κ- and δ- opioid receptors which are subsequently transported to peripheral terminals.<sup>63</sup> Immune cells infiltrating the inflammation site may release endogenous opioid- like substances, which act on the opioid receptors located on the primary sensory neuron.<sup>64</sup> Experimental and clinical studies have reported analgesic effects of peripherally administered opioids in pathologic pain.<sup>63</sup> Interestingly, in cannabinoid receptor type 1 and type 2 knockout mice, the antinociceptive effect of morphine injected into the paw in the inflammatory phase of the formalin test was decreased by 87% and 76%, respectively.<sup>65</sup> This finding may suggest the possibility that the release of endogenous cannabinoids in structures along the pain pathway is involved in opioid analgesia by the peripheral mechanism.

### Acupuncture

Acupuncture and electroacupuncture have been shown to block pain by activating a variety of bioactive chemicals through peripheral, spinal, and supraspinal mechanisms. Mechanistically, endogenous opioids and opioid receptors have been shown to be involved in acupuncture- and electroacupuncture- induced analgesia in various pain models.<sup>66</sup> Studies in carrageenan- induced inflammatory rat pain models show that an intraplantar injection of naloxone or selective antagonists against μ- , δ- , or κ- opioid receptors 1 hour before electroacupuncture treatment at acupoint _Zusanli_ (ST36) dose- dependently blocked electroacupuncture- produced inhibition of mechanical hyperalgesia assessed through paw- pressure threshold.<sup>67</sup> In a capsaicin- induced inflammatory hind paw pain model, stimulation by 2 Hz of four- train pulses with 100 Hz of intratrain frequency at _Houxi_ (SI3) and _San- yangluo_ (TE8) on the forelimb significantly raised the mechanical pain threshold of the injected paw. This analgesic effect was blocked by intrathecal μ- or δ- opioid receptor antagonists but not by a κ- opioid receptor antagonist.<sup>68</sup> Precise mechanisms for acupuncture and electroacupuncture, including the involvement of physiologic systems other than the opioid receptors, remain to be elucidated.

### MECHANISM OF MOOD ALTERATIONS AND REWARDING PROPERTIES

Selective μ- and δ- receptor agonists are rewarding when defined by place preference and intracranial selfadministration paradigms in rodents, whereas selective κ- receptor agonists produce aversive effects. The mechanisms by which opioids produce euphoria, tranquility, and other alterations of mood (including rewarding properties) remain an area of active investigation, especially given the expanding scope of opioid diversion and misuse. Behavioral

and pharmacologic evidence points to the role of the mesolimbic dopamine system, which originates in the ventral tegmental area (VTA) and projects to the nucleus accumbens (NAc) in the forebrain, in drug- induced reward. Functional magnetic resonance imaging studies demonstrated that a small intravenous dose (4 mg) of morphine induces positive signal changes in reward structures, including NAc, sublenticular extended amygdala, orbitofrontal cortex, and hippocampus, and decreases signal in cortical areas similar to the action of sedative–hypnotics such as propofol and midazolam.<sup>69</sup> These observations are consistent with results of pharmacologic studies. Dopaminergic and glutaminergic fibers originating from VTA and prefrontal cortex (PFC), respectively, terminate on NAc GABA- ergic neurons, which projects to the ventral pallidum (VP). In NAc, glutamate and dopamine activate and inhibit the GABAergic neuron, respectively. Drugs that suppress the NAc- VP GABA pathway leading to increased dopamine release are considered positively rewarding. Opioids activate the opioid receptors in VTA and NAc, leading to the increase in dopamine release in NAc<sup>1</sup> (Fig. 22.11).

The locus coeruleus contains both noradrenergic neurons and opioid receptors and is postulated to play a critical role in feelings of alarm, panic, fear, and anxiety. Neural activity in the locus coeruleus is inhibited by both exogenous opioids and endogenous opioid peptides.

### ANALYSIS OF KNOCKOUT MICE

The physiologic roles of the opioid receptors and endogenous opioid peptides have been investigated mainly by pharmacologic and physiologic methods. However, it has been difficult to separately analyze functional roles of these proteins. With analysis of knockout mice, in which a specific gene is inactivated by molecular biologic methods, additional insights into the physiologic role of the respective opioid receptors and/or endogenous opioid peptide precursors can be obtained.<sup>70</sup>

In the μ- receptor knockout mice analgesia, the reward effect, and the withdrawal effect of morphine were lost.<sup>71</sup> Morphine- induced respiratory depression was not observed in the μ- receptor knockout mice.<sup>72</sup> Therefore, the μ- receptor is a mandatory component of the opioid system for morphine action. In the μ- receptor knockout mice, ketamineinduced respiratory depression and antinociception are diminished,<sup>73</sup> a finding suggesting that ketamine directly or indirectly interacts with the μ- receptor to produce these pharmacologic effects. Furthermore, minimum alveolar concentration (MAC) of sevoflurane was significantly higher in the μ- receptor knockout mice than wild- type mice, an observation suggesting the involvement of the μ- receptor in the anesthetic potency of sevoflurane.<sup>72</sup> The δ- receptor knockout mice displayed a markedly reduced analgesic effect of opioids selective for δ- receptors at the spinal cord level.<sup>74</sup> However, at the supraspinal level, analgesia could be induced by δ- receptor agonists in δ- receptor knockout mice, a finding suggesting the existence of a second δ- like analgesic system. Disruption of the κ- receptor abolished the analgesic, hypolocomotor, and aversive actions of the κ- receptor agonists and induced hyperreactivity in the abdominal constriction test; this finding indicates that the κ- receptor is involved in the perception of visceral chemical

in affective behaviors and modulate responses to painful stimuli.<sup>79</sup>

Thus, the functional roles of individual components of the opioid system have been elucidated by analysis of knockout mice. The μ- opioid receptor continues to be recognized as a predominant signaling receptor for both the affective and antinociceptive action of opioid agonists.

### ACTIONS OF OPIOIDS ON TARGETS OTHER THAN OPIOID RECEPTORS

Pharmacologic analyses have shown that opioids can interact with molecules other than the opioid receptors. Buprenorphine, a partial μ- opioid receptor agonist, has a local anesthetic property and blocks voltage-dependent Na<sup>+</sup> channels through the local anesthetic binding site.<sup>80</sup> It was shown that meperidine exerts agonist activity at the α2B- adrenoreceptor in cultured cells, and was suggested that activation of the α2B- adrenoreceptor may be involved in the antishivering action of meperidine.<sup>81</sup> Methadone is clinically used as a racemic mixture of the _l_ and _d_ isomers. The opioid- like activity of the racemate seems to be almost entirely the result of _l_ - methadone, whereas _d_ - methadone acts as a _N_ -methyl-d-aspartate (NMDA) antagonist.<sup>82</sup> The serotonin type 3A (5- HT3A) receptor, which is directly and indirectly linked to gastrointestinal motility, visceral pain, nausea, and vomiting, is competitively inhibited by morphine and hydromorphone, as well as naloxone; however, fentanyllike opioids did not significantly affect activity of the 5- HT3A receptor, suggesting that the 5- HT3A- mediated effects are affected by morphine, hydromorphone, and naloxone through a mechanism not involving the opioid receptor.<sup>83,84</sup> The mechanism for tramadol analgesia is complex and appears to be composed of reuptake inhibition of noradrenaline and serotonin, and activation of the μ- opioid receptor. The analgesic effect of tramadol is only partially reversed by the μ- opioid receptor antagonist, naloxone. Furthermore, tramadol also acts as an agonist of transient receptor potential vanilloid- 1 (TRPV1) heterologously expressed in cultured cells.<sup>85</sup> Thus, a variety of pharmacologic effects of opioids that are not mediated by the opioid receptors are reported, but it remains to be clarified which of these “off- target” actions of opioids have physiologic or clinical implications.

### PHYSIOLOGIC ROLES OF NOCICEPTIN

pain.<sup>75</sup> Using gene knockout mice, a pharmacologic study demonstrated that the antinociceptive effect of N2O may not be mediated by μ- receptor<sup>76</sup> ; rather, N2O exerts its antinociceptive action and reduces MAC of volatile anesthetics in mice through complex mechanisms including activation of the κ- receptor and the descending inhibitory pathway in the spinal cord, whereas its hypnotic potency is not dependent on κ- receptor activation.<sup>77</sup>

In the mice lacking β- endorphin, morphine induced normal analgesia, but naloxone- reversible stress- induced analgesia could not be observed.<sup>78</sup> The preproenkephalin knockout mice were more anxious than wild- type mice, and males displayed increased offensive aggressiveness. The mutant mice showed marked difference from controls in supraspinal, but not in spinal, responses to painful stimuli. These results suggest that enkephalins play roles

Nociceptin is a 17- amino- acid peptide, the sequence of which resembles those of opioid peptides. Nociceptin precursor mRNA and peptide are present throughout the descending pain control circuits. In the spinal cord, nociceptin- receptor mRNA expression is stronger in the ventral horn than in the dorsal horn, while higher levels of ligand binding occur in the dorsal horn. Targeted disruption of the nociceptin receptor in mice had little effect on basal pain sensitivity in several measures, whereas targeted disruption of the nociceptin precursor consistently elevated basal responses in the tail- flick test, findings suggesting an important role of nociceptin in regulating basal pain sensitivity.<sup>86,87</sup> Furthermore, it was demonstrated that nociceptin inhibits both pain- facilitating and analgesia- facilitating neurons in the rostral ventromedial medulla (RVM).<sup>88</sup> Thus, the involvement of the nociceptin system in pain control is complex,

<!-- PDF page 13 -->

and appears to depend on the preexisting state of pain in the animal. Involvement of nociceptin and its receptor in physiologic functions such as regulation of feeding, body weight homeostasis, and stress response and in psychiatric disorders such as depression, anxiety, and drug or alcohol dependence has also been reported.<sup>89</sup>

Systemic administration of nonpeptide nociceptin receptor agonists revealed that such compounds were effective analgesics in animal pain models. It was reported that a nociceptin receptor agonist, Ro64- 6198, shows antinociceptive and antiallodynic effects without side effects of opioids such as itching, respiratory depression, and addiction in monkeys.<sup>90</sup> As nociceptin and opioid receptor agonists modulate pain by different targets, combining both mechanisms may constitute a novel approach for the development of innovative analgesics. Cebranopadol, a newly synthesized compound possessing agonist activity for both the nociceptin and opioid receptors, exhibits highly potent and efficacious antinociceptive and antihyperalgesic effects in several rat models of acute and chronic pain (tail- flick, rheumatoid arthritis, bone cancer, spinal nerve ligation, diabetic neuropathy).<sup>91</sup> In a phase 1 clinical trial, it was shown that cebranopadol produced respiratory depression with a ceiling effect, in contrast to apnea produced by full μ- opioid receptor agonists.<sup>92</sup>

Expression of opioid receptors on peripheral blood mononuclear cells is controversial. Williams and associates reported that human peripheral blood mononuclear cells express the nociceptin receptor, but not μ- , δ- , or κ- opioid receptors.<sup>93</sup> Nociceptin, produced by the peripheral blood mononuclear cells, might be involved in the control of immune functions.

## Neurophysiologic Effects of Opioids

### CHARACTERISTICS OF THE ANALGESIC ACTION OF OPIOIDS

In human beings, morphine- like drugs produce analgesia, drowsiness, changes in mood, and mental clouding. A significant feature of opioid analgesia is that it is not typically associated with loss of consciousness. When morphine in the same dose is given to a normal, pain- free individual, the experience may be unpleasant. The relief of pain by morphine- like opioids is relatively selective, in that other sensory modalities are not affected. Patients frequently report that the pain is still present, but that they feel more comfortable. It is also important to distinguish between pain caused by stimulation of nociceptive receptors and transmitted over intact neural pathways (nociceptive pain) and pain that is caused by damage to neural structures, often involving neural supersensitivity (neuropathic pain). Although nociceptive pain is usually responsive to opioid analgesics, neuropathic pain may respond poorly to opioid analgesics and require higher doses of drug. Opioid and neuropathic pain share neuropathologic mechanisms that can modulate each other, which may account for the low opioid analgesic efficacy in neuropathic pain. Pain hypersensitivity, resulting from potentiated C- fiber transmission and descending facilitation, and neuroinflammation including induction of

glial reactivity can be caused by both opioid administration and neuropathic pain, and may explain the absence of opioid treatment effectiveness in neuropathic pain.<sup>94</sup> The analgesic effects, as well as side effects, of opioids greatly vary among individuals. A pharmacogenomic twin study has shown that the interindividual variations in opioid effects are likely the result of genetic and environmental factors.<sup>95,96</sup> Animal and human studies indicate the existence of sex- related differences in opioid- mediated behavior.<sup>97</sup> Sarton and associates examined the influence of morphine on experimentally induced pain in healthy volunteers, and demonstrated the gender differences in morphine analgesia, with greater morphine potency but slower speed of onset and offset in women.<sup>98</sup> In contrast, in a study examining interindividual variability in alfentanil analgesic sensitivity in experimental pain models in humans, no gender differences were detected.<sup>99</sup> In a study examining the effects of genetic factors on pain sensitivity, it was shown that males had higher pain thresholds in both thermal skin pain and muscle pressure pain than females.<sup>100</sup> Therefore, the mechanism of gender difference in pain sensitivity and opioid effects remains to be clarified.

The pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic characteristics of opioids can vary throughout the day. The duration of intrathecal sufentanil analgesia exhibited a temporal pattern, with 30% variation throughout the day in women in the first stage of labor.<sup>101</sup> In contrast, Scavone and colleagues reported that the time of day of administration does not seem to influence duration of labor analgesia with spinal- epidural administration of fentanyl or with systemic hydromorphone administration.<sup>102</sup> The potential impact of chronobiology on clinical practice is not clear, and research on the influence of circadian rhythms on the actions of opioids is warranted.

Some controversy remains regarding analgesia produced by peripheral actions of opioids. One review concluded from meta- analysis that intra- articularly administered morphine has a definite but mild analgesic effect.<sup>103</sup> The analgesic effect may be dose- dependent, and a systemic distribution of locally administered opioids cannot be completely excluded. The addition of opioids in brachial plexus block improves the success rate and postoperative analgesia.<sup>104</sup> In contrast, it was reported that the addition of sufentanil may not prolong the duration of brachial plexus block.<sup>105</sup>

Despite variations in effect ascribed to the type of pain stimuli, genetics, gender, time of administration, or site of action (central versus peripheral), opioids remain one of the most potent classes of analgesic drugs.

### Assessment of Opioid Analgesia

Because objective and direct assessment of the patient’s pain is almost impossible to perform, to guide the administration of opioids in anesthetized patients, indirect parameters that reflect the patient’s physiologic reaction to nociception like sweating, movement, heart rate, and blood pressure should be monitored. However, the low specificity of these signs can cause underdosage or overdosage of intraoperative analgesia. The Analgesia Nociception Index (ANI), derived from an electrocardiogram trace, has been proposed as a noninvasive guide to analgesia.<sup>106</sup> The ANI monitor calculates heart rate variation with respiration, a response mediated primarily by changes in the parasympathetic nervous

<!-- PDF page 14 -->

system stimulation to the sinoatrial node of the heart. Patients receiving intraoperative ANI- guided fentanyl administration during sevoflurane anesthesia for lumbar discectomy and laminectomy demonstrated decreased pain in the recovery room, likely as a result of intraoperative fentanyl administration based on more objective assessment of the opioid analgesia.<sup>107</sup> The nociception level (NoL) index, an index of nociception based on nonlinear combination of heart rate, heart rate variability, photoplethysmograph wave amplitude, skin conductance, skin conductance fluctuations and their time derivatives, was reported to be superior to other compared measures and appears to accurately characterize nociception during general anesthesia using propofol and remifentanil.<sup>108,109</sup>

### EFFECTS OF OPIOIDS ON CONSCIOUSNESS, ANESTHESIA, AND SLEEP

Unconsciousness in humans could be produced with fentanyl infused at a rate of 150 mcg/min until a total of 15 mcg/kg.<sup>110</sup> Cortical acetylcholine originates in the basal forebrain and is essential for maintaining normal cognition and arousal. Injection of morphine to the substantia innominata or intravenous morphine administration significantly decreased acetylcholine release within the prefrontal cortex in the rat, and this effect may be the neurochemical basis of opioid- induced change in consciousness.<sup>111</sup>

Fentanyl can reduce the MAC of isoflurane at skin incision in patients, and 50% decrease in MAC was produced by a fentanyl concentration of 1.67 ng/mL.<sup>112</sup> The relationship between the plasma fentanyl concentration and reduction in MAC was not linear, and fentanyl had a ceiling effect on reduction of the MAC of isoflurane. Reduction of volatile anesthetic MAC for most opioids including sufentanil, fentanyl, remifentanil, and alfentanil has been shown,<sup>112–115</sup> but the MAC- reducing capabilities of the opioids are not complete; that is, these are not complete anesthetics. Therefore, opioids must be combined with other anesthetics to produce a “complete anesthetic.”

Postoperative patients are often sleep deprived, and opioids, commonly administered for postoperative pain control, are often mistakenly considered inducers of naturally occurring sleep. However, there have been many reports demonstrating opioid- induced sleep disorders. It was demonstrated that an overnight constant infusion of a small dose of remifentanil (0.01–0.04 mcg/kg/min) inhibited rapid eye movement sleep without suppressing the nocturnal melatonin surge in healthy volunteers, and melatonin administration did not prevent remifentanil- induced sleep disturbance, suggesting that opioids actually affected sleep but produced a minimal effect on the circadian pacemaker.<sup>116</sup> In a double- blind multiple crossover study comparing the effects of placebo, sustained- release morphine sulfate (15 mg), and methadone (5 mg) on sleep with polysomnography in healthy subjects, both opioid drugs significantly reduced deep sleep and increased stage 2 sleep, but neither had an effect on sleep efficiency, wake after sleep onset, or total sleep time.<sup>117</sup>

A clear link between opioid use and sleep- disordered breathing has been established. A systematic review which examined the prevalence of sleep- disordered breathing associated with chronic opioid therapy found that the dose

of opioids was strongly associated with the severity of sleep- disordered breathing, and the overall prevalence of central sleep apnea was 24% in patients with chronic opioid use.<sup>118</sup>

### HALLUCINATION

Opioid- induced hallucination is an uncommon but significant adverse effect of opioid treatment, frequently attributed to underlying psychiatric disease or personality disorder rather than a direct neurobiologic effect of opioids.<sup>119</sup> Hallucinations attributed to opioids have been typically described as auditory, visual, or, rarely, tactile hallucinations. Although many reports have cited morphine as the causative agent, there is no evidence that across a population, one particular class of opioid is associated with a lower incidence of hallucinations. There are reports of hallucinations and/or changes in mental status associated with fentanyl, methadone, tramadol, hydromorphone, buprenorphine, pentazocine, and oxycodone. Many hypotheses have been proposed to explain the etiology of opioid- induced hallucinations. Opioid- induced dopamine dysregulation in the mesolimbic dopaminergic system might be involved in hallucination. Accumulation of morphine metabolites, particularly morphine- 3- glucoronide, has been linked to the development of neurologic phenomena.<sup>120</sup> The simplest treatment for opioid- induced hallucination is discontinuing opioid therapy or changing to the other opioid, that is, opioid rotation, if practical.

### ELECTROENCEPHALOGRAPHY

Although the potency and rate of equilibrium between plasma and brain are different among opioids, the effects of fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil, and remifentanil on electroencephalography (EEG) are consistent.<sup>121</sup> Small doses of fentanyl (2–5 mcg/kg) produce minimal EEG changes, whereas higher doses (30–70 mcg/kg) result in high- voltage slow (δ) waves suggesting a state consistent with anesthesia. Overall, opioids produce dose- dependent changes in the EEG that can mimic those of volatile anesthetics, however opioids show a ceiling effect at higher doses. Although transient isolated (usually frontotemporal) sharp wave activity can be observed after large doses of fentanyl and other opioids, it is not generalized. In a study examining the effect of morphine (3–10 mg) on EEG in patients (14.8 ± 2.8 years) who stayed overnight for pain relief after otologic surgery, it was demonstrated that morphine reduced high- frequency β1 (13.5 to 20 Hz) and β2 (20 to 30 Hz) EEG powers and decreased coherence between frontal and occipital β2 EEG activities compared to wakefulness and non- rapid eye movement sleep, indicating that morphine induced a deep sedative state (Fig. 22.12).<sup>122</sup> Khodayari- Rostamabad and associates studied the effect of remifentanil administration on resting EEG functional connectivity and its relationship to cognitive function and analgesia in healthy volunteers.<sup>123</sup> Remifentanil administration was associated with significant changes in functional cortical connectivity, which seem to disrupt the complex cortical network contributing to normal brain function and can have the potential to be a biomarker for the sedative effects of opioids.

As an effect- site measure, EEG can be employed to assess onset of drug action and drug potency ratios. The spectral edge and serum concentration are closely parallel for remifentanil,<sup>124</sup> whereas a significant time lag is found for recovery of the spectral edge for fentanyl and sufentanil<sup>125</sup> (Fig. 22.13). In healthy volunteers, approximate entropy derived from a parietal montage showed significant correlation with remifentanil dose (1–8 mcg/kg/min for 15–20 min), and it was shown to be appropriate for the assessment of the remifentanil effect on the EEG.<sup>126</sup> Potency ratios based on EEG studies are similar to those obtained from studies determining the plasma drug levels of each opioid necessary to reduce the MAC of isoflurane by 50%.

### EVOKED RESPONSES

Because opioids do not appreciably alter sensory- evoked potentials elicited at the posterior tibial or median nerve, sensory- evoked potentials can be used for spinal cord

function monitoring during anesthesia using opioids.<sup>127</sup> Although administration of remifentanil (1 mcg/kg intravenous [IV] and 0.2 mcg/kg/min infusion; 2.5 mcg/kg IV and 0.5 mcg/kg/min infusion; and 5 mcg/kg IV and 1 mcg/ kg/min infusion) produced dose- dependent reduction in auditory- evoked potentials in patients under isoflurane anesthesia,<sup>128</sup> it was also reported that remifentanil infusion (target plasma concentrations of 1, 2, and 3 ng/mL) did not affect auditory- evoked potential amplitudes and latencies in volunteers with propofol sedation.<sup>129</sup> In healthy human volunteers receiving 3 mcg/kg fentanyl, amplitude and latency of motor- evoked responses to transcranial stimulation were not significantly affected.<sup>130</sup>

Middle latency auditory- evoked potentials (MLAEPs) have been shown to be depressed by increasing concentrations of inhaled and intravenous anesthetics, and these changes can be reversed by surgical stimuli. Thus, MLAEPs can be used as a surrogate measure of the level of anesthesia. Wright and associates examined the effect of remifentanil

### CEREBRAL BLOOD FLOW AND CEREBRAL METABOLISM

The effect of opioids on CBF can be influenced by the concomitant administration of other agents and anesthetic drugs, as well as by the patients’ conditions. Any increase in PaCO2 due to opioid- induced ventilatory depression should cause cerebral vasodilatation, thereby leading to increases in cerebral blood flow (CBF).

Opioids can affect cerebral autoregulation and cerebrovascular CO2 reactivity. When remifentanil (0.15– 0.25 mcg/kg/min) was used together with propofol, it induces a dose- dependent low- flow state with preserved cerebral autoregulation, suggesting that the low- flow state induced by propofol–remifentanil and the increase of cerebrovascular reactivity probably widens the autoregulatory plateau.<sup>133</sup>

A PET study in human volunteers showed that the effect of remifentanil (0.05–0.15 mcg/kg/min) on CBF is dose- and area- dependent, and demonstrated significant CBF increase in areas involved in pain processing, such as the lateral prefrontal cortex, inferior parietal cortex, and supplementary motor area.<sup>134</sup> At moderate doses (0.15 mcg/kg/min), regional CBF increases in mediofrontal and anterior cingulate cortices, occipital lobe transition, and caudal periventricular gray matter, while it decreases in the inferior parietal lobes.<sup>134</sup> Kofke _et al._ reported that remifentanil (0.05–0.2 mcg/kg/min) dose- dependently increases CBF in all areas without correction for PaCO2, and after normalizing for global CBF to correct for PaCO2 effects, the remifentanil- induced CBF increase in the cingulate persisted, with decreased flow occurring in the hippocampus and amygdala.<sup>135</sup> On the other hand, Klimscha _et al._ reported that high doses of remifentanil (2–4 mcg/ kg/min) decrease regional CBF and CBF velocity without impairing cerebrovascular CO2 reactivity in healthy male volunteers.<sup>136</sup> However, it was shown that fentanyl infusion (0.75–1.5 mcg/kg/min) was more effective than remifentanil infusion (0.75–1.5 mcg/kg/min) in preventing increases in CBF velocity during, and immediately following, laryngoscopy and tracheal intubation in children under general anesthesia with 2% sevoflurane.<sup>137</sup>

In general, opioids appear to have little or no effect on cerebral metabolism. However, in humans, high- dose remifentanil (up to 3 mcg/kg/min) has been reported to increase CMRO2 in the temporal lobe from 6.29 to 7.68 mg/100 g/ min.<sup>138</sup>

### INTRACRANIAL PRESSURE

(1 or 3 mcg/kg/min) on MLAEPs in tracheally intubated and nonintubated patients and showed that remifentanil attenuated the arousal response in MLAEPs induced by tracheal intubation but had no effect in the absence of a stimulus.<sup>131</sup> Schraag and associates found no significant effect of remifentanil (3 or 8 ng/mL target concentration

Opioids are generally thought to affect intracranial pressure (ICP) minimally under conditions of controlled ventilation. When given with background anesthesia (isoflurane- N2O), opioids do not cause significant increases in ICP in patients undergoing craniotomy for supratentorial space- occupying lesions.<sup>139,140</sup> ICP was similar under light sedation with

<!-- PDF page 17 -->

remifentanil (0.07 ± 0.03 mcg/kg/min) compared with propofol (4.3 ± 2.5 mg/kg/h) in patients undergoing stereotactic brain tumor biopsy, and cerebral perfusion pressure might be better preserved with remifentanil than propofol, because arterial blood pressure was better preserved with remifentanil.<sup>141</sup>

Opioids may produce increases in ICP in patients undergoing craniotomy for excision of supratentorial spaceoccupying lesions, especially if intracranial compliance is compromised. In a study of patients with severe head injury who have preserved or impaired autoregulation, morphine (0.2 mg/kg) and fentanyl (2 mcg/kg) moderately increased ICP but did not significantly affect cerebral blood flow, a finding suggesting that mechanisms other than vasodilation could be implicated in the opioid- induced ICP elevation.<sup>142</sup> It was also reported that no significant change in ICP was observed in hydrocephalic children after administration of alfentanil (40 mcg/kg).<sup>143</sup> It is not clear whether these discrepancies of the opioid effects on ICP are due to the pressure assessment method, the preoperative ICP, or the effects of co- administered drugs. If opioids do increase ICP, its mechanism remains to be clarified.

### NEUROPROTECTION

An _in vitro_ study using rat cerebellar brain slices demonstrated that pretreatment with morphine at clinically relevant concentrations induced acute neuroprotection mediated by activation of the δ1- opioid receptors, activation of adenosine triphosphate (ATP)- sensitive K<sup>+</sup> channels, and free radical production in mitochondria.<sup>144</sup> In the rat focal ischemia model, fentanyl neither increased nor decreased brain injury compared with awake unanesthetized rats.<sup>145</sup> It was demonstrated that at a supraclinical concentration remifentanil exerted _ex vivo_ antiapoptotic action on the immature mouse brain slices, by the mechanism involving the opioid and NMDA receptors, and the mitochondrialdependent apoptotic pathway.<sup>146</sup> It was reported that κ- opioid receptors were upregulated and played a critical role in brain ischemia and reperfusion in mice, and that κ- opioid receptor activation could potentially protect the brain and improve neurologic outcome via blood–brain barrier protection, apoptosis reduction, and inflammation inhibition.<sup>147</sup> Although there is contradicting evidence for a potential neuroprotection in animal models, there is no clear evidence for neuroprotective effects in humans.

### MUSCLE RIGIDITY

Opioids can increase muscle tone and may cause muscle rigidity. The incidence of rigidity noted with opioid anesthetic techniques varies greatly depending on differences in dose and speed of opioid administration, the concomitant use of N2O, the presence or absence of muscle relaxants, and the patient’s age. Opioid- induced rigidity is characterized by increased muscle tone that sometimes progresses to severe stiffness and associated clinical challenges including difficult ventilation leading to hypoxemia and hypercapnea. Clinically significant opioid- induced rigidity usually begins just as, or after, a patient loses consciousness during anesthetic induction. However, opioid- induced rigidity can also be observed during emergence from anesthesia,

and can appear as late as 5 hours after anesthesia.<sup>148</sup> Mild manifestations of rigidity, such as hoarseness, can occur in conscious patients. Vocal cord closure is primarily responsible for the difficult ventilation with bag and mask that follows the administration of opioids. Lower doses of opioids have also been documented to cause muscle rigidity in patients with particular risk factors: extremes of age (newborns, elderly), critical illness with neurologic or metabolic diseases, and use of medications that modify dopamine levels.<sup>148</sup>

The precise mechanism by which opioids cause muscle rigidity is not clearly understood. Muscle rigidity is not the result of a direct action on muscle fibers because it can be decreased or prevented by pretreatment with muscle relaxants. Mechanisms of opioid- induced muscle rigidity involving the CNS have been postulated. The nucleus raphe pontis within the reticular formation and the caudate nucleus within the basal ganglia have been implicated mechanistically.<sup>149</sup> Rigidity is also likely induced by activation of spinal motor neurons through the action of fentanyl at the locus coeruleus.<sup>150</sup> Some characteristics of opioid- induced catatonia and rigidity, increased incidence with age and muscle movements resembling extrapyramidal side effects, are similar to those of Parkinson disease and may suggest similarities in neurochemical mechanisms.

Pretreatment with or concomitant use of nondepolarizing muscle relaxants can decrease the incidence and severity of rigidity. Opioid- induced muscle rigidity can also be reversed with the μ- receptor antagonist naloxone. Induction doses of sodium thiopental and subanesthetic doses of diazepam and midazolam can prevent, attenuate, or successfully treat rigidity.

### NEUROEXCITATORY PHENOMENA

Although fentanyl causes seizure activity seen on the EEG in animals, EEG evidence of seizure activity after administration of opioids is generally lacking in humans. However, there have been several reports on opioid- induced seizures. Remifentanil infusion (1.0 mcg/kg/min for 3 min) induced generalized tonic– clonic seizure- like activity in an otherwise healthy adult.<sup>151</sup> It was reported that the seizure duration was prolonged in patients undergoing electroconvulsive therapy after administration of remifentanil (1 mcg/kg) and methohexital (0.625 mg/kg), compared with methohexital alone (1.25 mg/kg).<sup>152</sup>

The mechanisms underlying opioid- induced neuroexcitatory phenomena are not completely clear. Experimental study using excised mouse hippocampus showed that the proseizure effect of morphine is mediated by selective stimulation of the μ- and κ- opioid receptors but not the activation of the δ- opioid receptor.<sup>153</sup> In rats, midazolam, naloxone, and phenytoin prevented EEG seizure activity and histologically evident brain damage induced by large doses of fentanyl.<sup>154</sup>

Measurement of CBF by magnetic resonance imaging in human volunteers indicated that the cingulate cortex is the most susceptible to activation by remifentanil (0.05 to 0.2 mcg/kg/min), and the susceptibility is affected by the apolipoprotein E genotype.<sup>135</sup> This finding may support the notion that neuroactivation of limbic areas by the perioperative use of opioids plays a role in the genesis of postoperative cognitive dysfunction.

<!-- PDF page 18 -->

### PUPIL SIZE

Morphine and most μ- and κ- agonists cause constriction of the pupil by their excitatory action on the parasympathetic nerve innervating the pupil. Light induces excitation of the Edinger–Westphal nucleus, leading to pupillary constriction that is inhibited by hypercarbia, hypoxia, and nociception. Opioids release the effect of inhibitory neurons on the Edinger–Westphal nucleus, resulting in papillary constriction<sup>155</sup> (Fig. 22.14). It was reported that after intravenous administration of morphine (0.125 mg/kg) a 26% decrease in pupil diameter occurred after 1 hour, and more than 6 hours was necessary for complete recovery of pupil diameter.<sup>156</sup> The pupillary dilatation reflex has been successfully used to assess the analgesic component of a balanced anesthetic regimen. The pupillometer may be a valuable tool to guide morphine administration in the immediate postoperative period.<sup>157</sup> A prospective randomized study evaluated the impact of intraoperative pupillometry monitoring on perioperative opioid consumption in major gynecologic surgery. It was demonstrated that the use of pupillometry to guide intraoperative analgesia reduced intraoperative remifentanil consumption and postoperative morphine requirements.<sup>158</sup>

Pupillary unrest is the fluctuation in pupil diameter, caused by the oscillating activity of the Edinger–Westphal nucleus, and pupillary unrest under ambient light (PUAL) is present even in well- rested individuals. Although the underlying mechanisms were not clarified, it was shown that administration of fentanyl (1 mcg/kg) to healthy volunteers diminished PUAL.<sup>159</sup> The pretreatment magnitude of PUAL is correlated with the analgesic response to opioid

therapy, and patients who exhibit higher levels of PUAL change after opioid administration have a more beneficial analgesic effect from opioids.<sup>160</sup> This finding may suggest that assessment of PUAL might be clinically useful for adjustment of the opioid doses.

### THERMOREGULATION AND SHIVERING

Opioid- based anesthesia induces a slight increase in the sweating threshold, and reduces vasoconstriction and shivering thresholds to a degree similar to that of the potent inhaled anesthetics.<sup>161</sup> However, meperidine is unique among opioids in its ability to effectively terminate or attenuate shivering by disproportionate reduction of the shivering threshold compared with alfentanil.<sup>162</sup> The antishivering effect of meperidine was thought to be mediated by meperidine’s activity on the κ- receptor, but the involvement of the κ- receptor was denied, because the relatively specific κ- receptor agonist nalbuphine did not show significant antishivering activity in volunteers.<sup>163</sup> It was shown that meperidine exerts agonist activity at the α2B- adrenoreceptor in cultured cells, and was suggested that activation of the α2B- adrenoreceptor may be involved in the antishivering action of meperidine.<sup>81</sup> Tramadol (0.5 mg/kg) suppressed shivering after epidural anesthesia in parturients as effectively as meperidine (0.5 mg/kg).<sup>164</sup> A quantitative systematic review of randomized controlled trials found that meperidine 12.5 to 35 mg and tramadol 35 to 220 mg were effective for parenteral pharmacologic prevention of postoperative shivering.<sup>165</sup>

Remifentanil is associated with an increased incidence of postanesthetic shivering, which is not related with

intraoperative hypothermia. A meta- analysis showed that remifentanil induced dose- dependent postoperative shivering more often than fentanyl, and the incidence was not dependent on whether inhaled anesthetics or propofol was used concomitantly.<sup>166</sup> The higher incidence of postanesthetic shivering with higher doses of remifentanil probably reflects acute opioid tolerance and stimulation of the NMDA receptors.<sup>167</sup> A small dose of ketamine, 0.5 mg/kg at induction of anesthesia followed by infusion at 0.3 mg/kg/h, can prevent remifentanil- induced postanesthetic shivering.

### PRURITUS

Opioid- induced pruritis is one of several perpetual challenges associated with opioid- based analgesia. Although pruritus is occasionally observed with parenteral opioid use, it is more common side effect of neuraxial opioids, with an incidence ranging from 30% to 85%, depending on the dose and lipophilicity of the opioid administered (Table 22.3).<sup>168</sup> Parenteral morphine can cause pruritus through histamine release from mast cells, whereas neuraxial opioid- induced pruritus occurs through a mechanism of neuronal disinhibition in the spinal cord dorsal horn. It was shown that dynorphin, an endogenous opioid peptide activating the κ- opioid receptor, acts as a neuromodulator to inhibit itch in the dorsal horn of the spinal cord in mice.<sup>169</sup> Nguyen _et al._ found that spinal dynorphin neurons are both necessary and sufficient for itch induced by neuraxial morphine in mice, and that agonism of the κ- opioid receptor alleviated morphine- induced itch in mice and nonhuman primates.<sup>170</sup> Activation of a μ- opioid receptor isoform MOR1D<sup>10</sup> by spinal morphine induced activation of the gastrin- releasing peptide receptor that heterodimerized with MOR1D, resulting in activation of phospholipase Cβ3 and intracellular Ca<sup>2+</sup> increase in neurons leading to itch sensation in mice.<sup>171</sup> However, contribution of this mechanism to opioid- induced pruritus has not been shown in humans.

Current strategies to suppress opioid- induced pruritus have shifted away from antihistamines against consequences of morphine- induced histamine release and are focused on exploiting partial blockade of the μ- opioid receptor, activation of the κ- receptor, and modulation of nonopioid receptor pathways. Naloxone reverses opioid- induced itching, but opioid antagonists are not ideal therapeutic agents against pruritus, because opioid analgesia is often reversed by these agents. A subcutaneous administration (12 mg) of methylnaltrexone bromide, a peripherally acting μ- opioid antagonist, did not reduce the overall severity or incidence of pruritus in women having elective cesarean

section under spinal anesthesia with intrathecal morphine 100 mcg, suggesting that peripheral mechanisms do not significantly contribute to spinal opioid- induced pruritus.<sup>172</sup> Ondansetron, a serotonin 5- HT3 receptor antagonist has been proposed to treat spinal or epidural morphineinduced pruritus,<sup>173</sup> and a meta- analysis has demonstrated that prophylactic use of 5- HT3 antagonists significantly reduced the severity and the need for treatment of pruritus.<sup>174</sup> However, another meta- analysis showed that prophylactic intravenous administration of 8 mg ondansetron did not decrease the incidence of fentanyl- or sufentanilinduced pruritus but might decrease the need for pruritus rescue medication.<sup>175</sup>

Involvement of the κ- opioid receptor in the itch modulation has been demonstrated, and selective κ- opioid receptor agonists have been shown to suppress itch in preclinical models. A κ- agonist nalfurafine have been approved to treat chronic pruritus in Japan.<sup>176</sup> Pentazocine (15 mg), an agonist of the κ- opioid receptor and partial agonist of the μ- opioid receptor, was also tried to treat opioid- induced pruritus, and has been found to be superior to 4 mg of ondansetron for the treatment of pruritus induced by intrathecal morphine in parturients undergoing cesarean delivery.<sup>177</sup>

Other approaches include intravenous administration of droperidol (1.25 mg), propofol (20 mg), or alizapride (100 mg), which reduced the incidence of pruritus induced by the use of morphine 0.2 mg intrathecally in patients undergoing cesarean section under spinal anesthesia.<sup>178</sup> Preoperative gabapentin prevents pruritus induced by intrathecal morphine in patients undergoing lower limb surgery with spinal anesthesia.<sup>179</sup>

### OPIOID- INDUCED HYPERALGESIA

There is growing evidence that opioid- induced hyperalgesia (OIH) can represent a major adverse effect of opioid administration, especially when driven by potent formulations and escalating dosages. Both OIH and acute opioid tolerance develop by administration of high- dose opioids, and can cause the opioid dose necessary for analgesia to increase, but these two concepts are different (Fig. 22.15).<sup>180</sup> OIH means a paradoxical increased sensitivity to pain, whereas, in tolerance, increasing doses are required to maintain analgesia because of the patient’s reduced sensitivity to opioid action. Experimentally, opioids have elicited hyperalgesia in animal models after repeated opioid administration or continuous delivery.<sup>181</sup> A systematic review to evaluate the clinical implication of OIH demonstrated that high intraoperative doses of remifentanil are associated with
