**Fig. 22.15 Changes in opioid analgesia and pain during tolerance and hyperalgesia.** (A) Tolerance to opioid analgesia develops after ongoing exposure to the drug. The same dose of drug administered over time produces less analgesic effect. The rate of onset and extent of tolerance development is variable depending on the individual drug and patient characteristics. (B) Opioid analgesic tolerance produces a rightward shift in the dose–response relationship ( _red curve_ ). (C) Opioid- induced hyperalgesia describes a paradoxical increase in pain sensitivity during ongoing exposure to opioid drugs. Although the timeline in the graph is not specified, and will vary between different opioids, in the case of remifentanil (0.1 mcg/kg/ min), infusion for 30 minutes is sufficient to cause opioid- induced hyperalgesia. (D) In opioid- induced hyperalgesia, pain can be induced by stimuli of lower intensity ( _red curve_ ), and allodynia can occur (i.e., pain in response to stimuli that were of intensities [ _shaded red_ ] below threshold of pain before drug administration). (From Colvin LA, Bull F, Hales TG. Perioperative opioid analgesia – when is enough too much? A review of opioid- induced tolerance and hyperalgesia. _Lancet._ 2019;393:1558–1568.)

small but significant increases in acute pain after surgery as assessed by pain intensity at rest 24 hours after surgery, 24- hour morphine use, pain intensity on movement, and hyperalgesia measured after an operation.<sup>182</sup> In a randomized, double- blind, crossover study with 24 healthy male volunteers, it was demonstrated that when compared with low- dose (1 mcg/kg) fentanyl, high- dose fentanyl (10 mcg/ kg) increased the area of hyperalgesia 4.5–6.5 hours after fentanyl administration. This result suggests that fentanyl as well as remifentanil can produce OIH in humans.<sup>183</sup> In animal experiments, mechanisms for OIH have been shown to involve spinal sensitization to glutamate and substance P,<sup>184</sup> NMDA receptor plasticity in the spinal dorsal horn,<sup>185</sup> and α- amino- 3- hydroxy- 5- methyl- 4- isoxazolepropionic acid receptor (AMPAR) expression in the spinal dorsal horn.<sup>186</sup>

A variety of approaches for OIH have been suggested. There have been reports that OIH and subsequent acute opioid tolerance can be prevented by ketamine (10 mg/kg) in rats, suggesting the involvement of the NMDA receptor.<sup>187,188</sup> Low- dose buprenorphine (25 mcg/h for 24 hours), an opioid with NMDA antagonist activity, prevented postoperative secondary hyperalgesia in patients who received remifentanil infusion (effect- site concentration of 4 ng/mL) during major lung surgery.<sup>189</sup> Butorphanol (0.2 mcg/kg) was also effective for prevention of postoperative hyperalgesia after laparoscopic cholecystectomy performed with remifentanil (0.3 mcg/kg/min).<sup>190</sup> N2O (70%), an inhaled anesthetic, is an effective NMDA antagonist, and significantly reduced postoperative opioid- induced hyperalgesia in patients receiving propofol and remifentanil (0.3 mcg/ kg/min).<sup>191</sup> A randomized, double- blind, prospective study

showed that remifentanil (0.2 mcg/kg/min) enhances periincisional hyperalgesia in patients undergoing thyroidectomy, and intraoperative magnesium sulfate (30 mg/ kg at induction followed by 10 mg/kg/h) can prevent remifentanil- induced hyperalgesia.<sup>192</sup> Hyperalgesia after a 30- minute intravenous infusion of remifentanil (0.1 mcg/ kg/min) could be prevented by administration of parecoxib, a COX- 2 inhibitor,<sup>193</sup> suggesting the involvement of COX- 2 in OIH. In patients undergoing elective thyroid surgery, intraoperative use of small- dose naloxone (0.05 mcg/kg/h) reduced postoperative hyperalgesia after remifentanil infusion of 4 ng/mL.<sup>194</sup>

Interestingly, the occurrence of OIH might be affected by general anesthetics co- administered with opioids. When women undergoing breast cancer surgery were anesthetized with sevoflurane or propofol to keep BIS value at 40 to 50, postoperative hyperalgesia induced by intraoperative remifentanil infusion (effect- site concentration of 4 ng/mL) was significant with sevoflurane anesthesia, but not apparent with propofol anesthesia.<sup>195</sup>

A follow- up study on the postoperative course of cardiac surgery demonstrated that intraoperative remifentanil was predictive for chronic thoracic pain 1 year after surgery in a dose- dependent manner.<sup>196</sup> It can be anticipated that hyperalgesia during the perioperative period is linked to peripheral and central pain sensitization and thereby correlates with the development of postoperative chronic pain.<sup>197</sup> This suggests that hyperalgesia induced by remifentanil in the early postoperative period may predict the higher incidence of chronic pain.

Overall, human data generally support the existence of OIH in a few specific settings. Probably all potent opioids

<!-- PDF page 21 -->

such as fentanyl and remifentanil are apparently capable of inducing OIH, which may pose risks for development of postoperative chronic pain. Clinical significance of OIH should be further elucidated, and effective approach to OIH should be developed.

### OPIOID TOLERANCE

The precise mechanism for opioid dependence and tolerance is not completely understood. However, it is probable that the multiple mechanisms are involved in the development of opioid tolerance; from uncoupling of the opioid receptors from their second messengers to the adaptive changes in parallel antagonistic or facilitatory pain transmission systems. For example, in the locus coeruleus, the major noradrenergic nucleus in the brain, long- term opioid exposure results in inhibition of adenylyl cyclase, reduced activity of protein kinase A, and upregulation or supersensitization of the cyclic AMP pathway,<sup>198</sup> and these phenomena are thought to participate in opioid- induced tolerance or addiction. On the other hand, changes in μ- receptor density in brain that occur prior to or during the development of tolerance do not appear to be essential for development of opioid tolerance.<sup>199</sup>

Central glucocorticoid receptors (GRs) have been implicated in the cellular mechanism of neuronal plasticity that has many cellular steps in common with the mechanism of opioid tolerance. It was shown that the development of tolerance to the antinociceptive effect of spinal morphine was substantially attenuated when the GR antagonist was coadministered with morphine but the GR agonist dexamethasone facilitated the development of morphine tolerance, suggesting an important role of spinal GRs in the cellular mechanisms of morphine tolerance in rats.<sup>200</sup> Ketamine, an NMDA receptor antagonist, administered intraoperatively in small doses reduces postoperative opioid consumption in rats, and this effect was considered to be the result of attenuation of acute tolerance to the analgesic effect of opioids,<sup>188</sup> suggesting the involvement of the NMDA- nitric oxide (NO) system in development of acute tolerance to opioids. Neuroinflammation involving astrocytes and microglia, driven by chemokines may represent one of the major mechanisms underlying opioid tolerance as well as pathologic pain. A chemokine, CXCL1, was upregulated in both opioidtolerant patients and rodents, and the onset and extent of opioid tolerance was affected by antagonizing intrathecal CXCL1/CXCR2 signaling.<sup>201</sup> A chemokine, CXCL12, was significantly upregulated in the cerebrospinal fluid of opioid- tolerant patients, and CXCL12 neutralizing antibody and antagonist for CXCR4, a receptor which interacts with CXCL12, attenuated morphine tolerance in rats.<sup>202</sup>

Acute tolerance developed rapidly after acute opioid exposure and may be responsible for postoperative pain or increase in postoperative morphine consumption.<sup>203,204</sup> Intraoperative remifentanil infusion (0.3 mcg/kg/min) in patients undergoing major abdominal surgery under desflurane anesthesia increased postoperative pain and morphine requirement compared with low- dose remifentanil (0.1 mcg/kg/min), suggesting the development of acute remifentanil tolerance.<sup>205</sup> In contrast, there was a report that target- controlled infusion of alfentanil (target plasma concentration of 60–100 ng/mL) and remifentanil (target

plasma concentration of approximately 2 ng/mL) for postoperative analgesia for 6 hours did not lead to opioid tolerance.<sup>206</sup> Intraoperative use of 0.3 mcg **/** kg/min remifentanil for approximately 3 hours did not induce acute tolerance, but the administration of 0.6 and 0.9 mcg **/** kg/min remifentanil to young children resulted in acute tolerance for 24 hours after surgery revealed by increased use of postoperative fentanyl in an apparently dose- related manner.<sup>207</sup> Various reports on acute opioid tolerance in clinical settings may reflect divergent methodologies, difference in patient background, and limited sample size.

Differential opioid tolerance, an important phenomenon in clinical opioid pharmacology, proposes that different targets of opioid drugs do not develop tolerance at the same rate and to the same degree.<sup>208</sup> In a study using rhesus monkeys, acute morphine administration predictably induced dose- dependent analgesia, and chronic morphine administration induced dose- dependent tolerance to the analgesic effect, while no tolerance development to the respiratory depressive effect could be demonstrated in the chronically opioid- treated animals (Fig. 22.16).<sup>209</sup> This finding suggests that increasing the dose of opioids to overcome acute opioid tolerance may induce respiratory depression. There are few data to help predict the magnitude of an individual’s loss of opioid- induced analgesia and their resistance or vulnerability to the opioid- induced respiratory depression.

### OPIOID USE DISORDER

Opioid use disorder (OUD), defined as a pattern of opioid use associated with a range of physical, mental, social, and legal problems, and with increased mortality leading to clinically significant impairment or distress, is a chronic relapsing brain disorder with clinical symptoms of physical dependence, protracted physical symptoms, psychological dependence, and relapse.<sup>210</sup> In the 1980s, opioid pain medications began to play a role in the management of non-cancer chronic pain, and the opioid pain medications sales increased fourfold between 1990 and 2010 with a parallel increase in overdose death rates and admissions for the treatment of OUD. From 1999 to 2016, more than 200,000 people died in the United States from overdoses related to prescription opioids, and opioid- related overdose deaths have reached epidemic levels.<sup>211</sup>

A variety of problems should be taken into consideration for anesthetic management of opioid- dependent patients or patients suffering from OUD.<sup>212</sup> Complications in opioidaddicted patients include cardiopulmonary problems, renal problems and anemia, and long- term morphine administration causes adrenal hypertrophy and impairs corticosteroid secretion. Furthermore, viral and nonviral hepatitis, acquired immunodeficiency syndrome, osteomyelitis, muscle weakness, and neurologic complications may be found in patients suffering from OUD or poly- substance use disorder. As it is common to underestimate and undertreat pain in opioid- dependent patients, it is important to identify the goals in their acute pain management (Box 22.1).<sup>213</sup> Anesthetic management for the opioid- dependent or patient with OUD should include adequate premedication with opioids, administration of supplemental intraoperative and postoperative opioids, and providing nonopioid analgesics and neural blockade<sup>213</sup> (Fig. 22.17). There is no ideal anesthetic

### Box 22.1 Goals of Acute Pain Management in Opioid- Dependent Patients

1.  Identification of the population of at- risk patients receiving long- term opioid therapy for various chronic pain situations (musculoskeletal disease, neuropathic conditions, sickle cell disease, HIV- related disease, palliative care), drug abusers, recovering addicts in opioid maintenance programs

2.  Prevention of withdrawal symptoms and complications

3.  Symptomatic treatment of psychological affective disorders such as anxiety

4.  Effective analgesic treatment in the acute phase

5.  Rehabilitation to an acceptable and suitable maintenance opioid therapy

of countries.<sup>214</sup> Other treatment approaches have emerged including rapid opioid detoxification with high-dose naloxone or naltrexone. For this treatment, general anesthesia is induced before the start of opioid antagonization and maintained for several hours to prevent perception of withdrawal symptoms by the patient.<sup>215,216</sup> Blockade of μ- opioid receptors by naloxone (total dose of 12.4 mg) in opioid-addicted patients induces sympathetic neural activation, including increase in plasma catecholamine concentration and cardiovascular stimulation, which can be managed in part by α2- agonists.<sup>217</sup> Long- term opioid abstinence is not guaranteed even with an ongoing program including OMT, given the neurobiology and societal factors involved in addiction, and potential for repetitive exposure (often medically) in the perioperative period.<sup>218</sup>

agent or technique to employ in the patient with OUD or in the patient with an acute opioid overdose except for judicious use of an opioid antagonist as indicated. Combined techniques utilizing regional anesthetic approaches, as well as concurrent use of low- dose ketamine or α2- agonists, have been successful. Support of the circulatory system with fluids and monitoring of arterial blood gases and pulmonary function are essential.

Owing to the high risk of relapse and overdose in opioiddependent individuals, the most commonly prescribed treatment is opioid medication treatment (OMT), in which opioids with longer absorption times and half- lives are prescribed, such as the full opioid agonist methadone or partial agonist buprenorphine hydrochloride. Because of the injection- deterring potential of naloxone hydrochloride and a better safety profile compared with methadone, daily oral administration of combined buprenorphine and naloxone is becoming a first line choice for OMT in a number

## Respiratory Effects of Opioids

Opioids can affect respiration through multiple mechanisms, and opioid overdose can lead to fatal hypoxia caused by prolonged apnea (Fig. 22.18).<sup>219</sup> Opioid- induced respiratory depression is caused by its suppressive effect on the pontomedullary respiratory center, and characterized by slow, shallow, and irregular breathing, which in severe cases leads to respiratory arrest. Opioids also impair chemoreflexes and upper airway patency and induce muscle rigidity, sedation, and sleep- disordered breathing, all of which compromise patient outcomes.

### EFFECTS ON AIRWAYS

The antitussive actions of opioids are well known and central in origin. Opioids blunt or eliminate somatic and autonomic responses to tracheal intubation and allow

patients to tolerate endotracheal tube placement without coughing or bucking. Remifentanil (effect- site concentration of 2 ng/mL) can suppress coughing induced by extubation after propofol or sevoflurane anesthesia.<sup>220</sup> Conversely, two successive doses of 1.5 mcg/kg fentanyl did not effectively prevent laryngospasm in children, aged 2 to 6 years old, who were anesthetized with sevoflurane.<sup>221</sup>

Several studies have reported a depressant effect of morphine on respiratory mucus secretion in human bronchi, which is one of the most important defenses against respiratory tract infections. However, morphine had no effect on the beating frequency of nasal cilia _in vitro_ .<sup>222</sup> Opioids can affect pharyngeal function, airway protection, and coordination of breathing and swallowing. A sedative dose of morphine (0.1 mg/kg) was associated with increased incidence of pharyngeal dysfunction and discoordinated breathing and swallowing, a combination impairing airway protection and potentially increasing the risk for pulmonary aspirations.<sup>223</sup>

It is well known that opioids can induce cough, contrasting with their antitussive action. Equipotent boluses of sufentanil 0.3 mcg/kg and fentanyl 3 mcg/kg increase the

incidence of cough, although the incidence and severity of coughing with sufentanil are less than those with fentanyl.<sup>224</sup> The incidence of cough induced by fentanyl (100 or 150 mcg) administered through a peripheral intravenous cannula decreased from 18% to 1.3% as the injection time was increased from 2 seconds to 30 seconds,<sup>225</sup> as well as by the administration of 1.5 mg/kg lidocaine 1 minute before fentanyl administration.<sup>226</sup> A meta- analysis showed that the lowest effective dose of lidocaine on the risk of opioidinduced cough was 0.5 mg/kg.<sup>227</sup> It was also reported that preemptive use of fentanyl 25 mcg, administered 1 minute before bolus injection of fentanyl (125 or 150 mcg), can effectively suppress fentanyl- induced cough.<sup>228</sup> Propofol, α2- agonists (clonidine, dexmedetomidine), inhalation of β2- agonists (terbutaline, salbutamol), and NMDA- receptor antagonists (ketamine, dextromethorphan) were also effective for suppression of fentanyl- induced cough.<sup>229</sup> A prospective randomized controlled study demonstrated that a huffing maneuver, consisting of a forced expiration against open glottis, just before intravenous fentanyl administration, significantly reduced the incidence and severity of fentanylinduced coughing in the majority of the patients.<sup>230</sup> Li and associates reported that nonsmoking women undergoing

<!-- PDF page 24 -->

gynecologic surgery who develop fentanyl- induced cough during induction of anesthesia have a higher incidence of postoperative nausea and vomiting (PONV).<sup>231</sup>

In summary, opioids engender potent antitussive properties that can help blunt airway responsiveness to endotracheal intubation. However, depending on the class of opioid and rate of administration, they may evoke a brief cough that can be subverted by using preadministered agents such as lidocaine. Ultimately, caution should be exercised in the administration of opioids as they can also interfere with protective aspects of airway physiology.

### RESPIRATORY DEPRESSION

The respiratory- depressant actions of opioids represent their most serious adverse effect. Activation of the μ- opioid receptor in the caudal medullary raphe region, which is important for regulating pain and respiration, inhibits the ventilatory response to hypercapnia in anesthetized rats.<sup>232</sup> Consistently, administration of morphine or M6G did not produce significant respiratory depression in the μ- opioid receptor knockout mice.<sup>233</sup> Polymorphism of the μ- opioid receptor at nucleotide position 118, which is known to affect M6G- induced analgesia, does not significantly change the susceptibility of respiratory depressive effect of M6G (0.4 or 0.6 mg/kg) in humans.<sup>18</sup> This result suggests that analgesia and respiratory depression may be mediated by different signal transduction mechanisms activated by the μ- opioid receptor.

The lack of adequate postoperative pain relief in the thoracoabdominal region can inhibit deep breath and cause shallow respiration leading to postoperative respiratory dysfunction including atelectasis, and therefore opioids can prevent or correct respiratory impairment as a basic component of postoperative analgesia. However, opioids can also dose- dependently depress respiration, representing the most feared adverse effect, and can be a significant cause of death and brain damage in the perioperative period. The incidence of postoperative opioid- induced respiratory depression was reported to be 0.1%–37%, depending on the route of opioid administration, the type of opioid, the definition and method of monitoring opioid- induced respiratory depression, and the prospective versus retrospective nature of the study.<sup>234</sup>

Opioids activating the μ- receptor cause dose- dependent depression of respiration, primarily through a direct action on brainstem respiratory centers.<sup>235</sup> Although the preBötzinger complex (PreBötC), the main region of respiratory rhythm–pattern generation, has been known to be the main target of opioid- induced respiratory depression, it was demonstrated that preBötC partially mediates opioid effects on respiratory phase timing, but does not mediate the opioid- induced depression of respiratory rate.<sup>236</sup> The stimulatory effect of CO2 on ventilation is significantly reduced by opioids, and the pressure of end- tidal carbon dioxide (PETCO2) is increased by opioids<sup>237</sup> (Fig. 22.19). Opioids also decrease hypoxic ventilatory drive and induce characteristic changes in respiratory pattern. Respiratory rate is usually drastically decreased in opioid overdose, although

hypoxic CNS insult can counter this effect. The prolonged expiratory time in the respiratory cycle induced by opioids frequently results in greater reductions in respiratory rate than in tidal volume. Monitoring of breath intervals in spontaneously breathing patients can sensitively detect fentanyl- induced respiratory depression and can be used as a measure of dynamic opioid effect.<sup>238</sup> High doses of opioids usually eliminate spontaneous respirations without necessarily producing unconsciousness, and patients receiving high doses of opioids may still be responsive to verbal command and often breathe when they are directed to do so.

Plasma fentanyl concentrations of 1.5 to 3.0 ng/mL are associated with significant decreases in CO2 responsiveness, and when moderately large doses (20 to 50 mcg/kg or greater) of fentanyl are used, the potential need for postoperative mechanical ventilation should be anticipated. In healthy humans, the EC50 for depression of minute ventilation with remifentanil and alfentanil was 1.17 ng/mL and 49.4 ng/mL, respectively.<sup>239</sup> In healthy volunteers, fentanyl 1 mcg/kg and remifentanil 0.5 mcg/kg had similar maximum decreases in minute ventilation (∼50%), but the onset of and recovery from ventilatory depression were faster with remifentanil.<sup>240</sup> Naloxone has been accepted as a standard therapy for opioid- induced respiratory depression. However, reports have noted naloxoneresistant respiratory depression after intrathecal morphine administration.<sup>241</sup>

The mechanism of opioid- induced respiratory depression has been explored, and pharmacologic therapies based on

the research have been tried. By the use of knockout mice and pharmacologic approaches, G- protein- gated inwardly rectifying K channels were shown to contribute to respiratory depression by μ- opioid receptors and opioids.<sup>242</sup> A major component of the excitatory synaptic drive necessary for respiratory rhythmogenesis and activation of respiratory motoneurons is mediated by the amino- 3- hydroxy- 5- methyl- 4- isoxazolepropionate (AMPA) type of glutamate receptors.<sup>243</sup> This finding has led to investigations of ampakine (positive modulators of AMPA receptors) therapy to alleviate opioid- induced respiratory depression.<sup>244</sup> 5- HT released from the raphe nuclei potently alters the excitability of respiratory motoneurons in preBötC and other brainstem respiratory nuclei, and the activation of 5- HT1A receptors with befiradol alleviated fentanyl- induced respiratory depression in rats.<sup>245</sup> In healthy humans, GAL021, a calcium- activated potassium (BKCa) channel blocker possessing a stimulatory effect on ventilation at the carotid bodies, was preliminarily shown to reverse alfentanilinduced respiratory depression.<sup>246</sup> It was recently reported that esketamine effectively countered remifentanil- induced respiratory depression in healthy volunteers. Esketamine seems to increase the ventilatory CO2 chemosensitivity, which was reduced by remifentanil.<sup>247</sup>

### Factors Affecting Opioid- Induced Respiratory Depression

Many factors affect the magnitude and duration of opioidinduced respiratory depression (Box 22.2). Opioids should be carefully administered to older patients, because they are more sensitive to the respiratory- depressant effect of opioids and experience higher plasma concentrations of opioids administered on a weight basis, compared with young patients. In addition, morphine can produce greater respiratory depression on a weight basis in neonates than adults, because morphine easily penetrates the brain of neonates and infants through incomplete blood– brain barriers.

The respiratory- depressant effects of opioids are increased and/or prolonged when these drugs are administered with other CNS depressants, including potent inhaled anesthetics, alcohol, barbiturates, benzodiazepines, most intravenous sedatives, and hypnotics. However, droperidol, scopolamine, and

### Box 22.2 Factors Increasing the Magnitude and/or Duration of Opioid- Induced Respiratory Depression

High dose Sleep Neonate Old age Central nervous system depressant Inhaled anesthetics, alcohol, barbiturates, benzodiazepines Hyperventilation, hypocapnia Respiratory acidosis Hyperoxia Decreased clearance Reduction of hepatic blood flow Renal insufficiency Pain

<!-- PDF page 26 -->

clonidine do not enhance the respiratory-depressant effects of fentanyl or other opioids. Curiously, Setnik and associates observed a decrease in PETCO2 when 0.7 g/kg ethanol was administered in addition to 80 mg oral morphine in healthy males, suggesting a stimulatory effect of ethanol on opioidinduced respiratory depression.<sup>248</sup> In contrast, it was reported that ethanol (1 g/L of breath ethanol concentration) caused a significant increase in the apneic events and potentiation of PETCO2 increase, which were induced by 20 mg oxycodone orally administered in opioid- naive healthy volunteers.<sup>249</sup>

Although the opioid action is usually dissipated by redistribution and hepatic metabolism, rather than by urinary excretion, the adequacy of renal function may influence the duration of opioid activity and respiratory depression by opioids. In renal insufficiency, the respiratory depressant properties of morphine and hydromorphone, as well as of the morphine metabolite M6G, become evident as these substances accumulate.

Opioid- induced respiratory depression can be affected by the patient’s original respiratory status. Hypocapnic hyperventilation enhances and prolongs postoperative respiratory depression after fentanyl (10 and 25 mcg/kg). Possible explanations for these findings include increased brain opioid penetration (increased un- ionized fentanyl with hypocarbia) and decreased removal (decreased cerebral blood flow with hypocarbia). Thus, in patients who hyperventilate because of anxiety or pain, even small doses of intravenous opioids can result in transient apnea because of acute shifts in apneic thresholds. Intraoperative hypercarbia can produce the opposite effects. Respiratory depression by remifentanil (50 mcg infused over 60 seconds) was more pronounced in hyperoxia (inhaling 50% O2) than normoxia as determined by minute ventilation, PETCO2, and respiratory rate.<sup>250</sup> During hyperoxia, respiratory depression may be masked when measuring oxygen saturation (SpO2), as pulse oximetry remains in normal values during the first minutes of respiratory depression.

All opioid agonists with a longer plasma half- life than naloxone have a hypothetical potential to show renarcotization with time, especially when a bolus dose of naloxone is used to treat opioid- induced respiratory depression.

Overall, opioids exert their primary respiratory depressant effects through μ- receptor activation in the brainstem respiratory center, although other pathways may be involved. Dose- dependent opioid respiratory depression increases the apneic threshold and reduces the respiratory stimulatory drive of CO2 and hypoxia. Research efforts to block or reverse opioid- induced respiratory depression are ongoing and include a focus on AMPA, 5- HT1A, and calcium- activated potassium channels. The μ- receptor antagonist naloxone continues to be the most commonly used clinical intervention to reverse opioid- induced respiratory depression; however, its effectiveness may be limited if the administered opioid has a greater binding affinity and/ or longer half- life than naloxone, and naloxone can induce severe pain by blocking the analgesic effect of opioid.

## Cardiovascular Effects of Opioids

Numerous reports have demonstrated that large doses of opioid, administered as the sole or primary anesthetic, result in hemodynamic stability throughout the operative period.

Multiple mechanisms are involved in the ability of opioids to provide perioperative cardiovascular stability: reduce sympathetic tone and enhance parasympathetic activity, often producing bradycardia but not tachycardia; minimally change cardiac contractility; function generally as an antiarrhythmic; potentially function as cardioprotective agents to reduce the effect of ischemia by mimicking preconditioning pathway; have no significant effect on the coronary circulation; produce modest vascular smooth muscle relaxation with the exception of a morphine- induced hypotension mediated by histamine release; reduce the surgical stress response through the nervous system and adrenal– pituitary axis – depending on the opioid class.

### NEUROLOGIC MECHANISMS

Key areas of the brainstem that integrate cardiovascular responses and maintain cardiovascular homeostasis are the nucleus solitarius, the dorsal vagal nucleus, the nucleus ambiguous, and the parabrachial nucleus. The nucleus solitarius and parabrachial nucleus, where enkephalincontaining neurons and opioid receptors are distributed, play an important role in the hemodynamic control of vasopressin secretion. The ventrolateral PAG region, a key central site mediating opioid analgesia, also affects hemodynamic control.<sup>251</sup> Direct administration of μ- agonists into the CNS of rats most commonly, but not always, produces hypotension and bradycardia.<sup>252</sup> In addition, opioids can modulate the stress response through opioid receptormediated actions on the hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal axis. As a result, most systemically administered opioids reduce sympathetic and enhance vagal and parasympathetic tone. Accordingly, patients who are volume depleted, or individuals depending on high sympathetic tone or exogenous catecholamines to maintain cardiovascular function, are predisposed to hypotension after opioid administration. The predominant and usual effect of opioids on heart rate is bradycardia resulting from stimulation of the central vagal nucleus. Blockade of sympathetic actions may also play a role in opioid- induced bradycardia.

### CARDIAC MECHANISMS

The direct cardiac actions of opioids, in particular the effects on myocardial contractile mechanisms, are significantly less pronounced than those of many other intravenous and inhaled anesthetics. However, it is clearly shown that opioid receptors actually exist in cardiac myocytes of several species.<sup>253</sup>

### Contractility

Opioids have experimentally been shown to affect myocardial contractility, but clinical reports on their effects on contractility are scarce. Morphine decreases Ca<sup>2+</sup> transients and enhances myofilament Ca<sup>2+</sup> sensitivity through the action on the δ1- opioid receptor expressed in the guinea pig heart.<sup>254</sup> In rabbit ventricular myocytes, morphine prolonged action potential duration by increasing L- type Ca<sup>2+</sup> current, an effect mediated by δ- and κ- opioid receptors, and hyperpolarized cardiac resting membrane potential by increasing the inwardly rectifiying K<sup>+</sup> current, which is not mediated by opioid receptors.<sup>255</sup> Regarding the effect of

<!-- PDF page 27 -->

morphine on the contractility, investigators demonstrated that morphine decreased the isometric force of contraction in atrial muscles from nonfailing and failing human hearts through a naloxone- insensitive mechanism.<sup>256</sup> Fentanyl produces little or no change in myocardial contractility in dogs,<sup>257</sup> and most hemodynamic variables usually remain unchanged after large doses of fentanyl. Alfentanil, at concentrations achieved in clinical practice, increases contraction in ventricular cells by a mechanism involving an increase in the sensitivity of the contractile apparatus to Ca<sup>2+</sup> .<sup>258</sup> The negative inotropic effect of tumor necrosis factor- α (TNF- α) and interleukin- 1β (IL- 1β) on rat ventricular myocytes caused by disruption of sarcoplasmic reticulum Ca<sup>2+</sup> handling and Ca<sup>2+</sup> transient was reported to be ameliorated by alfentanil, but this response may not be mediated by opioid receptors.<sup>259</sup> A study using transthoraic echocardiography demonstrated that continuous targetcontrolled infusion of remifentanil (target effect- site concentration 2 ng/mL, infusion rate 0.08–0.09 mcg/kg/min) did not affect systolic and diastolic left ventricular function in young healthy subjects during spontaneous breathing.<sup>260</sup> A prospective observational study of 30 patients with ischemic heart disease undergoing echocardiographic imaging before and after bolus sufentanil 1.5–2.0 mcg/kg demonstrated that sufentanil preserves hemodynamic parameters as well as echocardiographic indices of left ventricular systolic and diastolic function.<sup>261</sup>

### Cardiac Rhythm Conduction

Opioid- induced bradycardia is primarily mediated by the CNS. However, there have been reports on direct effects of opioids on cardiac rhythm conduction. A recent review reported that many of the commonly prescribed opioid medications can uniquely affect ECG, and can lead to the development of various cardiac arrhythmias.<sup>262</sup> One of the most significant side effects of these drugs is QTc interval prolongation in ECG, especially when administered to patients with a baseline risk for QTc prolongation. A prolonged QTc interval can cause lethal torsades de pointes and ventricular fibrillation. It has been reported that methadone can increase the risk of prolongation of the QTc interval.<sup>263</sup> During induction of anesthesia in patients undergoing coronary artery bypass graft surgery, the QT interval increased significantly after the injection of fentanyl.<sup>264</sup> However, pretreatment with fentanyl 2 mcg/kg or remifentanil 1 mcg/kg significantly attenuated QTc prolongation associated with laryngoscopy and tracheal intubation during propofol or sevoflurane induction.<sup>265,266</sup> Both sufentanil and alfentanil have been demonstrated to be devoid of electrophysiologic effects on the normal or accessory pathways in patients with Wolff–Parkinson– White syndrome.<sup>267,268</sup> Clinically, cardiac conduction disturbances attributable to opioids are not frequently observed, but may be more likely to occur in the presence of Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel or β- adrenergic blockers.

### Coronary Circulation and Myocardial Ischemia

Opioids appear to have no significant effect on coronary vasomotion or myocardial metabolism, do not produce steal phenomena, nor diminish the ability of large coronary arterioles to respond to vasoactive agents.<sup>269</sup> Coronary conductance is regulated by arterial baroreflex control, and

vasodilator response is induced by a rise in aortic pressure. In dogs, the baroreflex control of coronary artery conductance was enhanced by low plasma concentration of fentanyl (1–2 ng/mL), but appears to be depressed at increased concentrations of fentanyl.<sup>270</sup>

Determining the effects and consequences of opioid action on myocardial ischemia is complex, because results can depend on a variety of factors including the species studied and experimental design. Opioids can mimic ischemic preconditioning, and opioid receptor stimulation results in a reduction in infarct size similar to that produced by ischemic preconditioning in rats.<sup>271</sup> Although the preconditioning effect of opioids is mediated mainly by the cardiac κ- and δ- opioid receptors in rats,<sup>272</sup> part of the protective effect of remifentanil may be produced by μ- agonist activity outside the heart.<sup>273</sup> Preconditioning with small doses of intrathecal morphine can provide comparable cardioprotection to myocardial ischemic preconditioning and preconditioning with intravenous morphine in rats, and the effect seems to involve μ- , δ- and κ- opioid receptors.<sup>274</sup> Late preconditioning, in which cardioprotective effects can be observed 24 hours after drug administration, was also produced by morphine- induced activation of the opioid receptor in rat hearts.<sup>275</sup>

Remote preconditioning by brief ischemia of other distant organs, such as intestine, kidney, and limb, similarly provides cardioprotection that is as effective as classic ischemic preconditioning. The myocardial κ- opioid receptors were demonstrated to mediate remote preconditioning by femoral artery occlusion.<sup>276</sup> Brief cycles of myocardial ischemia and reperfusion during the early phase of reperfusion protect the heart from infarction. This phenomenon, termed postconditioning, was shown to be induced by activation of the δ- opioid receptor in rat heart.<sup>277</sup> Volatile anesthetics may also be capable of producing protection against ischemic injury when administered solely on reperfusion. This isofluraneinduced postconditioning in rabbits can be enhanced by morphine through the activation of phosphatidyl- 3- kinase and opioid receptors.<sup>278</sup> It has been shown that brief exposure to exercise (1 to 3 days of moderate- intensity exercise) preconditions the heart against tissue injury and death resulting from myocardial ischemia–reperfusion. It was demonstrated that endogenous opioids produced in the heart mediate exercise- induced cardioprotection against ischemia–reperfusion injury through its action on the δ- opioid receptor in rats.<sup>279</sup> Involvement of adenosine A1 receptor and protein kinase C in the cardioprotective effect of opioids was also suggested.<sup>280,281</sup>

Whether the experimental results showing protective effects of opioid against myocardial ischemia can translate into reductions in morbidity and mortality in patients with coronary artery disease has yet to be established by clinical trials. Clinically, opioid- based anesthesia might be beneficial for coronary artery bypass graft surgery because high doses of opioids can maintain myocardial perfusion and the O2 supply–demand ratio as well or better than can inhalation- based techniques. While there have been relatively few clinical trials of cardioprotection using opioid receptor agonists, pretreatment with morphine and remifentanil showed beneficial effects in postoperative cardiac troponin release after surgical ischemia–reperfusion and angioplasty.<sup>282</sup>

<!-- PDF page 28 -->

### Circulatory Reflexes

In an experiment examining baroreceptor reflex responses induced by perfusion of the carotid sinus in cats, baroreceptor reflexes were well preserved by moderate doses of fentanyl while high doses of fentanyl depressed baroreceptor reflexes.<sup>283</sup> The oculocardiac reflex, which is caused by traction of extraocular muscles during strabismus surgery, was significantly augmented by fentanyl, sufentanil, and remifentanil.<sup>284</sup>

### VASCULAR MECHANISMS

Pharmacologic studies demonstrated direct peripheral vessel smooth muscle relaxation by alfentanil, fentanyl, and sufentanil in the dog, but a precise mechanism was not clarified.<sup>285,286</sup> A supraclinical dose of alfentanil attenuated the phenylephrine- induced contraction via an inhibitory effect on Ca<sup>2+</sup> influx by blocking the voltage- dependent L- type Ca<sup>2+</sup> channels in the rat aortic vascular smooth muscle.<sup>287</sup> Measurement of forearm blood flow after infusion of sufentanil (0.083–0.833 mcg/min) into the brachial artery in healthy volunteers indicated that sufentanil has a direct vasodilatory effect on human vascular tissue that is likely independent of a neurogenic or systemic mechanism.<sup>288</sup>

Remifentanil can cause transient instability in hemodynamic variables in the clinical setting, but this hemodynamic change may not be solely the result of autonomic or central nervous system inhibition or of centrally mediated vagal stimulation. A pharmacologic study using rat thoracic aortic rings indicated that remifentanil vasodilates by an endothelium- dependent mechanism, involving prostacyclin and nitric oxide released from the endothelium and an endothelium- independent mechanism probably mediated by the suppression of voltage- dependent Ca<sup>2+</sup> channels.<sup>289</sup> It was also reported that remifentanil dose- dependently inhibits L- type Ca<sup>2+</sup> channels in human mesenteric arterial smooth muscle cells.<sup>290</sup> In patients with a total artificial heart in which cardiac output is preload- independent, remifentanil (0.25 mcg/kg/min) induces dose- dependent and significant systemic vasodilation without significant effects on capacitance vessels,<sup>291</sup> suggesting the presence of direct vascular effect of remifentanil in humans.

Opioids may affect the pulmonary vasculature as well as systemic circulation. It was shown that phenylephrineinduced contraction of canine pulmonary artery was primarily mediated by α1B- adrenergic receptor activation, and was attenuated by binding of fentanyl to the α1B- adrenergic receptor.<sup>292</sup> Pharmacologic studies in cats demonstrated that sufentanil and remifentanil have potent vasodepressor activity in pulmonary vascular bed and these responses are mediated by histamine and blocked by naloxone.<sup>293,294</sup>

### OPIOIDS IN SHOCK

Opioids are often administered for patients requiring surgical intervention for the control of hemorrhage. An animal study demonstrated that pretreatment with morphine before inducing hemorrhagic shock decreased leukocyte adhesion and vascular permeability in the microcirculation of the mesenteric venule, suggesting that morphine can prevent shock- induced damages in microvasculature.<sup>295</sup> In contrast, it has been shown that endogenous opioids

contribute to the pathophysiology of hypovolemic shock through central and peripheral sympathetic inhibition and contribute to hypotension during severe hemorrhage. Liu _et al._ reported that the selective δ- opioid receptor antagonist ICI 174,864 may be beneficial for the early management of traumatic hemorrhagic shock in rats, suggesting a pathophysiologic role of the δ- opioid receptor in hemorrhagic shock.<sup>296</sup> A meta- analysis showed that naloxone therapy for shock was associated with statistically significant hemodynamic improvement in humans, but clinical effectiveness of naloxone in shock still remains controversial.<sup>297</sup>

## Endocrinologic Effects of Opioids and Stress Responses

Opioids can induce a variety of endocrinologic responses (Table 22.4).<sup>298</sup> In humans, opioids generally increase growth hormone, thyroid stimulating hormone, and prolactin, and decrease luteinizing hormone, testosterone, estradiol, and oxytocin. The effects of opioids on arginine vasopressin and ACTH are conflicting. The primary endocrine disorder that results from opioid misuse is hypogonadism, particularly in males.

Hormonal and metabolic responses to the surgical invasion are often extreme and are thought to contribute to operative mortality. Opioids can reduce the stress response by modulating nociception at several levels of the neuraxis, as well as by influencing centrally mediated neuroendocrine responses. The main components of the neuroendocrine stress response are the corticotropin- releasing hormone brain centers (e.g., paraventricular hypothalamic nucleus) and the locus coeruleus– norepinephrine/autonomic nervous system. Excessively high levels of stress hormones are considered undesirable because they can promote hemodynamic instability and intraoperative and postoperative metabolic catabolism. Endogenous opioid

<!-- PDF page 29 -->

peptides may serve as stress hormones themselves and not just as modulators of secretion of other hormones. This notion is consistent with the finding that β- endorphin, a representative opioid peptide, and ACTH, an essential stress hormone, are derived from the same precursor preproopiomelanocortin and co-secreted in response to stress.

Opioid agonists can modify hormonal responses to surgical trauma in a dose- related fashion through blockade of ACTH release, suppression of surgically induced increases in plasma cortisol, and attenuation of the pituitary–adrenal response to surgical stress. Morphine can increase some stressresponding hormones by evoking histamine release from mast cells, which can induce catecholamine release from sympathetic nerve endings and adrenal medulla. Fentanyl and its congeners are more effective than morphine in modifying hormonal responses to surgery. A randomized controlled trial showed that remifentanil (0.83 mcg/kg/min), when compared with fentanyl (total doses of 12 and 24 mcg/kg), blunts the hypertensive responses and cortisol excretion associated with cardiac surgery but is associated with more hypotension.<sup>299</sup> It was demonstrated in coronary artery bypass grafting that remifentanil (0.25 mcg/kg/min) could attenuate increase in stress hormones (ACTH, vasopressin, epinephrine, and norepinephrine) and inflammatory mediators (IL- 6, IL- 8 and TNF- α) as compared to intermittent fentanyl (total dose 2.6 ± 0.3 mg).<sup>300</sup> These reports indicate that opioids can efficiently control surgery- induced stress responses, which may affect patient outcomes including morbidity and mortality. It was shown that large- dose opioids (remifentanil 0.83 mcg/ kg/min or fentanyl 24 mcg/kg) were associated with lower level of cortisol secretion and a decreased rate of myocardial infarction after cardiac surgery compared with small-dose fentanyl (12 mcg/kg).<sup>299</sup>

## Effects of Opioids on Digestive Organs

### EFFECTS ON GASTROINTESTINAL TRACT

Gastrointestinal side effects still constitute a major drawback in both acute and chronic use of opioids. The adverse gastrointestinal effects of opioids include nausea, vomiting, altered fluid dynamics, inhibited gastric emptying, inhibited intestinal coordinated propulsive activity, and increased transit time, all of which may contribute to postoperative ileus (Table 22.5).<sup>301</sup> Opioid- dependent mechanisms driving these effects are complex, and effects on gastrointestinal motility are believed to involve opioid receptors expressed throughout the myenteric plexus. Several opioid receptor types can be demonstrated on myenteric neurons, and both κ- and μ- receptor agonists regulate cholinergic transmission in the myenteric plexus. κ- Agonists appear to modulate acetylcholine release more potently than μ- agonists by inhibition of N- type voltage- dependent Ca<sup>2+</sup> channels via a pertussis toxin- sensitive G protein in guinea pig ileum.<sup>302</sup> In healthy volunteers, morphine (100 mcg/kg) increased the velocity but did not alter the amplitude or duration of primary peristalsis of the esophagus, and decreased the duration and magnitude of swallowing- induced lower esophageal sphincter relaxation.<sup>303</sup> Gastric emptying is delayed by opioids, via supraspinal (vagus nerve- mediated)

and spinal, as well as peripheral, mechanisms. Intrathecal morphine (0.4 mg) significantly decreased the gastroduodenal propagation velocity and acetaminophen absorption, and intramuscular morphine (4 mg) gave additional effects.<sup>304</sup> Tramadol (1.25 mg/kg IV) has a measurable but smaller inhibitory effect on gastric emptying compared with codeine (1 mg/kg IV) or morphine (0.125 mg/kg IV).<sup>305</sup> Opioids administered epidurally as well as intrathecally reduce gastrointestinal motility.<sup>304</sup> Propofol (0.3 mg/ kg bolus and 1.0 mg/kg/h) abolished the decrease of gastric tone induced by morphine (0.1 mg/kg IV), but did not abolish morphine- induced delay of gastric emptying.<sup>306</sup> Naloxone, an opioid antagonist, reverses opioid- induced delays in gastric emptying. Methylnaltrexone (0.3 mg/kg), a quaternary naloxone derivative that does not cross the blood–brain barrier, can attenuate morphine (0.09 mg/kg)induced delays in gastric emptying in healthy volunteers, suggesting that a peripheral mechanism is involved in the opioid effect on gastrointestinal tract.<sup>307</sup> Intravenous, but not intramuscular, metoclopramide (10 mg) also can reverse delays in gastric emptying induced by morphine (10 mg).<sup>308</sup>

### OPIOID- INDUCED CONSTIPATION

Opioid- induced constipation (OIC) is the frequent side effect in patients who are administered with opioids. OIC is common in advanced cancer patients, associated with severe distress, work productivity reduction, poor quality of life, and increased health care utilization. OIC has a complex pathophysiology that involves propulsive and peristalsis impairment, intestinal mucosal secretion inhibition, intestinal fluid absorption enhancement, and impairment of anal sphincter function.<sup>309</sup> Naloxone methiodide suppressed the oxycodone- induced inhibition of gastrointestinal transit more potently than the inhibition induced by morphine.<sup>310</sup> Different classes of agents for treatment of OIC, including peripherally acting μ- opioid receptor antagonists, naldemedine, naloxegol, and methylnaltrexone, have been recommended.<sup>311</sup>

**Table 22.5** Effects of Opioids on the Gastrointestinal Tract

|**Pharmacologic Action**|**Clinical Effect**|
|---|---|
|Decreased gastric motility and<br>emptying|Decreased appetite; increased<br>gastroesophageal reflux|
|Decreased pyloric tone|Nausea and vomiting|
|Decreased enzymatic secretion|Delayed digestion; hard, dry stools|
|Inhibition of small and large<br>bowel propulsion|Delayed absorption of medication;<br>straining; incomplete evacuation;<br>bloating; abdominal distention;<br>constipation|
|Increased fluid and electrolyte<br>absorption|Hard, dry stools|

### BILIARY AND HEPATIC EFFECTS

Opioid agonists increase biliary duct pressure and sphincter of Oddi (choledochoduodenal sphincter) tone in a dose- and drug- dependent manner through opioid receptor- mediated mechanisms. However, the clinical consequences of opioidinduced biliary tract actions are usually minimal. Although traditional teaching dictates that morphine induces “spasm” in the sphincter of Oddi and should not be used in acute pancreatitis, no studies or evidence exist to indicate morphine is contraindicated for use in acute pancreatitis.<sup>312</sup> Increases in biliary pressure caused by opioids are reversible with naloxone. Oddi’s sphincter manometry via choledochoscope demonstrated that the regular dose of morphine could increase common bile duct pressure, whereas meperidine had no effect on Oddi’s sphincter motility and tramadol inhibited motility of the sphincter of Oddi.<sup>313</sup> Fragen _et al._ studied the effect of remifentanil (0.1 mcg/kg/min) on the flow of dye from the gall bladder into the duodenum in healthy volunteers, and showed that remifentanil delays the drainage of dye from the gall bladder into the duodenum, but the delay is shorter than that reported after morphine or meperidine.<sup>314</sup>

Opioids produce minimal effects on liver function during anesthesia and surgery but can affect ischemia–reperfusion injury in animal experiments. Remifentanil pretreatment can attenuate liver injury induced by ischemia–reperfusion in rats, which was shown to be mediated by inducible nitric oxide synthase expression and exhausting reactive oxygen species by a mechanism independent from opioid receptors.<sup>315</sup> Morphine administered either intravenously or intrathecally 10 minutes before 1 hour of ischemia protects against ischemia–reperfusion injury after 6 hours of reperfusion in both normal and cirrhotic rat liver by a mechanism involving the opioid receptor.<sup>316</sup> Remifentanil significantly attenuated increases in serum aminotransferase levels and the liver histologic changes induced by ischemia–reperfusion injury of the liver in rats, by a mechanism possibly involving hepatic interleukin- 18.<sup>317</sup> These reports might suggest the beneficial effects of opioids in anesthetic management of liver surgery, but it has not been clarified whether opioids are beneficial for the ischemia–reperfusion injury of the liver in clinical settings.

### NAUSEA AND VOMITING

Postoperative nausea and vomiting (PONV) are serious problems that severely affect patients’ well- being. Etiology, treatment, and prevention of postoperative nausea and vomiting have been extensively investigated (Fig. 22.20).<sup>318</sup> Intraoperative use of opioids is a well- known risk factor for PONV.<sup>319</sup> Opioids stimulate the chemoreceptor trigger zone in the area postrema of the medulla possibly through δ- receptors, leading to nausea and vomiting. Irrespective of the specific opioid administered, opioids increase the risk for PONV in a dose- dependent manner, and the effect appears to last for as long as opioids are used in the postoperative period.<sup>320</sup>

The use of propofol in balanced or total intravenous anesthesia significantly reduces the incidence of opioid- induced nausea and vomiting. When opioids are employed, antiemetic prophylaxis should be considered, which includes

**Fig. 22.20 Mechanism of nausea and vomiting induced by various anesthetic- related agents and stimuli.** Vomiting is caused by noxious stimulation of one or more of four sites: the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, the vestibular system, the chemoreceptor trigger zone (CTZ) in the area postrema of the floor of the fourth ventricle, and higher centers in the central nervous system. The CTZ lacks the blood–brain barrier, so that its chemoreceptors are stimulated by various anesthetic- related agents, including opioids. (From Watcha MF, White PF. Postoperative nausea and vomiting: its etiology, treatment, and prevention. _Anesthesiology._ 1992;77:162–184.)

anticholinergics, butyrophenones, dopamine antagonists, serotonin antagonists, and acupressure. Ondansetron, a serotonin type 3 (5- HT3) receptor antagonist, was proved to be effective for opioid- induced PONV.<sup>321</sup> A meta- analysis concluded that prophylactic use of 5- HT3 receptor antagonists significantly reduced the incidence of PONV and the need of rescue antiemetic therapy in parturients who received intrathecal morphine for cesarean delivery.<sup>174</sup> Nausea and vomiting after epidural morphine (3 mg) for post–cesarean section analgesia could be prevented by dexamethasone (8 mg IV) as efficiently as by droperidol (1.25 mg IV).<sup>322</sup> Cannabinoid receptor agonists have been demonstrated to be effective antiemetics in some clinical settings. Animal experiments have shown that the cannabinoid agonist suppresses opioid- induced retching and vomiting by activation of the cannabinoid CB1 receptor,<sup>323</sup> and the cannabinoid CB1 receptor agonist has been shown to be useful for chemotherapy- related nausea and vomiting,<sup>324</sup> but the effectiveness of nabilone, a CB1 receptor agonist, for prevention of PONV was not demonstrated.<sup>325</sup> Nonpeptide neurokinin NK1 receptor antagonists have demonstrated activity against both peripheral and central emetic stimuli in animal models, and rolapitant, a potent, selective NK1 receptor antagonist, was shown to be effective in reducing emetic episodes after surgery and reduces the incidence of

<!-- PDF page 31 -->

vomiting in a dose- dependent manner.<sup>326</sup> A continuous low- dose naloxone infusion (0.25 mcg/kg/h) ameliorated some of the opioid- induced side effects, including nausea, vomiting and pruritus, in many but not all patients without adversely affecting analgesia.<sup>327</sup> Transdermal scopolamine was shown to be effective for prophylactic use for nausea and vomiting in parturients receiving intrathecal morphine while undergoing cesarean delivery, but was associated with higher incidence of side effects such as dry mouth and blurry vision.<sup>328</sup> Taken together, the prevention and/ or treatment of opioid- induced nausea and vomiting continues to be a clinical challenge. Strategies that include the administration of 5- HT3 receptor antagonists and/or the steroid dexamethasone show efficacy in well- controlled trials. However, such approaches require exposure to additional medications that, in themselves, may carry additional risks of side effects.

## Other Opioid Effects

### RENAL AND URODYNAMIC EFFECTS OF OPIOIDS

Opioids can significantly affect renal function by their effects on the central nervous system. Studies in different experimental animal species and in humans indicate that κ- opioid receptor agonists decrease vasopressin secretion and release from the hypothalamus and posterior pituitary, and decrease response to vasopressin in kidneys.<sup>329</sup> However, the absence of increases in plasma vasopressin, renin, and aldosterone indicate that systemically administered fentanyl, sufentanil, alfentanil, and probably remifentanil most likely indicate that these opioids preserve or minimally affect renal function in humans. If renal function does change during opioid anesthesia and surgery, it is probably due to secondary changes in systemic and renal hemodynamics.

Opioid effects on the lower urinary tract include disturbances of micturition characterized by urinary retention, especially after intrathecal opioid administration. Intrathecal administration of morphine and sufentanil in healthy male volunteers caused dose- dependent suppression of detrusor contractility and decreased sensation of urge.<sup>330</sup> Mean times to recovery of normal lower urinary tract function were 5 and 8 hours after 10 or 30 mcg sufentanil and 14 and 20 hours after 0.1 or 0.3 mg morphine, respectively, indicating that the recovery time is also dose dependent. Malinovsky _et al._ compared urodynamic effects of intravenously administered morphine (10 mg), buprenorphine (0.3 mg), fentanyl (0.35 mg), and nalbuphine (20 mg) before anesthesia for endoscopic extraction of a ureteral stone.<sup>331</sup> It was shown that all of the opioids altered bladder sensations, but that detrusor contraction decreased only after administration of fentanyl and buprenorphine, suggesting that not all opioid agonists behave similarly. Urinary retention induced by intravenous infusion of remifentanil (0.15 mcg/kg/min) could be reversed by a single intravenous dose of methylnaltrexone 0.3 mg/kg or naloxone 0.01 mg/kg.<sup>332</sup> Reversal of urinary retention by methylnaltrexone indicates that peripheral mechanisms may play a role in opioid- induced bladder dysfunction.

A retrospective study for adult patients with chronic kidney disease undergoing orthopedic surgery showed that

glomerular filtration rate during the postoperative period was significantly higher in the group in which remifentanil was used (average infusion dose 0.17 ± 0.08 mcg/kg/ min) for anesthesia management than the group in which remifentanil was not used. This finding may suggest that anesthesia management using remifentanil may have a renal protective effect in adult patients with chronic kidney disease.<sup>333</sup>

### PERINATAL OPIOID EFFECTS AND OBSTETRICS

Opioid use by women during pregnancy has risen dramatically since 2004. Newborns of mothers with opioid use disorder (OUD) or taking prescription opioids can exhibit opioid withdrawal. This is often referred to as neonatal abstinence syndrome (NAS) and requires appropriate treatment and observation,<sup>334,335</sup> and opioid- induced birth defects are a concern.

Teratogenic actions of opioids, at least in animal models, appear to be minimal,<sup>336</sup> but the impact of prenatal opioid exposure on fetal neurodevelopment are largely unknown. In a study of buprenorphine- maintained OUD women, maternal buprenorphine administration has acute suppressive effects on fetal heart rate and movement, and the magnitude of these effects increases as gestation progresses.<sup>337</sup> In an animal experiment, when female mice were treated with oxycodone before mating and switched to buprenorphine to mimic OUD in humans,<sup>338</sup> reduced cortical thickness, altered corticogenesis, and ventriculomegaly were found in fetal brain, and adolescent offspring exposed to maternal opioid use during pregnancy exhibited hyperactivity in late adolescence, providing critical evidence of effects of opioid use during pregnancy on neurodevelopment of the offspring. Thus, the possibility of impact of prenatal exposure to high- dose opioids on fetal neurodevelopment cannot be excluded.

Opioids are commonly used for analgesia in vaginal and cesarean delivery, but should be carefully administered. Nociception due to uterine cervical distention could be suppressed by μ- and κ- agonists in rats,<sup>339</sup> but the analgesic effect of μ- agonist but not κ- agonist was reduced by estrogen.<sup>340</sup> To minimize transfer of opioids to fetus and neonatal adverse effects, fetal acidosis should be avoided, because fetal acidosis can increase opioid transfer from the mother, and opioid administration during the first stage of labor should be restricted. The short- acting opioid alfentanil administered before cesarean delivery attenuated the maternal stress response, but led to slightly reduced Apgar score.<sup>341</sup> In a randomized, double- blind, controlled study, it was shown that a single bolus of 1 mcg/kg remifentanil administered to patients undergoing elective cesarean delivery effectively attenuated hemodynamic changes after induction and tracheal intubation, but remifentanil crosses the placenta and may cause mild neonatal depression.<sup>342</sup>

Because the fetus is capable of pain perception after the 26th week of gestation, adequate postoperative fetal pain management is essential after fetal surgery. It was shown that the sheep fetus absorbs sufentanil after intra- amniotic instillation, and that significantly greater plasma concentrations were obtained in the fetal lamb as compared with the mother sheep,<sup>343</sup> suggesting that investigation of intraamniotic opioids for fetal analgesia might be promising.

<!-- PDF page 32 -->

Breastfeeding can expose the infant to opioids administered to the mother through milk. Exposure of the infant to the drug administered to the mother can be estimated by relative infant dose (RID), a weight- adjusted time- averaged (e.g., daily) dose of drug in milk ingested by the infant, expressed as a percentage of the time- averaged maternal therapeutic dose on a body weight basis. RID of opioids are reported to be 0.4%–3%, implying that opioid use for pain management during labor and delivery are almost compatible with breastfeeding.<sup>344</sup> However, variations in pharmacokinetics in mother and infant can cause serious risks to the infant. Thus, opioid use by the mother during breastfeeding should be limited to 2–3 days for acute pain management.

### HISTAMINE RELEASE AND ANAPHYLACTOID REACTIONS

It is well known that morphine causes histamine release in an opioid receptor- independent mechanism leading to sympathoadrenal activation. It was recently demonstrated that morphine activates a G- protein- coupled receptor, MRGPRX- 2, expressed in mast cells, resulting in mast cell degranulation and histamine release.<sup>345</sup> Increases in plasma histamine after morphine cause dilation of terminal arterioles and direct positive cardiac chronotropic and inotropic actions. In patients pretreated with both H1- and H2- antagonists, the cardiovascular responses are significantly attenuated despite comparable increases in plasma histamine concentrations. Codeine and meperidine are examples of other opioids that can induce mast cell activation with the release of histamine and tryptase probably by a mechanism other than the μ- opioid receptors.<sup>346</sup> Unlike morphine or meperidine, fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil, and remifentanil do not produce increases in plasma histamine, and subsequent hypotension is less frequent with their administration.

True allergic reactions and systemic anaphylactoid reactions to opioids are rare.<sup>347</sup> More commonly, local reactions caused by preservatives may occur. In 32% of heroin addicts dying suddenly after injection, the concentration of tryptase was elevated (>10 mcg/L), but no correlation was found between the immunoglobulin E (IgE) levels and tryptase, supporting the hypothesis that mast cell degranulation was not mediated by allergic reaction.<sup>348</sup> This report suggests that heroin fatalities may be in part driven by anaphylactoid reaction.

### OCULAR EFFECTS

The use of opioids during induction of anesthesia can help to prevent increases in intraocular pressure. Remifentanil (1 mcg/kg) combined with propofol (2 mg/kg) or thiopental (5 mg/kg) was reported to be effective for prevention of intraocular pressure change after succinylcholine and tracheal intubation.<sup>349,350</sup> In patients undergoing elective non- ophthalmic surgery, the intraocular pressure decreased from 13.6 ± 2.6 to 7.1 ± 3.1 mm Hg in the remifentanil group (0.25–0.5 mcg/kg/min) and from 13.7 ± 2.2 to 9.7 ± 3.4 mm Hg in the fentanyl group (2–5 mcg/ kg).<sup>351</sup> Thus, opioids can be used to perioperatively control intraocular pressure, and can be used in patients with glaucoma.

### IMMUNE EFFECTS

Opioids can affect immune function through adaptive immunity, innate immunity, and the neuroendocrine system (Box 22.3).<sup>352</sup> Studies on the function of opioid receptors (μ, δ, and κ) present on immune cells suggest a complex relationship between clinically used opioids and immune function, including through indirect mechanisms. However, because the μ- opioid receptor knockout mice showed no immune modulatory effects after systemic administration of morphine, the immune modulatory effect of morphine is mediated by μ- opioid receptors.<sup>353</sup>

Many reports have been published on the effects of opioids on immune functions and inflammatory responses in animal experiments and clinical cases with some controversial results. It was shown that the maximal suppression

<!-- PDF page 33 -->

of natural killer (NK)- cell activity, proliferation of splenic T- and B- cells and interferon- γ production are observed 0.5–1 hour after 15 mg/kg morphine injection in rats.<sup>354</sup> Postoperative administration of morphine (10 mg intramuscular [IM]) did not significantly affect the NK- cell activity, while tramadol (100 mg IM) enhanced NK- cell activity.<sup>355</sup> Narahara reported that the long- term postoperative administration of fentanyl (total dose 4.0 mg) in the intensive care unit suppressed natural killer cell cytotoxicity, but lymphocyte subsets and plasma levels of TNF- α, IL- 1β, IL- 6, and IL- 10 were not significantly affected.<sup>356</sup> In contrast, It was reported that intravenously administered fentanyl (initial dose of 3 mcg/kg followed by a 2- h IV infusion of 0.02 mcg/kg/min) causes a rapid increase in NK- cell cytotoxicity, which was coincident with an increase in the percentage of CD16<sup>+</sup> and CD8<sup>+</sup> cells in peripheral blood.<sup>357</sup> Compared with fentanyl (1000 mcg), administration of morphine (40 mg) as part of balanced anesthetic technique suppressed several components of the inflammatory response (IL- 6, CD11b, CD18, postoperative hyperthermia) to cardiac surgery and cardiopulmonary bypass.<sup>358</sup> Several reports showed features of remifentanil in the effects on inflammatory or immune responses. With respect to the effects on neutrophils, it was reported that remifentanil, but not sufentanil, alfentanil, or fentanyl, could attenuate activation of human neutrophils exposed to lipopolysaccharides, and decreased activation of intracellular signaling pathways, including p38 and ERK1/2, and expression of proinflammatory cytokines, including TNF- α, IL- 6, and IL- 8, through a mechanism involving the κ- opioid receptor.<sup>359</sup> It was also reported that remifentanil attenuates lipopolysaccharide- induced acute lung injury in rats by inhibition of proinflammatory cytokine production by downregulating the nuclear factor  κB (NF- κB) pathway, suggesting a beneficial effect of remifentanil in acute lung injury or acute respiratory distress syndrome in sepsis.<sup>360</sup> A prospective study for adult patients who underwent elective colorectal surgery demonstrates that the number of patients who developed surgical site infection was higher after remifentanil- based anesthesia (11.6%) compared with fentanyl- based anesthesia (3.4%).<sup>361</sup> A possible reason for the difference in the incidence of surgical site infection may be due to the difference in immunosuppression between fentanyl and remifentanil, but the significance of the finding remains to be explored.

### CANCER PROGRESSION

Epidemiologic studies have suggested that patients who receive general anesthesia with opioids have a greater rate of cancer recurrence than patients who receive local anesthetics.<sup>362</sup> A recent study compared cancer incidence between patients with chronic pain with and without opioid use, and reported that multivariate regression of more than 63,000 propensity score- matched patients with chronic pain showed an adjusted hazard ratio of 2.66 for cancer occurrence in the long- term opioid use group.<sup>363</sup>

Opioids may directly stimulate proliferation and invasion of tumor cells and inhibit apoptosis of tumor cells, or indirectly affect cancer recurrence by immunosuppression.<sup>364</sup> Overexpression of the μ- opioid receptor in human non–small cell lung cancer was suggested to promote

tumor growth and progression.<sup>365</sup> Furthermore, it was reported that women possessing the μ- opioid receptor of A118G genotyope, with reduced function compared to the wild- type μ- opioid receptor, have decreased breast cancer– specific mortality, suggesting that opioid pathways may be involved in promoting tumor growth.<sup>366</sup> Opioids may promote tumor growth and metastasis through multiple mechanisms, whereas it is also reported that opioids can direct various anticarcinogenic pathways (Fig. 22.21).<sup>367</sup> Preclinical data have suggested that μ- opioid receptor inhibition can reverse the stimulatory effect of μ- opioid receptor on cancer progression, and it was hypothesized that a combination of methylnaltrexone, which blocks peripheral μ- opioid receptor, and chemotherapy may be useful for cancer therapy while maintaining CNS- mediated analgesic effect of opioids.<sup>368</sup>

One of the mechanisms involved in the effects of opioids on cancer prognosis is their effects on angiogenesis. Morphine can stimulate angiogenesis by a variety of mechanisms involving nitric oxide, mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK), vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF), and Rho/Rho kinase.<sup>369</sup> In contrast, Blebea _et al._ reported that activation of opioid receptors inhibited angiogenesis, via endogenous opioid ligands.<sup>370</sup> Although both pro- and antiangiogenic actions of opioids have been reported, it is generally believed that the proangiogenic (or neovasculogenic) effects predominate.

### WOUND HEALING

It was shown that the μ- and δ- opioid receptors expressed in keratinocytes participate in skin homeostasis, wound healing is delayed in δ- receptor knockout mice, and overstimulation of μ- , δ- and κ- opioid receptors can trigger the formation of hypertrophic scars.<sup>371</sup> The opioid receptors in the peripheral nervous system can also affect cell differentiation, migration, and adhesion in the skin. Activation of peripheral opioid receptors on primary afferent neurons reduces the excitability of these neurons and suppresses the antidromic release of substance P and calcitonin generelated peptide, which play an essential role in wound repair.

Considering the roles played by the opioid receptors in the skin, it might be possible to control wound healing as well as pain by topical administration of opioids. It was shown that topical morphine application significantly reduced the number of myofibroblasts and macrophages in the closing wound, resulting in decreases in skin thickness and an increase in residual scar tissue in healed skin in rats.<sup>372</sup> These findings might limit the topical application of opioids as an analgesic therapeutic strategy in the treatment of painful cutaneous wounds. In contrast, it was shown that activation of the δ- opioid receptors destabilizes intercellular adhesion and promotes the migratory keratinocyte phenotype, which is required for fast wound closure.<sup>373</sup> If these divergent findings can be resolved in favor of wound healing and enhanced localized analgesia, there is great potential for topical opioid applications in wound healing. Most clinical studies have not focused on opioid effects on the wound healing process, but it has been reported that topical application of opioids can produce substantial analgesic effects with facilitation of wound healing.<sup>374</sup>

## Pharmacokinetics and

## Pharmacodynamics of Opioids

With the advent of modern drug assay technology and the widespread availability of computers, it is now possible to analyze and model pharmacologic data which separates drug response into pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic components. Pharmacokinetic parameters govern the relationship between opioid dose and the opioid concentrations in the blood (or other body fluids). Pharmacodynamic parameters describe the relationship between opioid concentration in blood (or other fluids) and opioid effect.

Computer simulation techniques predict “contextsensitive half- times,” the time necessary to achieve a 50% decrease in drug concentration after termination of a variable- length continuous infusion to a steady- state drug level (see Chapter 23). Such simulations are intended to provide more clinically relevant meaning to pharmacokinetic parameters. The context- sensitive half- time and computer simulations have helped clinicians to select opioids and arrange their administration in a more rational fashion. After 1- hour continuous infusion, the context- sensitive half- time of fentanyl is nearly 6 times that of alfentanil or sufentanil. Remifentanil’s context- sensitive half- time is independent of infusion duration.

### PHYSICOCHEMICAL PROPERTIES

Table 22.6 depicts physicochemical properties of commonly used opioid agonists. Opioids are weak bases, and when dissolved in solution, they are dissociated into protonated and free- base fractions, with the relative proportions depending on the pH and p _K_ a. The free base fraction is more lipid- soluble than the protonated fraction. High lipid solubility facilitates opioid transport into the biophase or site of action. Therefore, highly lipid- soluble opioids have a more rapid onset of action. However, because the opioid receptor recognizes an opioid molecule in the protonated form, the intensity of opioid effects is closely related to the ionized concentration of drug in the biophase.

All opioids are to some extent bound to plasma proteins, including albumin and α1- acid glycoprotein. It is only the un- ionized, unbound fraction that constitutes the diffusible fraction and provides the concentration gradient that promotes diffusion of opioid from the blood to the tissue of interest. Thus the speed of onset of opioid effect is affected by both the lipid solubility and the protein binding.

### PHARMACOKINETIC FEATURES OF INDIVIDUAL DRUGS

Representative pharmacokinetic parameters for the opioids commonly used in anesthesia are displayed in Table 22.6.
