### Morphine

Pharmacokinetics of morphine is notably different from that of the fentanyl congeners. This difference is in large part due to morphine’s comparatively low lipid solubility. There is relatively little transient first- pass uptake of morphine by the lung. Because the p _K_ a of morphine (8.0) is greater than physiologic pH, and thus after intravenous injection only a small fraction (10%–20%) of morphine is un- ionized, penetration of morphine into and out of the brain is presumably slower compared with that of the other opioids. Approximately 20%–40% of morphine is bound to plasma proteins, mostly albumin. Because hepatic extraction ratio of morphine is high, the bioavailability of orally administered morphine is significantly lower (20%–30%) than after intramuscular or subcutaneous injection. Morphine is principally metabolized by conjugation in the liver, and the water- soluble glucuronides, morphine- 3- glucuronide (M3G) and morphine- 6- glucuronide (M6G), are excreted via kidney. The kidney also plays a role in the conjugation of morphine, accounting for nearly 40% of its metabolism.<sup>375</sup> M3G is the major metabolite of morphine, but does not bind to opioid receptors and possesses little or no analgesic activity. M3G may actually antagonize morphine, and this effect may contribute to both variability in response and resistance to morphine analgesic therapy. M3G was reported to produce seizures in animals and cause allodynia in children.<sup>376</sup> M6G accounts for nearly 10% of morphine metabolite and is a more potent μ- receptor agonist than morphine, with a similar duration of action. It was reported that based on area under the concentration–time curve there was a major contribution of M6G to the overall analgesic effect; the mean contributions being estimated as 96.6%, 85.6%, 85.4%, and 91.3% after oral, subcutaneous, intravenous, and rectal administration of morphine, respectively.<sup>377</sup> In patients with renal insufficiency, 97.6% of the analgesic effect was caused by M6G when morphine was given orally. It appears that M6G is in fact the primary active compound when morphine is administered orally

(Fig. 22.22).<sup>378</sup> In contrast to the reports suggesting the high potency of M6G, there have been reports showing that short- term intravenous administration of M6G does not provide effective analgesia.<sup>379</sup>

Especially in patients with renal dysfunction, the accumulation of M6G can lead to an increased incidence of adverse effects, including respiratory depression. M6G can induce respiratory depression similar to morphine, but the site of action in the ventilatory control system might be different between M6G and morphine.<sup>380</sup> It was suggested that single nucleotide polymorphism A118G at the μ- opioid receptor affects the susceptibility to M6G- related opioid toxicity in patients with renal failure.<sup>381</sup> In addition to renal function, M6G accumulation increases by probenecid, implying that M6G are transported by the probenecidsensitive transmembrane transporters, including organic anion transporter peptide, multidrug resistance- related protein, and organic anion transporter families.<sup>382</sup>

### Fentanyl

A three- compartment model is typically used to describe plasma fentanyl concentration decay. The lungs exert a significant first- pass effect and approximately 75% of injected fentanyl is transiently taken up by pulmonary endothelial cells.<sup>383</sup> Approximately 80% of fentanyl is bound to plasma proteins, and significant amounts (40%) are taken up by red blood cells. Fentanyl is relatively long acting, and its context- sensitive half-time is longer than that of alfentanil and sufentanil, in large part because of this widespread distribution in body tissues. Opioids belonging to the phenylpiperidine class, including meperidine, fentanyl, sufentanil, alfentanil, are subject to oxidative metabolism by the CYP450 enzyme system in the liver.<sup>384</sup> Fentanyl, in particular, is metabolized by CYP3A4/5 and its pharmacokinetics is affected by polymorphism of CYP3A4/5. Norfentanyl, which is formed by _N_ - dealkylation of fentanyl, begins to appear in the plasma as early as 1.5 minutes after injection, and is detectable in the urine for up to 48 hours in humans.

### Alfentanil

Following IV injection, alfentanil plasma concentrations are described by either two- compartment or threecompartment models. After intravenous injection, first- pass pulmonary extraction is significantly less than that of fentanyl.<sup>383</sup> Alfentanil is bound to plasma proteins (mostly glycoproteins) in higher proportions (92%) than fentanyl. At physiologic pH, it is mostly (90%) un- ionized because of its relatively low p _K_ a (6.5). Thus, despite more intense protein binding, the diffusible fraction of alfentanil is higher than fentanyl. This explains, in part, its short latency to peak effect after intravenous injection.

The main metabolic pathway of alfentanil is similar to those of fentanyl and includes CYP3A4/5- mediated oxidative _N_ - dealkylation.<sup>385</sup> The degradation products of alfentanil have little, if any, opioid activity. It was reported that alfentanil is metabolized by human liver microsomal CYP3A4 as well as CYP3A5, and that CYP3A5 shows more than 20- fold pharmacogenetic variability in expression level leading to significant differences in human liver alfentanil metabolism.<sup>386</sup> _In vitro_ study demonstrated that propofol in clinically relevant concentrations interferes with oxidative metabolic degradation of alfentanil and sufentanil in the microsomal fraction of pig and human liver.<sup>387</sup>

### Sufentanil

The pharmacokinetic property of sufentanil is adequately described by a three- compartment model. After intravenous injection, first- pass pulmonary extraction, retention, and release are similar to those of fentanyl.<sup>388</sup> The p _K_ a of sufentanil at physiologic pH is the same as that of morphine (8.0), and, therefore, only a small amount (20%) exists in the un- ionized form. Sufentanil is twice as lipid- soluble as fentanyl and is highly bound (93%) to plasma proteins including α1- acid glycoprotein.

The major metabolic pathways of sufentanil include CYP3A4/5- mediated oxidative _N_ - dealkylation.<sup>384</sup> It was reported that CYP3A4 genetic polymorphism correlated

with sufentanil consumption in parturients undergoing cesarean delivery.<sup>389</sup>

### Remifentanil

Remifentanil constitutes the first ultra- short- acting opioid for use as a supplement to general anesthesia due to its rapid degradation. Although chemically related to the fentanyl congeners, remifentanil is structurally unique because of its ester linkages. Remifentanil’s ester structure renders it susceptible to hydrolysis by blood- and tissue- nonspecific esterases, resulting in rapid metabolism and rapid reduction of blood concentration after the infusion has stopped<sup>390</sup> (Fig. 22.23). Because remifentanil is not a good substrate for pseudocholinesterase, metabolism of remifentanil is not influenced by pseudocholinesterase deficiency.<sup>391</sup>

Pharmacokinetic property of remifentanil is best described by a three- compartment model. Its clearance is several times greater than normal hepatic blood flow, consistent with widespread extrahepatic metabolism, but remifentanil is not significantly metabolized or sequestered in the lungs.<sup>392</sup> It is a weak base with a p _K_ a of 7.07. It is highly lipid- soluble with an octanol/water partition coefficient of 17.9 at pH 7.4. Remifentanil is highly bound (=70%) to plasma proteins (mostly α1- acid glycoprotein). The remifentanil free base is formulated with glycine. Because glycine has been shown to act as an inhibitory neurotransmitter that causes a reversible motor weakness when injected intrathecally in rodents, remifentanil is not approved for spinal or epidural use.<sup>393</sup>

The primary metabolic pathway of remifentanil is de- esterification to form a carboxylic acid metabolite, GI90291 (Fig. 22.24),<sup>394</sup> which is 0.001 to 0.003 times as potent as remifentanil. The low _in vivo_ potency of GI90291 (GR90291) can be explained by its low affinity to the μ- receptor in combination with a poor penetration to the brain.<sup>395</sup> Excretion of GI90291 is dependent on renal clearance mechanisms. However, pharmacokinetics of remifentanil is not appreciably influenced by renal or hepatic failure.

### FACTORS AFFECTING PHARMACOKINETICS AND PHARMACODYNAMICS OF OPIOIDS

### Age

Pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of opioids can be influenced by age. It is clear that neonates and infants exhibit

a reduced rate of elimination of essentially all opioids.<sup>396,397</sup> This is presumably due to immature metabolic mechanisms, including the cytochrome P450 system. The prolonged elimination and reduced clearance of opioids observed in premature and infant populations quickly normalizes toward adult values within the first year of life.<sup>396–398</sup>

<!-- PDF page 38 -->

Intraoperative requirements of opioids are affected by age. The infusion rate of remifentanil to block somatic and autonomic response to skin incision was almost twofold higher in children (2–11 years) than adults (20–60 years).<sup>399,400</sup> With advanced age, although pharmacokinetic changes may play a minor role, pharmacodynamic differences are primarily responsible for the decreased dose requirement in the elderly. Age was inversely correlated with central volume of distribution, clearance, and potency of remifentanil<sup>401</sup> (Fig. 22.25). These combined pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic changes result in a reduction in remifentanil dosage by at least 50% or more in the elderly. Pharmacokinetic studies have shown increases in the sensitivity of the central nervous system to alfentanil, fentanyl, and sufentanil at the extremes of age, and increases in the percentage of body fat, and decreases in plasma protein content, liver blood flow, and enzyme function have been demonstrated to be responsible for the agerelated effects.<sup>402</sup>

### Body Weight

Many opioid pharmacokinetic parameters, especially clearance, appear to be more closely related to lean body mass. Total body weight–based dosing in an obese patient results in much higher remifentanil effect- site concentrations than does lean body mass- based dosing.<sup>403</sup> In contrast, for lean patients, the concentrations that result from total body weight–based dosing are not much greater than those based on lean body mass (Fig. 22.26). Clinically, context- sensitive half- times are not significantly different between obese and

lean subjects (Fig. 22.27). There is mounting evidence to suggest that lean body mass is a better predictor of metabolic capacity than total body weight. Ideal body weight, a parameter closely related to lean body mass and one that is perhaps more easily estimated by the clinician, is probably an acceptable alternative to lean body mass. Because obesity and obesity- associated disease have increased and continue to do so, obese patients will frequently present for anesthesia and operations. Understanding the influence of obesity on the disposition of opioids is important in contemporary anesthesia practice, and new pharmacokinetic models incorporating the influence of body mass have been recently reported.<sup>404,405</sup>

### Renal Failure

Renal failure has important implications with respect to clinical pharmacology of morphine, hydromorphone, and meperidine. For the fentanyl congeners, the clinical importance of renal failure is less marked.

As mentioned above, active metabolites of morphine, M3G and M6G, are dependent on renal clearance mechanisms for elimination. A simulation study demonstrated that even small doses of morphine can produce high plasma concentration of morphine and M6G in patients with renal dysfunction depending on the remaining renal function (Fig. 22.28),<sup>406</sup> which can cause life- threatening respiratory depression. In view of these changes induced by renal failure, morphine is not considered a good choice in patients with severely altered renal clearance mechanisms. There are similar concerns for the use of hydromorphone

and meperidine in the setting of renal dysfunction. Because normeperidine, the main metabolite of meperidine with analgesic and CNS excitatory effect, is subject to renal excretion, the potential CNS toxicity secondary to normeperidine accumulation is especially a concern in patients in renal failure.

In contrast, the clinical pharmacology of the fentanyl congeners is not grossly altered by renal failure, although a decrease in plasma protein binding may potentially alter the free fraction of the fentanyl class of opioids. In the presence of renal impairment, none of fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil, and remifentanil deliver a highly active metabolite load, or suffer from significantly prolonged clearance.<sup>407</sup> Neither the pharmacokinetics nor the pharmacodynamics of remifentanil is altered by impaired renal function. In summary, a reduction in renal function may have the greatest effects on morphine due to a decrease in capacity for extrahepatic glucuronidation and clearance. Therefore, fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil, and remifentanil may be safer choices in renal failure patients due to their lack of active metabolites.

### Hepatic Failure

Even though the liver is the metabolic organ primarily responsible for opioid biotransformation, the degree of liver failure typically observed in perioperative patients, except for patients undergoing liver transplantation, does not have a major impact on the pharmacokinetics of most opioids. In addition to reduced metabolic capacity (i.e., cytochrome P450 system and conjugation), liver disease may also lead to reductions in hepatic blood flow, hepatocellular mass, and plasma protein binding. The increase in total body water and the edema of advanced liver disease may alter the distribution characteristics of a drug. Enzyme induction such as observed in early alcoholism can actually increase metabolic capacity of the liver.

Morphine pharmacokinetics is relatively unchanged by developing liver disease, such as liver cirrhosis and hepatic carcinoma, because of the substantial compensatory extrahepatic metabolism of morphine. A reduction in hepatic blood flow would be expected to slow the decline of morphine plasma concentrations. After liver resection, M6G/morphine and M3G/morphine ratios were significantly reduced and circulating morphine concentration was increased mainly due to a lower morphine clearance.<sup>408</sup> In patients with cirrhosis, the metabolism of meperidine is decreased, leading to accumulation of the parent drug and possible CNS depressive effects similar to hepatic encephalopathy. The disposition of fentanyl and sufentanil appears to be unaffected in liver diseases.<sup>409</sup> Reductions in liver blood flow that result from either liver disease or some other disorder (e.g., shock) can affect the pharmacokinetic parameters of alfentanil, fentanyl, and sufentanil. It was demonstrated that there was a significant decrease in clearance of alfentanil in patients with mild to moderate cirrhosis compared with volunteers from the historical control group.<sup>410</sup>

Remifentanil is an opioid whose phamacokinetics is not significantly changed by liver disease (Fig. 22.29).<sup>411</sup> Its kinetics does not change during the anhepatic phase of orthotopic liver transplantation.<sup>412</sup> It was reported that 0.25–0.5 mcg/kg/min remifentanil could provide perioperative analgesia without neurologic deterioration in a patient suffering from chronic hepatic failure with mild encephalopathy.<sup>413</sup> However, in adult patients undergoing liver transplantation, the remifentanil requirement was lower in the group with high Model for End- Stage Liver Disease (MELD) score during the dissection and anhepatic phases (0.105 ± 0.067 vs. 0.064 ± 0.055 mcg/kg/min; and 0.167 ± 0.096 vs. 0.108 ± 0.079 mcg/kg/min, respectively; low MELD group vs. high MELD group).<sup>414</sup> This finding may suggest that remifentanil dose should be reduced in patients with severe liver cirrhosis, considering individual patient characteristics.

### Cardiopulmonary Bypass

Cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB) produces significant alterations in the pharmacokinetics of most opioids. These alterations are results of CPB- induced modifications in distribution volumes (secondary to priming), changes in acid–base balance, organ blood flow, plasma protein concentrations, and body temperature. The binding of drugs to components of the bypass circuit can also alter opioid pharmacokinetics.

Miller _et al._ examined the effect of CPB on plasma fentanyl concentration and showed that total concentration of fentanyl in plasma was significantly decreased and the unbound fraction of fentanyl rose on initiation of CPB. The total fentanyl concentration remained relatively stable during bypass until near the end of CPB when the mean total concentration increased, coinciding with rewarming.<sup>415</sup> Population pharmacokinetic modeling applied to concentration- versus- time data from patients undergoing coronary artery bypass grafting using CPB demonstrated

that the effect of CPB on fentanyl pharmacokinetics is clinically insignificant, and that a simple three- compartment model accurately predicts fentanyl concentrations throughout surgery using CPB.<sup>416</sup>

Elimination of alfentanil is prolonged by CPB primarily because of increased distribution. It was reported that the volume of distribution in steady state (Vdss) and the volume of central compartment for alfentanil were significantly greater in the CPB groups, compared to the non- bypass group.<sup>417</sup> However, the elimination half- life (t1/2β) of alfentanil did not differ significantly between the normothermic CPB, hypothermic CPB, and non- bypass groups. The free fraction of alfentanil during CPB remains constant despite complex changes in binding protein concentrations.<sup>418</sup>

In adult patients undergoing elective myocardial revascularization with hypothermic CPB who received continuous infusions of remifentanil 1.0–2.0 mcg/kg/min, no evidence of accumulation or sequestration was found.<sup>392</sup> Russell _et al._ reported that normothermic CPB did not significantly affect the clearance of remifentanil, but hypothermic CPB reduced it by an average of 20%, and this was attributed to the effect of temperature on blood and tissue esterase activity.<sup>419</sup> In pediatric patients undergoing CPB for repair of atrial septal defect, there was no change in the Vdss, the volume of the central compartment, t1/2α and t1/2β, but clearance values of remifentanil increased 20% in the postbypass period.<sup>420</sup> In patients undergoing coronary artery bypass graft surgery with hypothermic CPB receiving continuous infusion of remifentanil, the volume of distribution increased by 86% with institution of CPB and remained increased after CPB, elimination clearance decreased by 6.37% for each degree from 37°C.<sup>421</sup> Thus, although clearance of remifentanil may reduce during CPB, remifentanil remains a very short- acting drug even during CPB.

### Hemorrhagic Shock

It is a common practice to administer reduced doses of opioids to patients suffering from hemorrhagic shock to minimize adverse hemodynamic consequences and to prevent prolonged opioid effect. This is at least partially attributable to pharmacokinetic mechanisms. Analysis with pigs receiving fentanyl suggested that central clearance and central- and second- compartment distribution volumes were significantly reduced in hemorrhagic shock, resulting in higher fentanyl concentrations for any given dosages and prolonged context- sensitive half- time (Fig. 22.30).<sup>422</sup>

Hemorrhagic shock also altered pharmacokinetics of remifentanil, suggesting that less remifentanil would be required to maintain a target plasma concentration.<sup>423</sup> However, because of its rapid metabolism, changes in context- sensitive half- time of remifentanil in hemorrhagic shock are small. In a stepwise hemorrhage model of pigs receiving total intravenous anesthesia with remifentanil (0.5 mcg/kg/min) and propofol (6 mg/kg/h after 2 mg/kg bolus), the plasma remifentanil concentration showed a threefold greater increase than that of propofol.<sup>424</sup> Thus, the remifentanil dose should be reduced substantially compared with propofol during total intravenous anesthesia for patients with significant blood loss.

### Genetic Variations in Opioid Metabolism

All opioid drugs are substantially metabolized mainly by cytochrome P450 (CYP) and to a lesser extent by

<!-- PDF page 42 -->

UDP- glucuronosyltransferases (UGTs). Although CYP3A4 is involved in the metabolism of many of the opioids, it is the role of CYP2D6 that is of greater clinical interest with respect to the weaker opioids, codeine, dihydrocodeine, oxycodone, hydrocodone and tramadol, because of the formation of their more potent hydroxyl metabolites (morphine, dihydromorphine, oxymorphone, hydromorphone and _O_ - desmethyltramadol [M1], respectively), which have about a 30- fold higher affinity for the μ- receptor. The CYP2D6 gene is highly polymorphic, with 100 allelic variants identified, and enzymatic activity of some alleles may be either significantly higher or lower than other alleles. In the case of a variant more rapidly producing metabolites of higher affinity there exists the possibility of administering an unintended opioid overdose. Alternately, variants leading to low production of potent metabolites may result in subtherapeutic outcomes.<sup>425</sup> Therapeutic recommendations for the use of CYP2D6 genotype results for prescribing codeine and tramadol have been published.<sup>426</sup>

UGTs mediate mainly the formation of glucuronides from buprenorphine, codeine, dihydrocodeine, dihydromorphine, hydromorphone, morphine, naloxone, naltrexone, and oxymorphone. UGT2B7 is believed to be the major UGT isozyme involved, and although variation in the UGT2B7 gene is suggested to influence morphine glucuronidation, the results presented so far are inconsistent. It is possible that differences in metabolic activity among UGT alleles affect pharmacokinetics of opioids.<sup>425</sup> It was reported that

## Anesthetic Techniques Using Opioids

### ANALGESIA

Opioids are administered during monitored anesthesia care (MAC) to provide analgesia for procedures in which local anesthesia may be ineffective or inappropriate, for local anesthetic infiltration or block placement, for uncomfortable positioning. Opioids with rapid onset and short duration of action are generally preferred for MAC, and the most commonly used opioids for MAC are fentanyl and remifentanil. For MAC, fentanyl is often administered in intermittent IV boluses of 25 to 50 mcg, with reduced doses in older adults or when administered in combination with other sedatives/hypnotics. Remifentanil is administered as infusion of 0.025–0.1  mcg/kg/min. For short- lasting analgesia, single- bolus IV administration of opioids can be used: for example, fentanyl (1–3 mcg/kg), alfentanil (5–10 mcg/kg), or sufentanil (0.1–0.3 mcg/kg).<sup>428</sup> Plasma concentrations of opioids necessary for various purposes, reflecting the difference in potency of these opioids, are listed in Table 22.7. Patient-controlled analgesia (PCA) with opioids has become a widely accepted technique in which to provide postoperative analgesia, but pharmacokinetic optimization of opioid treatment in acute pain is a complex matter. Without considering effect- site drug concentrations over time, the choice of opioid and the amount, method, and frequency of its administration cannot be optimal. Morphine and fentanyl are often used for PCA therapy, and piritramide is also frequently used for PCA in European countries. A double- blind randomized study demonstrated that effectsite target- controlled remifentanil PCA with a slow and progressive adapted algorithm is feasible in young women undergoing uterine artery embolization.<sup>429</sup> As a metaanalysis of randomized trials showed no significant differences in maternal satisfaction or most clinical outcomes between remifentanil PCA and conventional epidural analgesia (EA), for obstetric patients who are not candidates for epidural analgesia, remifentanil PCA may provide an alternative for analgesia in the peripartum period, but caution is warranted particularly regarding hypoxemia.<sup>430</sup>

<!-- PDF page 43 -->

Combination of opioids with other drugs might be a method for improvement of PCA. For thoracic surgery, addition of ketamine to opioid for intravenous PCA was superior to intravenous PCA opioid alone, whereas the effect of added ketamine was not significant for orthopedic or abdominal surgery.<sup>431</sup> Despite the utility of an opioid- based PCA, patients should be strictly monitored for opioid- induced respiratory depression.

### SEDATION

Critically ill subjects in the intensive care unit (ICU) often experience anxiety and agitation while being exposed to numerous stressful or noxious stimuli. ICU patients generally require a combination of analgesia and sedation to relieve their state of anxiety, improve adaptation to the endotracheal tube, and aid compliance with mechanical ventilation. Morphine, fentanyl, and sufentanil are frequently used intravenous analgesic agents in the ICU. However, there is a debate over pros and cons of ICU sedation strategies with opioids, and it was demonstrated that fentanyldominant sedation was associated with an increased risk of delirium, a composite outcome of delirium or death, duration of mechanical ventilation, and ICU and hospital length of stay, compared with propofol- or midazolam- dominant sedation.<sup>432</sup>

The rapid onset and offset of action of remifentanil could make it quickly adjustable to the required level of sedation in critically ill patients. A randomized double- blind study indicated that remifentanil (0.15 mcg/kg/min) and morphine (0.75 mcg/kg/min) could provide comparable level of sedation, and the remifentanil- based regimen allowed a more rapid emergence from sedation and facilitated earlier extubation.<sup>433</sup> However, as discussed previously, use of ultrapotent opioid agents such as remifentanil may also drive tolerance development and opioid- induced hyperalgesia.

### BALANCED ANESTHESIA

Anesthesia with a single agent can often require doses that produce excessive hemodynamic depression. Alternately, “balanced anesthesia” with a balance of agents and techniques can be used to more selectively direct different components of anesthesia (i.e., analgesia, amnesia, muscle relaxation, and abolition of autonomic reflexes with maintenance of homeostasis).<sup>434</sup> Inclusion of an opioid as a component of balanced anesthesia can reduce preoperative pain and anxiety, decrease somatic and autonomic responses to airway manipulations, improve hemodynamic stability, lower requirements for inhaled anesthetics or intravenous anesthetics, and provide immediate postoperative analgesia.

The timing, rate of administration, and dose of supplemental opioids should be tailored to the specific condition of the patient and the expected duration of the operation to avoid various problems. The opioid ideal for balanced anesthesia would permit rapid titration, prevent unwanted responses to noxious stimuli, require little supplementation, not depress cardiovascular function, permit the return of adequate spontaneous ventilation in a timely manner, and produce effective postoperative analgesia with minimal side effects. Alfentanil and remifentanil provide the greatest

ability to titrate opioids rapidly because of their extremely rapid time to onset (1–2 min) of peak effect.

### Balanced Anesthesia with Volatile Anesthetics and Opioids

Anesthetic induction for balanced anesthesia can be achieved by combining an opioid, fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil or remifentanil, with a sedative–hypnotic, most commonly thiopental or propofol, and a muscle relaxant. And then, anesthesia is generally maintained by administration of volatile anesthetics, opioids, and muscle relaxants.

Numerous studies have examined the interactions of opioids including morphine, fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil, and remifentanil with volatile anesthetics to obtain immobility and hypnosis, assessed by MAC and MACawake, respectively, and showed synergistic interaction between opioids and volatile anesthetics regarding immobility.<sup>435</sup> A clinical research demonstrated that MACawake of sevoflurane was little affected by 1–2 ng/mL fentanyl but reduced by approximately 24% at a plasma concentration of 3 ng/mL.<sup>436</sup>

**Fentanyl.** Anesthetic induction is usually achieved by combining a loading dose of fentanyl (2–10 mcg/kg) with thiopental or propofol and a muscle relaxant. Maintenance of anesthesia can be achieved with low concentrations of potent inhaled anesthetics, and additional fentanyl (intermittent boluses of 0.5–1.0 mcg/kg every 15 to 30 min or a constant infusion of 0.02–0.2 mcg/kg/min).

The MAC of sevoflurane was dose- dependently reduced by fentanyl, with 3 ng/mL resulting in a 61% reduction in MAC (Fig. 22.31).<sup>437</sup> A ceiling effect was observed for MAC; 6 ng/mL fentanyl provided only an additional 13% reduction in the MAC of sevoflurane. The volatile anesthetic concentration that prevents movement in response to laryngoscopy and tracheal intubation in 50% of patients (MAC- TI) is higher than the MAC that prevents movement in response to surgical incision (MAC). The MAC- TI of sevoflurane was 3.55% and was reduced markedly to 2.07%, 1.45%, and 1.37% by the addition of fentanyl 1, 2, and 4 mcg/kg, respectively, with no significant difference in the reduction between 2 and 4 mcg/kg, thus showing a ceiling effect.<sup>438</sup> The volatile anesthetic concentration that prevents sympathetic responses to surgical incision in 50% of patients (MAC- BAR) also decreased with increasing concentrations of fentanyl in plasma, and the initial steep reduction followed by a ceiling effect (see Fig. 22.31).<sup>437</sup> The plasma concentration of fentanyl required for postoperative analgesia after abdominal surgery performed under balanced anesthesia with sevoflurane and fentanyl in adult patients was approximately 1.5 ng/mL.<sup>439</sup>

Opioid pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics vary considerably among patients. It was reported that the relationship between postoperative analgesic dosing requirements for fentanyl and total body weight is nonlinear, and the fentanyl dose based on total body weight may cause overdosing in obese patients.<sup>439</sup> As discussed previously, considerations for dosing based on lean body weight and/or ideal body weight may have merit in this context. Repeated doses or continuous infusions of fentanyl, especially in the case that the fentanyl dose is determined based on the total body weight, are likely to result in significant depression of

spontaneous ventilation and may make it difficult to extubate when the operation is finished.

**Alfentanil.** Because alfentanil penetrates the brain so rapidly, equilibration of alfentanil between plasma and CNS can be achieved while plasma alfentanil levels are relatively low compared with sufentanil and fentanyl. This property explains the reason why low doses (10–30 mcg/kg) of alfentanil, administered just before or simultaneously with a sedative–hypnotic, are effective for tracheal intubation. Bolus administration of alfentanil should be carefully performed because of the possibility of hypoventilation due to thoracic muscle rigidity.

Alfentanil (25–50 mcg/kg IV), followed by small, titrated sleep doses of any sedative–hypnotic (e.g., 50–100 mg sodium thiopental), is usually successful in preventing significant hemodynamic stimulation from laryngoscopy and intubation. In a previous report, the optimum dose of alfentanil, co- administered with 2.5 mg/kg propofol, when inserting a classic laryngeal mask airway was 10 mcg/kg,

and afterwards anesthesia was maintained with 1.5% isoflurane and 70% nitrous oxide in oxygen.<sup>440</sup> Further opioid supplementation can be achieved with an alfentanil infusion (0.5–2.0 mcg/kg/min) or intermittent boluses of alfentanil (5–10 mcg/kg) for short procedures.

In balanced anesthetic techniques in which potent inhaled anesthetics are employed, relatively low plasma alfentanil concentrations (e.g., 29 ng/mL) can reduce the MAC of isoflurane by approximately 50%.<sup>113</sup> In patients aged 18–67 years, the MAC of isoflurane was 1.28 ± 0.13%, and was significantly decreased by alfentanil (effectsite target of 50 ng/mL) to 0.96 ± 0.09%.<sup>441</sup>

**Sufentanil.** In combination with propofol for induction of anesthesia in children, bolus administration of sufentanil 0.3 mcg/kg can completely abolish the cardiovascular response to tracheal intubation.<sup>442</sup> In healthy normotensive adult patients, a bolus sufentanil 0.1 mcg/kg followed by a continuous infusion at 0.08 mcg/kg/min for at least 5 minutes proved to be effective for blunting the cardiovascular response to intubation.<sup>443</sup> Maintenance of anesthesia can be achieved with sufentanil (intermittent boluses, 0.05–0.3 mcg/kg, or constant infusion, 0.005–0.03 mcg/ kg/min) and volatile anesthetics. The Cp50 for sufentanil during skin incision in the presence of 70% nitrous oxide was reported to be 2.08 ± 0.62 ng/mL in unpremedicated patients.<sup>444</sup> In patients undergoing coronary artery bypass grafting, sufentanil greater than 1.25 ± 0.21 ng/mL reduced isoflurane requirements to less than 0.5% through the operation.<sup>445</sup>

**Remifentanil.** Very short duration of action of remifentanil necessitates infusion (0.1–1.0 mcg/kg/min) to maintain adequate opioid effect for balanced anesthesia. Remifentanil can reliably suppress automatic, hemodynamic, and somatic responses to noxious stimulation and allows the most predictable and rapid trouble- free emergence from anesthesia without postoperative respiratory depression. Infusion rates of 0.1 ± 0.05 mcg/kg/min should permit the return of spontaneous ventilation and responsiveness with maintenance of analgesia.

The synergistic effect of remifentanil and sevoflurane regarding MAC and MAC-BAR has been reported. In female patients, aged 20–50 years, undergoing balanced anesthesia for elective abdominal surgery, the MAC of sevoflurane with remifentanil (target concentration 1 ng/mL and 3 ng/mL) was 3.96% (3.69%–4.23%) and 0.36% (0.24%– 0.47%), respectively.<sup>446</sup> In 3- to 7- year- old child patients, the MAC-BAR of sevoflurane during laparoscopic surgery was 3.29 ± 0.17%, 2.12 ± 0.10%, and 1.29 ± 0.11%, in the presence of remifentanil plasma target concentration (0, 1, and 2 ng/mL), respectively, indicating that remifentanil can effectively reduce the required concentration of sevoflurane in balanced anesthesia.<sup>447</sup>

Associated with emergence from remifentanil anesthesia, the need for alternative analgesic therapies should be anticipated and administered in a timely fashion. Exposure to high doses of remifentanil may paradoxically reduce the pain threshold after its discontinuation, resulting in postoperative hyperalgesia, which is known to be associated with acute and persistent pain. In children undergoing strabismus surgery, combination of sevoflurane (2.5%) and remifentanil

<!-- PDF page 45 -->

(1 mcg/kg followed by 0.1–0.2 mcg/kg/min) showed less frequent postoperative vomiting but higher postoperative pain scores, compared with fentanyl (2 mcg/kg followed by 1 mcg/kg every 45 min).<sup>448</sup> It was reported that perioperative administration of morphine (0.15 or 0.25 mg/kg IV) or fentanyl (150 mcg) did not provide entirely adequate immediate postoperative pain control after general anesthesia with remifentanil and isoflurane in major abdominal surgery.<sup>449,450</sup> On the other hand, administration of ketamine (0.15 mg/kg followed by 2 mcg/kg/min) decreased intraoperative remifentanil requirement during abdominal surgery with isoflurane and postoperative morphine consumption without increasing the incidence of side effects.<sup>451</sup>

### Total Intravenous Anesthesia with Propofol and Opioids

When delivery of inhaled agents is compromised, total intravenous anesthesia (TIVA) techniques are especially useful as balanced anesthesia. By keeping the goals of balanced anesthesia in mind, combining intravenous analgesics and intravenous anesthetics, utilizing infusion pumps, and understanding of pharmacokinetics, clinicians can successfully perform TIVA techniques. Many different IV anesthetic and analgesic compounds can be employed to perform TIVA, but most commonly, one of the opioids is combined with another drug which provide hypnosis and amnesia. Approximate opioid doses and infusion rates for TIVA are listed in Table 22.8.

The pharmacodynamic synergism between opioids and sedative–hypnotics such as propofol is profound. Choosing an opioid with a short context- sensitive half- time allows greater doses of the opioid to be administered, along with reduced doses of propofol, without compromising the time for recovery from anesthesia. Drug dosing regimens as well as assessment of plasma concentrations of opioids and sedative–hypnotics are necessary to provide optimal antinociceptive, hypnotic, and hemodynamic control during TIVA.

Fentanyl interacts synergistically and markedly reduces the dose of propofol required for loss of consciousness and during noxious stimulation such as skin incision<sup>452,453</sup> (Fig. 22.32). Kazama _et al._ characterized the pharmacodynamic interaction between propofol and fentanyl by comparing the propofol Cp50 at which 50% of the patients did not respond to the stimulation including skin incision, peritoneum incision, and abdominal wall retraction, and showed that increasing the fentanyl concentration markedly reduced propofol Cp50 for somatic response, indicating the potential synergistic interaction of both drugs.<sup>454</sup>

**Table 22.8** Approximate Opioid Loading (Bolus) Doses, Maintenance Infusion Rates, and Additional Bolus Doses for Total Intravenous Anesthesia

||**Loading Dose**<br>**(mcg/kg)**|**Maintenance**<br>**Infusion Rate**<br>**(mcg/kg/min)**|**Additional Bolus**<br>**Dose (mcg/kg)**|
|---|---|---|---|
|Alfentanil|10–50|0.5–2|5–10|
|Sufentanil|0.1–0.5|0.005–0.03|0.05–0.3|
|Fentanyl|2–10|0.02–0.2|0.5–3|
|Remifentanil|1–2|0.1–1.0|0.1–1.0|


Anesthetic induction with alfentanil (25–50 mcg/kg) and propofol (0.5–1.5 mg/kg) followed by infusions of 0.5– 1.5 mcg/kg/min of alfentanil and 80–120 mcg/kg/min of propofol will produce complete anesthesia for a variety of procedures. It was proposed that alfentanil concentrations as low as 85 ng/mL, when combined with a blood propofol concentration of 3.5 mcg/mL, can produce both optimal anesthetic conditions and speed of recovery.<sup>455</sup>

It was reported that hemodynamic changes induced by propofol might have an important influence on the pharmacokinetics of alfentanil. In healthy volunteers, propofol (target concentration, 1.5 mcg/mL) decreased the elimination clearance of alfentanil by 15%, rapid distribution clearance by 68%, slow distribution clearance by 51%, and lag time by 62%.<sup>456</sup> TIVA using remifentanil and propofol has become popular. In patients undergoing ear- nose- throat surgery, TIVA with remifentanil and propofol provided a more rapid respiratory recovery after brief surgical procedures, compared with TIVA with alfentanil and propofol.<sup>457</sup> In a study testing the pharmacodynamic interaction between remifentanil and propofol, remifentanil alone had no appreciable effect on the response to shaking and shouting or response to laryngoscopy, propofol could ablate both responses, and modest remifentanil concentrations dramatically reduced the concentrations of propofol required to ablate both responses.<sup>458</sup> Response surface analysis showed considerable synergy between propofol and remifentanil for sedation and suppression of responses to various noxious stimuli.<sup>459</sup>

The need for alternative analgesic therapies should be considered after TIVA with remifentanil as well as after balanced anesthesia using remifentanil and volatile anesthetics. In a study examining adolescent patients undergoing surgery for idiopathic scoliosis performed under propofolbased anesthesia with multimodal analgesia including morphine, sufentanil, ketamine, and dexmedetomidine, no association was found between the total dose of intraoperative remifentanil (0.08–0.16 mg/kg) and postoperative opioid consumption,<sup>460</sup> suggesting that, if administered as part of a multimodal analgesia approach, clinically used dose ranges of remifentanil do not necessarily induce hyperalgesia or opioid tolerance in pediatric scoliosis patients. Postoperative pain relief with low- dose remifentanil infusion was also reported. After general anesthesia with propofol (75 mcg/kg/min) and remifentanil (0.5–1.0 mcg/kg/min) for abdominal or thoracic surgery, continuous infusion of remifentanil (0.05 or 0.1 mcg/kg/min) provided adequate postoperative analgesia.<sup>461</sup>

### OPIOID- BASED (HIGH- DOSE OPIOID) ANESTHESIA FOR CARDIAC SURGERY

Opioids can be administered as the primary anesthetic in opioid- based anesthetic techniques. High- dose opioid anesthesia was introduced as a stress- free anesthetic method for cardiac surgery. High- dose opioid anesthesia was first performed using morphine, and fentanyl and sufentanil have been recommended later. However, several factors have diminished the popularity of high- dose opioid anesthesia, even in cardiac anesthesia. These include the lack of evidence substantiating any significant outcome benefit associated with the use of large doses of opioids, the added

drug costs, and the trend toward “fast track” approaches to the cardiac patient that can be impeded by large doses of opioids. However, opioids, particularly when administered by continuous infusion, are still among the most effective anesthetics for patients undergoing cardiac or other extensive operations.

In an attempt to decrease the costs of cardiac surgery, fast- track programs have become popular. Engoren _et al._ reported that the more expensive but shorter- acting opioids, sufentanil and remifentanil, produced equally rapid extubation, similar length of intensive care units stays, and similar costs compared with fentanyl, indicating that any of these opioids can be chosen for fast- track cardiac surgery to conserve resources for cardiac surgery.<sup>462</sup> A meta- analysis has demonstrated that there was no difference between anesthesia using high- and low- dose opioids, including morphine, fentanyl, sufentanil, or remifentanil, in ICU and hospital length of stay, ventilation time, use of vasopressors, myocardial infarction **_,_** and stroke in adult cardiac surgery patients.<sup>463</sup>

### Fentanyl

Many different techniques have been used to achieve anesthesia with high- dose fentanyl. Rapid or slow bolus injections of high- dose fentanyl (5–75 mcg/kg) will establish plasma fentanyl concentrations (10–30 ng/mL) that are often sufficient to provide stable hemodynamics throughout the induction/intubation sequence. Continuous infusions of high- dose fentanyl for cardiac surgery range from 0.1 to 1.0 mcg/kg/min up to or continuing through CPB. High- dose fentanyl anesthesia has also proved effective and safe for pediatric heart surgery. It was indicated that fentanyl 25–50 mcg/kg combined with isoflurane 0.2%–0.4% was sufficient to obtund hemodynamic and stress responses in the pre- CPB phase of open heart surgery in infants and young children<sup>464</sup> (Fig. 22.33). It was shown that

high- dose fentanyl (50 mcg/kg) is not associated with a difference in the incidence of postoperative cognitive dysfunction at 3 or 12 months after coronary artery bypass surgery in the elderly, whereas low- dose fentanyl (10 mcg/kg) leads to shorter postoperative ventilation times and may be associated with a greater incidence of postoperative cognitive dysfunction 1 week after surgery.<sup>465</sup> It is not clear why the high- dose fentanyl group showed less cognitive dysfunction 1 week after surgery, but it may be possible that high- dose fentanyl can obtund the stress response more effectively than low- dose fentanyl. This finding may present an advantage of high- dose fentanyl anesthesia.

### Remifentanil

Remifentanil has been widely employed in cardiac anesthesia. It was shown that induction with remifentanil 2 mcg/kg with propofol and maintenance with remifentanil at 0.25 or 0.5 mcg/kg/min provided appropriate anesthesia for minimally invasive coronary artery bypass surgery with rapid awakening and tracheal extubation.<sup>468</sup> Kazmaier _et al._ anesthetized patients undergoing elective coronary artery bypass grafting with high- dose remifentanil (2.0 mcg/kg/ min) alone or with remifentanil 0.5 mcg/kg/min combined with propofol (target plasma concentration of 2.0 mcg/ mL).<sup>469</sup> It was demonstrated that high- dose remifentanil reduces stroke volume index, heart rate, mean arterial pressure, myocardial blood flow, and myocardial oxygen uptake and its effects do not differ from those of remifentanil/propofol anesthesia. Geisler et al. examined efficacy and safety of high- dose remifentanil anesthesia in patients undergoing coronary artery bypass graft surgery.<sup>470</sup> It was demonstrated that continuous infusions of remifentanil 1.0–2.0 mcg/kg/min in combination with propofol 3 mg/ kg/h provided profound suppression of responses to surgical stimuli in the majority of patients, but there was a high incidence of muscle rigidity when remifentanil was used

to induce anesthesia. They concluded that there was no apparent advantage in starting the remifentanil infusion rate above 1.0 mcg/kg/min and remifentanil is not suitable for use as a sole induction agent.

### OTHER APPLICATIONS OF OPIOIDS

Other than intravenous, intramuscular, and oral administration, specific pharmaceutical formulations of opioids can be systemically administered via several routes. Selection of the administration method depends on physicochemical and pharmacologic characteristics of opioids.

### Transdermal Therapeutic System

Transdermal drug delivery generally requires high solubility in both water and oil, low molecular weight, high potency, and little or no skin irritation. Fentanyl is available in a transdermal therapeutic system (TTS) for chronic pain treatment. Potential advantages of delivering fentanyl transdermally include no first- pass drug metabolism by the liver, improved patient compliance, convenience and comfort, and consistent analgesia. Transdermal fentanyl patches constantly deliver 12.5, 25, 50, 75 or 100 mcg/h over 72 hours and fentanyl is detected in plasma after 1–2 hours, but the onset of the full analgesic effect was obtained between 12 and 24 hours after patch application. The mean maximal plasma concentrations of fentanyl are proportional to the delivery rate provided by the patch, i.e., 0.3, 0.6, 1.4, 1.7, and 2.5 ng/mL, respectively, and are maintained during steady state after repeated patch applications.<sup>471</sup> The absorption rate is subject to changes in local conditions such as raised skin temperature, for instance during fever, with estimated increases in fentanyl plasma concentrations by approximately 33% at body temperature of 40°C, with the risk of acute opioid overdose.<sup>472</sup> In chronic and cancer pain, TTS fentanyl offers an interesting alternative to oral morphine, and its effectiveness and tolerability in this indication has been demonstrated by a number of trials.<sup>473</sup> Investigations about the efficacy of fentanyl

TTS for acute postoperative pain management have also been reported. In a clinical trial for patients undergoing laparoscopic cholecystectomy, the fentanyl TTS (25 mcg/h) affixed 14 hours before surgery reached a higher constant concentration than the same dose setting of a constant IV infusion of fentanyl after surgery, and reduced pain score and the use of rescue analgesics similar to IV fentanyl infusion.<sup>474</sup> However, some patients suffered severe nausea before surgery, and even removing the patch did not eliminate the symptoms immediately.

In general, TTS fentanyl produces the same adverse effects as other opioids, mainly sedation, nausea, vomiting, and constipation, but TTS fentanyl causes fewer gastrointestinal adverse events in comparison with oral morphine. Death related to TTS fentanyl is a serious problem. In Australia, TTS fentanyl utilization increased 5.1- fold between 2003 and 2015, and a total of 291 fatal fentanyl overdoses were recorded, increasing from no recorded deaths in 2003 to 2.23 deaths/1,000,000 population in 2015.<sup>475</sup>

Buprenorphine is suitable for transdermal application because of its properties that facilitate transdermal absorption such as low molecular weight, high lipophilicity, and high potency. The transdermal formulation of buprenorphine in the United States is currently available in 5 dosage strengths: 5, 7.5, 10, 15, and 20 mcg/h, and the bioavailability decreases to 15% after a 7- day application. Beyond its application in the management of opioid use disorder (OUD), TTS buprenorphine may be effective for moderate and possibly severe cancer pain.<sup>476</sup> Poulain _et al._ studied the effect of TTS buprenorphine in patients with cancer pain, and demonstrated a reduction in mild to moderate pain scores (from 3.5 ± 2.2 to 1.5 ± 1.5) in the group that received TTS buprenorphine, with worsening of pain scores (from 1.5 ± 1.5 to 2.7 ± 1.9) in the placebo group.<sup>477</sup> TTS buprenorphine has been used for perioperative analgesia.<sup>478</sup> When the patch was applied prior to surgery and left in place for the prescribed seven days, it was associated with reduced postoperative pain, lower consumption

<!-- PDF page 48 -->

of other analgesics, and patient satisfaction in gynecologic and orthopedic surgery.

### Transmucosal Drug Delivery

Similar to transdermal drug delivery, transmucosal delivery through the oropharynx and nasopharynx eliminates hepatic first- pass metabolism (drugs are absorbed directly into the systemic circulation) and improves patient comfort, convenience, and compliance. The other mucosa, including airway and rectum, can also be used to deliver opioids.

**Oral Mucosal.** Low lipid solubility of morphine makes it an unlikely candidate for effective transmucosal absorption. In fact, in female patients undergoing lower abdominal surgical procedures, buccal morphine did not reduce significantly postoperative meperidine consumption, compared with placebo, and all patients receiving buccal morphine reported a taste which reduced its acceptability.<sup>479</sup> Opioids with high lipid solubility, such as buprenorphine, fentanyl and methadone, are more effectively absorbed sublingually than morphine.

Buprenorphine is readily absorbed from sublingual mucosal tissues. Because the portion of the drug that is swallowed is almost completely metabolized by the liver, only a small fraction can reach the systemic circulation when swallowed. Systemic bioavailability after sublingual buprenorphine is approximately 50% of that following intravenous administration. Sublingual buprenorphine (0.3 mg) was compared with transdermal buprenorphine (5, 10, and 20 mcg/h) for osteoarthritis pain in the hips and/or knees and found to provide comparable analgesia.<sup>480</sup> Buccal buprenorphine has been introduced and approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) in 2015 for the management of chronic pain. It is composed of flexible, water- soluble polymeric films that stick to the buccal mucosa and dissolve within a few minutes. The bioavailability of this formulation is 46%–65%, and it is useful in patients who are taking over 80 mg oral morphine sulfate equivalent per day for pain management.<sup>481</sup>

Oral transmucosal fentanyl citrate (OTFC) is a solid dosage form of fentanyl that consists of fentanyl incorporated into a sweetened lozenge on a stick. A portion of fentanyl is absorbed through the oral mucosa, and the rest is swallowed and absorbed through the gastrointestinal tract. The recommended doses range from 5 to 20 mcg/kg.<sup>482</sup> OTFC should be administered approximately 30 min before surgery (or painful procedure) to obtain peak effect. Plasma concentrations after OTFC (15 mcg/kg) administration in volunteers peak at 2.0 ± 0.5 ng/mL, 15 to 30 minutes after OTFC administration, then decline to less than 1 ng/mL an hour later.<sup>483</sup> Unlike transdermal fentanyl, OTFC leaves no significant depot in the mucosal tissues after it is removed. The systemic bioavailability of OTFC is 50% and reflects both buccal and gastrointestinal absorption. OTFC bioavailability is similar to that of buprenorphine (55%), but much greater than buccal morphine and other opioids with low lipid solubility. Egan _et al._ demonstrated that the pharmacokinetics of OTFC did not change with repeated dosing and the decline of plasma concentration was as rapid as when administered intravenously<sup>484</sup> (Fig. 22.34). Furthermore, Kharasch _et al._ reported that the pharmacokinetics of OTFC

were not altered in older volunteers (67 ± 6 years), and no change in OTFC dosing in the elderly would appear necessary.<sup>485</sup> Peak fentanyl concentration and clinical effects after OTFC were minimally affected by hepatic/intestinal CYP3A induction by rifampicin and intestinal CYP3A inhibition by grapefruit juice, suggesting that first- pass metabolism minimally influences OTFC bioavailability.<sup>486</sup> Preoperative OTFC was reported to be effective for postoperative analgesia in pediatric tonsillectomy patients.<sup>487</sup> However, OTFC can induce perioperative emesis and respiratory depression. OTFC has been also introduced for treatment of breakthrough cancer pain.<sup>488</sup> OTFC may be ideally suited to treat breakthrough cancer pain, because fentanyl is rapidly absorbed from OTFC and patients can easily selfadminister OTFC. OTFC has been also reported to be useful for prehospital pain management for traumatic patients.<sup>489</sup>

**Nasal Mucosal.** Delivery of opioids through the nasal mucosa has also been investigated. Pharmacokinetic studies in volunteers were performed for nasal administration of fentanyl, alfentanil, sufentanil, butorphanol, oxycodone, and buprenorphine,<sup>490</sup> and demonstrated that mean times for achieving maximum serum concentrations vary from 5 to 50 min, while bioavailability varies from 46% to 71%. Nasal administration of fentanyl, meperidine, and butorphanol have been studied for postoperative pain, and mean onset times were shown to vary from 12 to 22 minutes and times to peak effect from 24 to 60 min. PCA with intranasal fentanyl was demonstrated to be as effective as IV PCA for postoperative pain management.<sup>491</sup> There was no difference in the efficacy of intranasal fentanyl (2 mcg/kg), intramuscular morphine (0.1 mg/kg), and intravenous morphine (0.1 mg/ kg) in controlling postoperative pain and emergence delirium in children undergoing bilateral myringotomy and placement of ventilating tubes.<sup>492</sup> A novel intranasal morphine formulation composed of morphine monohydrate and chitosan, a nontoxic naturally occurring mucoadherent derived

**Fig. 22.34 Time- dependent change in plasma concentration of fentanyl after oral transmucosal fentanyl citrate (OTFC) application and intravenous administration.** Three 800- mcg doses of OTFC were administered with 6- hour intervals between doses, and fentanyl was infused at a constant rate of 50 mcg/min to a total of 15 mcg/kg. (From Egan TD, Sharma A, Ashburn MA, et al. Multiple dose pharmacokinetics of oral transmucosal fentanyl citrate in healthy volunteers. _Anesthesiology._ 2000;92:665–673.)

<!-- PDF page 49 -->

from shellfish, was shown to offer a noninvasive alternative to intravenous morphine for postoperative analgesia in patients after third molar extraction.<sup>493</sup> Intranasal administration of remifentanil (4 mcg/kg) produces good intubating conditions in 2–3 minutes after induction of anesthesia with sevoflurane in children.<sup>494</sup>

**Inhalational.** Fentanyl (300 mcg) inhalation produces low plasma drug level (0.1 ng/mL) after 15 minutes and analgesia that may be disproportionately greater than expected.<sup>495</sup> Inhaled liposome- encapsulated fentanyl was also demonstrated to be a noninvasive route of administration with a rapid increase and prolonged maintenance of plasma fentanyl concentration.<sup>496</sup> Inhalation of nebulized fentanyl citrate significantly improved patient perception of breathing, respiratory rate, and oxygen saturation in terminally ill cancer patients,<sup>497</sup> suggesting that this inexpensive and readily available treatment may offer substantial relief of end- of- life dyspnea. The advent of specialized and efficient pulmonary drug delivery systems by the use of air- jet nebulization has facilitated the evaluation of inhaled opioids, such as morphine and fentanyl, for management of severe pain associated with surgery or malignant disease.<sup>498</sup> It was shown that remifentanil delivered by inhalation is rapidly absorbed, pharmacologically active, rapidly cleared, and noninjurious to respiratory tissues in rodents.<sup>499</sup>

**Rectal Transmucosal.** The rectal mucosa is another site for transmucosal drug delivery. The bioavailability of 30mg morphine sulfate controlled- release suppository formulation was significantly greater than that of 30- mg oral controlled- release morphine sulfate tablets, which may be the result of partial avoidance of hepatic biotransformation with rectal administration.<sup>500</sup> The hydrogel formulation of rectal morphine may also be useful for premedication and analgesia in pediatric patients.<sup>501</sup> A pharmacokinetic study in volunteers, in which three escalating doses of morphine hydrochloride (10 mg, 15 mg, and 20 mg) were administered 20 cm from the anal verge, showed that the mean morphine absorption transit time was 0.6 h, clearance 78 L/h, and absolute bioavailability 24%, and suggested that a single dose of 35 mg will provide sufficient peak serum concentration levels and a 46- mg dose four times daily is suggested to maintain clinically relevant steadystate concentrations.<sup>502</sup>

### Oral Controlled- Release Medications

Controlled- release or sustained- release dosage forms are designed to release a drug at a predetermined rate in order to maintain a constant drug concentration for a specific period of time with minimum side effects. This can be achieved through a variety of formulations, including liposomes and drug–polymer conjugates. Despite its high first- pass metabolism, morphine has been formulated into an oral, sustained- release tablet (MST) and has been evaluated for premedication, postoperative analgesia, and as an analgesic for chronic cancer pain. MST provides unreliable preoperative anxiolysis and postoperative pain relief, possibly because of delayed time to onset of peak effects (3–5 hours), which can be increased by impaired gastric emptying and absorption from the small intestine. However, in a

study comparing effects of MST (20 mg, 12 hourly) and intramuscular morphine (10 mg, 6 hourly) in patients undergoing total hip arthroplasty under lumbar spinal anesthesia, MST and intramuscular morphine gave similar levels of postoperative analgesia, suggesting that MST is a suitable alternative to the IM route in this population undergoing surgery under spinal anesthesia.<sup>503</sup> As an analgesic for chronic cancer pain, MST was shown to be an excellent formula.<sup>504</sup>

The relative analgesic potency of single doses of oral controlled- release oxycodone (20 or 40 mg) and oral controlled- release morphine (45 or 90 mg) was compared in a randomized, double- blind trial in women with moderate to severe pain following abdominal hysterectomy.<sup>505</sup> Controlled- release oxycodone at doses of 20 mg or 40 mg was comparable with controlled- release morphine at doses of 45 mg or 90 mg, respectively, for total and peak analgesic effects, indicating that oral controlled- release oxycodone was at least twice as potent as oral controlled- release morphine. A randomized, double- blind, cross- over trial indicated that controlled- release oxycodone was as safe and effective as controlled- release morphine in the treatment of cancer pain.<sup>506</sup> Growing evidence has associated chronic use of sustained-release formulation of opioids for non- cancer pain to increased risk of fatal overdose without significant benefit over other nonopioid modalities of analgesia.

### Extended- Release Epidural Morphine (DepoDur)

DepoDur is a drug that delivers morphine by using DepoFoam technology, a drug- delivery system composed of multivesicular lipid particles containing nonconcentric aqueous chambers that encapsulate the active drug. When the plasma morphine concentration after epidural administration of 5 mg of standard morphine was compared with those of 5 mg of DepoDur, the terminal half- life was comparable, but the peak concentration was significantly smaller, and the peak systemic absorption occurs later with DepoDur. Randomized controlled studies demonstrated that 5–15 mg of DepoDur can be a potentially beneficial analgesic after elective cesarean delivery with no significant increases in adverse events for the period from 24 to 48 hours after surgery.<sup>507,508</sup> When compared to singleshot plain epidural morphine, DepoDur has been shown to provide a longer duration of postoperative pain control, and decrease the use of postoperative opioid in lower abdominal surgery,<sup>509</sup> total hip arthroplasty,<sup>510</sup> and total knee arthroplasty.<sup>511</sup> The adverse-effect profile of DepoDur is similar to plain epidural morphine and includes nausea, vomiting, pruritus, and hypotension. It was reported that a large dose of epidural lidocaine before DepoDur administration alters the pharmacokinetics and drug effects of DepoDur.<sup>512</sup>

## Other Opioid Agonists

Many opioid analgesics can be used to treat pain. To achieve the balance between adequate pain control and adverse effects, the selection and dosage of opioids need to be taken into consideration. Opioid rotation refers to a switch from one opioid to another to improve the response to analgesic

<!-- PDF page 50 -->

therapy or reduce adverse effects, and the analgesic potency and the pharmacokinetic properties should be considered to plan adequate opioid rotation. Equianalgesic tables have been utilized historically to guide dose of analgesic therapy, but there is no widely accepted guideline for opioid equianalgesic conversions. Table 22.9 shows an example of opioid equianalgesic conversion based on equianalgesia tables from 32 institutions.<sup>513</sup>

### CODEINE

Codeine (methylmorphine) is approximately one- half as potent as morphine, has a high oral–parenteral potency ratio (2:3) and a plasma half- life of 2 to 3 hours. Codeine has mild to moderate analgesic but strong cough- suppressant properties after oral administration. Cytochrome P450 2D6 (CYP2D6) was identified as the enzyme responsible for _O_ - demethylation of codeine to morphine, whereas CYP3A4 was shown to be involved in _N_ - demethylation of codeine to norcodeine. Therefore, co- administration of codeine with selective inhibitors of CYP3A4 – for example, cimetidine, erythromycin, and grapefruit juice – may result in increased morphine production and enhanced pharmacodynamic effects due to shunting down the CYP2D6 pathway.<sup>514</sup> IV codeine produces profound hypotension and is neither approved nor recommended.

### OXYCODONE

In humans, oxycodone is converted into several metabolites by CYP2D6 and CYP3A4 (Fig. 22.35). The CYP2D6 enzyme is principally responsible for the conversion of oxycodone to oxymorphone, an active metabolite with potent opioid receptor agonist activity. The CYP2D6 gene is highly polymorphic with encoded protein activities, ranging from non- functioning to high- functioning enzymes, and several pharmacogenetic studies have shown the importance of

CYP2D6- mediated conversion of oxycodone to oxymorphone for analgesic efficacy.<sup>515</sup> Rifampin, a strong inducer of several drug- metabolizing enzymes, induces CYP3A, reduces the plasma concentration of intravenous and oral oxycodone, and modestly attenuates pharmacologic effects of oxycodone.<sup>516</sup> Oxycodone is a potent analgesic after systemic administration, but its analgesic potency is poor after intrathecal administration.<sup>517</sup> It was shown that oxycodone is more potent than morphine for visceral pain relief in intravenous patient- controlled postoperative analgesia after laparoscopic hysterectomy.<sup>518</sup> With respect to pharmacologic effects other than analgesic effects, it was reported that the extent and speed of onset of oxycodoneinduced respiratory depression was dose- dependent and greater than an equivalent dose of morphine.<sup>519</sup> It should be noted that misuse of oxycodone, high abuse liability of which may be based on its unique pharmacologic profile different from morphine, greatly contributed to the opioid crisis.<sup>520</sup>

### MEPERIDINE (PETHIDINE)

Meperidine is predominantly a μ- opioid receptor agonist that produces pharmacologic effects similar but not identical to those of morphine. Meperidine sometimes causes excitation of the CNS, characterized by tremors, muscle twitches and seizures, that are largely due to accumulation of a metabolite, normeperidine. Meperidine has well- known local anesthetic properties.

Unlike morphine, after intravenous injection, first- pass uptake of meperidine by the lungs is approximately 65%. Meperidine is more highly bound to plasma proteins than is morphine, principally (70%) to α1- acid glycoprotein. As with morphine, a relatively high hepatic extraction ratio results in biotransformation that depends on hepatic blood flow. The major metabolite normeperidine has analgesic activity and is roughly twice as potent as meperidine in producing seizures in animals. The elimination half- life of normeperidine is considerably greater than that of meperidine, and thus repeated doses can easily produce accumulation of this toxic metabolite in patients with renal disease, with the potential for inducing seizures.

Meperidine is frequently used for postoperative pain management, but meperidine (50–100 mg IV) produces variable degrees of pain relief and is not always effective in patients with severe pain. A comparative study demonstrated that morphine, meperidine, and tramadol resulted in equivalent pain scores in intravenous PCA after abdominal hysterectomy.<sup>521</sup> Meperidine is also used to relieve pain and distress in women in labor.<sup>522</sup> Intravenous meperidine (50 mg) and butorphanol (1 mg) significantly reduced pain intensity 15 minutes after injection in women with moderate to severe labor pain, but the analgesia was often inadequate.<sup>523</sup> Meperidine (12.5 to 35 mg) is effective for prevention and treatment of postoperative shivering.<sup>165,524</sup>

### HYDROMORPHONE

_PO_ , _per os_ (by mouth); _IV_ , intravenous. From Wen RY, Atayee RS, Edmonds KP. A comparison of institutional opioid equianalgesia tools: a national study. _J Palliat Med_ . 2022;25:1686.

Hydromorphone is structurally related to morphine but is approximately five to ten times as potent. Because
