A bolus administration of hydromorphone reaches its peak effect in approximately 20 min, whereas an equivalent morphine bolus will require 94 minutes to reach peak. Analgesia after hydromorphone lasts 4 to 5 hours. Hydromorphone has been used for both acute and chronic pain conditions in adults and children.<sup>526</sup> It was reported that hydromorphone PCA provided adequate postoperative analgesia in gynecologic surgery patients and there was no systematic difference between morphine and hydromorphone in opioid- related side effects.<sup>527</sup>

### LEVORPHANOL

Levorphanol is the only available semisynthetic opioid agonist of the morphinan series with a long halflife. It is five times as potent as morphine with an IM/ oral potency ratio of 1:2. Levorphanol may have utility in patients with chronic pain and who demonstrate morphine tolerance, perhaps because of differences in opioid receptor activity. Analgesia produced by levorphanol is mediated via its interactions with μ- , δ- , and κ- opioid receptors. Levorphanol has many similarities to methadone,<sup>528</sup> and is an NMDA receptor antagonist. The long half- life of the drug increases the potential for drug accumulation.<sup>529</sup> Methadone

Methadone is a potent μ- opioid receptor agonist, with the longest half- life among the clinically used opioids. By virtue of one of methadone’s isomers, it also exerts an

<!-- PDF page 52 -->

inhibitory effect on NMDA receptors, which are implicated in the development of opioid tolerance, hyperalgesia, and chronic pain. Furthermore, methadone inhibits the reuptake of serotonin and norepinephrine, which may play a role in antinociception and mood elevation. Its unique pharmacology and cellular effects make it a valuable agent in the treatment of both acute and chronic pain of various etiologies. Methadone itself is associated with less abuse potential than either morphine or heroin, and because of its long half- life and high oral bioavailability, methadone has had a primary role in the outpatient treatment of patients with a history of opioid abuse or addiction.<sup>530</sup> Thus, treatment with methadone has been considered to be the “gold standard” for medicationassisted treatment (MAT), a comprehensive treatment program for OUD.

Methadone has an equivalent potency (IV) but longer duration of action than morphine. The plasma half- life of methadone is very long and variable (13–100 hours), but many patients require dosing every 4 to 8 hours to maintain analgesic effects. Its major clinical applications are in the prevention of opioid withdrawal symptoms and in the treatment of chronic pain. It was shown that the same dose of IV methadone (20 mg) that is effective for postoperative pain was also suitable for the induction of anesthesia in combination with etomidate, and that substantial increases in plasma histamine occurred in 2 of 23 patients who received methadone.<sup>531</sup> In patients undergoing posterior spinal fusion surgery, methadone 0.2 mg/kg at the start of surgery reduced postoperative opioid requirements, decreased pain scores, and improved patient satisfaction.<sup>532</sup> However, perioperative use of methadone represents a major clinical challenge, given the tremendous interpatient half- life variability which could contribute to unexpected postoperative respiratory depression. Methadone can increase the risk of prolongation of the corrected QT (QTc) interval in electrocardiography and subsequent development of torsades de pointes, a life- threatening cardiac arrhythmia.<sup>263</sup>

### OXYMORPHONE

Oxymorphone is a semisynthetic opioid agonist that is specific for the μ- opioid receptor and approved to treat both acute and chronic pain. Due to extensive liver metabolism, oral oxymorphone is contraindicated in patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment.<sup>533</sup> Oxymorphone, structurally related to morphine, is almost ten times as potent, but has a similar duration of action. It was shown that in patients with acute moderate to severe postsurgical pain, oral immediate- release oxymorphone 10, 20, or 30 mg provided significant dose- related pain relief compared with placebo, and this relief was maintained over several days with a safety profile comparable to that of immediate- release oxycodone.<sup>534</sup>

### PIRITRAMIDE

Piritramide, a synthetic opioid structurally related to meperidine, acting on the μ- opioid receptor is often used for postoperative analgesia in several European countries.<sup>535</sup> Lack of hemodynamic effects and less side effects

reported in early work made piritramide more suitable for postoperative pain control compared with other potent opioids. Its relative analgesic potency compared with morphine is approximately 0.7. Intramuscular bolus injections of 7.5–15 mg were considered to be adequate for approximately 4–6 hours.<sup>535</sup> Pharmacokinetic analysis showed that piritramide is distributed extensively and eliminated slowly, and recommended for intermittent bolus administration.<sup>536</sup> A randomized controlled trial showed that intravenous PCA with piritramide can produce pain management as satisfactory as oral oxycodone after cesarean section.<sup>537</sup> It was also demonstrated that piritramide is at least as effective and as well tolerated as morphine for postoperative analgesia via a PCA system after hysterectomy.<sup>538</sup>

### TRAMADOL

Tramadol is a prodrug that is metabolized by CYP2D6 to its more potent opioid analgesic metabolites, particularly the _O_ - demethylation product M1. Because there is a significant variability in the efficiency and amount of CYP2D6 enzymes among individuals, the large phenotypic variation affects the speed of metabolism and the rate of accumulation or elimination of tramadol.<sup>539</sup>

The action of tramadol to induce analgesia represents the combination of two predominant mechanisms, reuptake inhibition of norepinephrine and serotonin, and activation of the μ- opioid receptor and to a lesser extent the δ- and κ- opioid receptors.<sup>540</sup> Tramadol is a unique racemic mixture, and each of the two racemic isomers inhibits norepinephrine and serotonin reuptake, respectively.<sup>541</sup> It was suggested that tramadol may also have a direct serotoninreleasing action.<sup>542</sup> Given the analgesic effect of tramadol is only partially reversed by naloxone, its serotonergic and noradrenergic effects likely represent its predominant analgesic action.

Tramadol is one- fifth to one- tenth as potent as morphine. In rats, tramadol could reduce MAC of isoflurane in a naloxone- sensitive manner.<sup>543</sup> It was shown that intravenously administered tramadol was effective for postthoracotomy pain relief.<sup>544</sup> Analgesic doses of tramadol may produce less respiratory depression than the other opioids in part because of its nonopioid receptor–mediated analgesic actions, and have minimal effects on gastrointestinal motor function.<sup>545</sup> Nevertheless, tramadol used as a sole drug cannot be considered the drug of choice after moderately to severe painful surgery because of a significant variability in the efficiency. The dose of tramadol needed to relieve pain in 80% of patients (ED80) was 260 mg, and much larger than the usual dose of 50–100 mg.<sup>546</sup>

Tramadol is also used as an adjunct to regional anesthesia. Tramadol added to lidocaine for intravenous regional anesthesia provided a shorter onset time of sensory block.<sup>547</sup> Tramadol added to 1.5% mepivacaine for brachial plexus block enhances the duration of analgesia in a dose- dependent manner with acceptable side effects.<sup>548</sup> Intra- articular tramadol was also used for management and prevention of pain after arthroscopic knee surgery. It was shown that the intra- articular admixture of tramadol 100 mg with 0.25% bupivacaine provides a pronounced prolongation of analgesia

<!-- PDF page 53 -->

compared with either drug alone in patients undergoing arthroscopic knee surgery.<sup>549</sup>

Tramadol has dose- and time- dependent bactericidal activity against _Escherichia coli_ and _Staphylococcus epidermidis_ , as well as antibacterial activity against _Staphylococcus aureus_ and _Pseudomonas aeruginosa_ . The antibacterial properties of tramadol might be useful for reduction of bacterial infection after regional anesthesia.<sup>550</sup>

Co- administration of tramadol with proserotonergic medications can result in a hyperserotonergic state, serotonin syndrome, which can be subacute or chronic and range from mild to severe.<sup>551</sup> In mild cases, patients are afebrile and may report symptoms of diarrhea, tremor, tachycardia, shivering, diaphoresis, or mydriasis.<sup>552</sup> In severe cases, neuromuscular hyperactivity, autonomic hyperactivity, altered mental state, gastrointestinal symptoms, and even death have been reported. Serotonergic medications that can interact with tramadol include selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, serotonin–norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors, tricyclic antidepressants, triptans (e.g., sumatriptan), antipsychotics, anticonvulsants, antiparkinsonian agents, cough and cold medications containing dextromethorphan, herbal products containing St. John’s wort, and medications that inhibit the metabolism of serotonin, such as monoamine oxidase inhibitors.<sup>551</sup> Because symptoms of serotonin and norepinephrine withdrawal are possible upon abrupt cessation of tramadol, gradual tapering or symptomatic support are necessary when tramadol administration is stopped.<sup>539</sup>

### TAPENTADOL

Analgesic effect of tapentadol is mediated by μ- opioid receptor activation, with potency between morphine and tramadol, and inhibition of norepinephrine reuptake into nerve endings. Distinct from tramadol, the analgesic effect of tapentadol does not depend on metabolism mediated by CYP2D6 and inhibition of serotonin reuptake.<sup>541</sup> It was also reported that tapentadol does not appear to cause the confusional states sometimes associated with tramadol. In various studies, tapentadol has been found to be effective even for severe postoperative pain.<sup>553</sup> Tapentadol consistently demonstrated superior gastrointestinal tolerability, including nausea, vomiting, and constipation at doses that provide comparable efficacy with oxycodone.<sup>554</sup>

### MORPHINE- 6- GLUCURONIDE

Morphine- 6- glucuronide (M6G) is a potent metabolite of morphine, and its affinity for the μ- opioid receptor is higher than morphine itself.<sup>377</sup> In contrast to morphine, M6G is not metabolized but excreted via the kidneys and exhibits enterohepatic cycling, as it is a substrate for multidrug resistance transporter proteins in the liver and intestines.<sup>555</sup> M6G exhibits a delay in its analgesic effect (blood effectsite equilibration half- life 4–8 h), which is partly related to slow passage through the blood–brain barrier and distribution within the brain compartment. M6G can be used as an analgesic in humans. Osborne _et al._ reported that 0.5–4 mg IV of M6G was effective for cancer pain for 2–24 hours without nausea and vomiting.<sup>556</sup> M6G 100 mcg and 125 mcg given intrathecally provided excellent analgesia

after total hip replacement, as did intrathecal morphine sulfate 500 mcg.<sup>557</sup> In a randomized double- blind study it was shown that M6G has an analgesic effect similar to that of morphine over the first 24 hours postoperatively. However, initial onset of M6G effect may be slower than morphine.<sup>558</sup> M6G has also paradoxically been observed to increase pain sensitivity in mice and humans. The pronociceptive effect of M6G can be shown in knockout mice lacking μ- , κ- , and δ- opioid receptors, and might be due to activation of the NMDA receptors.<sup>559</sup>

## Agonist–Antagonist Opioid Compounds

Nalorphine, the first agonist–antagonist opioid, was successfully synthesized by Weijland and Erickson in 1942 and was found to be strongly antagonistic to almost all the properties of morphine. Although nalorphine was found to possess strong analgesic actions, it was unsuitable for clinical uses because of its psychotomimetic effects. Nalorphine was used in lower doses as an opioid antagonist.

Agonist–antagonist opioids are usually produced by alkylation of the piperidine nitrogen of the morphine molecule. Buprenorphine is a partial agonist at the μ- receptor and an antagonist at the κ- receptor. The other compounds are μ- antagonists and full or partial agonists at the κ- receptors. Agonist–antagonist opioids are less prone (but not immune) to abuse because they have analgesic ceiling effects, cause less euphoria, and are associated with less drug- seeking behavior and physical dependence.

Dosing data of these compounds are shown in Table 22.10. The agonist–antagonists compounds depress respiration similarly to morphine, but ceiling effects exist<sup>560</sup> (Table 22.11). Effects on the cardiovascular system differ among these compounds<sup>560</sup> (Table 22.12).

### PENTAZOCINE

Pentazocine is one- half to one- fourth as potent as morphine as an analgesic, and analgesia by pentazocine is primarily related to κ- receptor stimulation. Ceilings to both analgesia and respiratory depression were suggested, but the ceiling doses were not clearly reported, probably because the psychotomimetic effects of pentazocine can make it difficult to study the dose–response relationship in humans. Although the potential for abuse is less than with morphine, prolonged use of

pentazocine can lead to physical dependence. Nalorphine- like dysphoric side effects are common, especially after high doses (>60 mg) of pentazocine in the elderly. The dysphoric effects of pentazocine can be reversed with naloxone. Pentazocine depresses myocardial contractility and increases arterial blood pressure, heart rate, systemic vascular resistance, pulmonary artery pressure, and left ventricular work index. Pentazocine also increases blood catecholamine levels. Pentazocine inhibits gastric emptying and gastrointestinal transit in rats, whereas U50488H, a pure κ- agonist, did not significantly inhibit either.<sup>561</sup> Therefore, it might be speculated that pentazocine affects gastrointestinal function through a mechanism other than the opioid receptors.

Pentazocine has been shown to be an effective treatment for pruritus after cesarean delivery under spinal anesthesia with opioids, the incidence of which ranges from 50% to 100%. A single 15- mg dose of IV pentazocine after delivery was shown to reduce both the incidence and severity of pruritus in women who have received subarachnoid opioids during cesarean delivery.<sup>562</sup> Pentazocine has limited application, because it is associated with a high incidence of postoperative nausea and vomiting, it provides limited analgesia, it partially antagonizes other opioids, and it can produce undesirable cardiovascular and psychotomimetic effects.

### BUTORPHANOL

Butorphanol is an agonist at κ- receptors. Its activity at μ- receptors is either antagonistic or partially agonistic. It is five to eight times as potent as morphine and is only available in

parenteral form. After IM injection, onset of effect is rapid, and peak analgesia occurs within 1 hour. Whereas duration of action of butorphanol is similar to that of morphine, its plasma half- life is only 2 to 3 hours. Although butorphanol (10 mg IM) causes as much respiratory depression as the same dose of morphine, higher doses reach a ceiling. Side effects after butorphanol include drowsiness, sweating, nausea, and CNS stimulation. In healthy volunteers, butorphanol (0.03 or 0.06 mg/kg IV) produces no or minimal cardiovascular changes. However, in patients with cardiac disease, butorphanol causes significant increases in cardiac index, left ventricular end- diastolic pressure, and pulmonary artery pressure.

Because butorphanol decreases the MAC for enflurane by only a small fraction, it cannot serve like the fentanyl congeners as a component of balanced anesthesia. Butorphanol is subject to less abuse and has less addictive potential than morphine or fentanyl. Acute biliary spasm can occur after butorphanol but increases in biliary pressure are less than after equipotent doses of fentanyl or morphine. Transnasal butorphanol is effective in relieving migraine and postoperative pain.<sup>563</sup>

### BUPRENORPHINE

######

Buprenorphine is a thebaine derivative with μ- receptor partial agonist activity approximately 33 times more potent than morphine and is also a κ- receptor antagonist. Whereas fentanyl dissociates rapidly from μ- receptors (half- life of 6.8 minutes), buprenorphine has a higher affinity and takes much longer (half- life of 166 minutes) to dissociate. Onset of action of buprenorphine is slow, its peak effect may not occur until 3 hours, and the duration of effect is prolonged (&lt;10 h). Volume of distribution of buprenorphine is 2.8 L/kg and its clearance is 20 mL/kg/min. Plasma concentrations of the metabolites of buprenorphine, norbuprenorphine, buprenorphine- 3- glucuronide, and norbuprenorphine- 3- glucuronide may approximate or exceed those of the parent drug. Both glucuronide metabolites are biologically active and may contribute to the overall pharmacology of buprenorphine.<sup>564</sup>

Subjective effects (e.g., euphoria) of buprenorphine are similar to morphine. Buprenorphine produces depression of minute ventilation which leveled off at doses higher than 3.0 mcg/kg to about 50% of baseline, in contrast to the case of fentanyl, in which dose- dependent respiratory depression results in apnea at doses higher than 2.9 mcg/kg<sup>565</sup> (Fig. 22.36). Buprenorphine has been successfully used for premedication (0.3 mg IM), as the analgesic component in balanced anesthesia (4.5 to 12 mcg/kg), and for postoperative pain control (0.3 mg IM). Buprenorphine, like the other agonist–antagonist compounds, is not acceptable as a sole anesthetic, and its receptor kinetic profile restricts its usefulness if other μ- agonists are used. Opioid withdrawal symptoms develop slowly (5–10 days) after buprenorphine is discontinued following long- term use. A recent systematic review reported that, while a theoretical ceiling effect may exist with respect to respiratory depression by buprenorphine, in a clinical setting, it can still cause significant adverse effects on respiratory function.<sup>566</sup> However, given that buprenorphine is an equally efficacious analgesic agent, it is a useful alternative opioid because of its ease of administration and reduced incidence of pruritus.

Comparison of the respiratory effects of intravenous buprenorphine and fentanyl in humans and rats. _Br J Anaesth._ 2005;94:825–834.)

A sublingual combination tablet composed of buprenorphine and naloxone in a fixed 4:1 ratio may provide pain relief in patients with chronic pain with OUD, and is one of the FDA- approved medications for OUD.<sup>567</sup> However, the unique pharmacologic profile of buprenorphine/ naloxone confers it to be a weak analgesic relative to full μ- receptor agonists. Possible mechanisms of pain relief by buprenorphine/naloxone therapy in opioid- dependent patients with chronic pain may include reversal of opioidinduced hyperalgesia and improvement in opioid tolerance and addiction.<sup>568</sup>

Acute administration of κ- opioid receptor agonists and antagonists has been shown to produce effects characterized as prodepressive or antidepressive, respectively, in rodent behavioral tests, encouraging further investigation of κ- opioid receptor antagonists as potential antidepressive treatments in humans.<sup>569</sup> In a preliminary clinical study, low doses of buprenorphine, which possess κ- receptor antagonist activity, produced impressive alleviation of depressive symptoms within 1 week of initiating administration in patients that were considered treatment- resistant.<sup>570</sup> The potential of using buprenorphine for treating major depressive disorder, treatment- resistant depression, and posttraumatic stress disorder has been reported.<sup>571</sup>

### NALBUPHINE

Nalbuphine is an agonist–antagonist opioid that is structurally related to oxymorphone and naloxone and binds to μ- receptors as well as to κ- and δ- receptors. Nalbuphine acts as an antagonist at the μ- receptor and an agonist at the κ- receptor. Nalbuphine, like other agonist–antagonist compounds, interferes with the analgesia produced by pure μ- agonists. The most common adverse effects following nalbuphine administration are sedation, sweating,

nausea, vomiting, dizziness, vertigo, dry mouth, or headache. Nalbuphine is only available for parenteral use. The recommended single maximum dose is 20 mg in an opioid-nontolerant individual with a maximum daily dose of 160 mg. In opioid- dependent patients, the dose should be reduced by 25%, and the patient should be observed for signs of opioid withdrawal. Onset of effects is rapid (5 to 10 minutes), and duration is long (3 to 6 hours) because of a long plasma elimination half- life (5 hours).

Nalbuphine has been administered as an analgesic supplement for conscious sedation or balanced anesthesia and as an analgesic for postoperative and chronic pain problems. In patients undergoing myocardial revascularization, continuous infusion of nalbuphine (0.05–0.1 mg/kg/min) and fentanyl (0.15–0.3 mcg/kg/min) was compared.<sup>572</sup> It was shown that nalbuphine lacks the ability to attenuate cardiovascular and hormonal responses to tracheal intubation and surgical procedures, and was concluded that continuous infusion of nalbuphine cannot be recommended for anesthesia in patients undergoing myocardial revascularization. For postoperative patient- controlled epidural analgesia, the combination of hydromorphone 0.075 mg/mL and nalbuphine 0.04 mg/mL resulted in lower incidence of nausea and decreased need of bladder catheterization compared with hydromorphone alone.<sup>573</sup> A randomized double- blind controlled study in patients undergoing gynecologic operations demonstrated that interaction between morphine and nalbuphine in intravenous PCA admixture is additive, and combination of the two drugs can decrease the incidence of pruritus.<sup>574</sup>

A prospective randomized double- blinded study demonstrated that nalbuphine (4 mg IV) was as effective as ondansetron (4–8 mg IV) for prevention of intrathecal morphine- induced pruritus after cesarean delivery.<sup>575</sup> Some reports showed that nalbuphine provides a rapid and

<!-- PDF page 56 -->

potent antishivering effect similar to meperidine.<sup>576</sup> However, a quantitative systematic review of randomized controlled trials did not support the conclusion.<sup>165</sup>

### DEZOCINE

Dezocine is slightly more potent and acts faster than morphine, with a similar duration. Although no longer used clinically in Western countries, dezocine is gaining popularity in China as an alternative medication for perioperative pain management. A pharmacologic study showed the unique molecular pharmacologic profile of dezocine as a partial μ- receptor agonist, a κ- receptor antagonist, and a norepinephrine and serotonin reuptake inhibitor ( _via_ norepinephrine transporter and serotonin transporter).<sup>317</sup> Although dezocine was shown to be an effective alternative to fentanyl when administered during outpatient laparoscopy with propofol and nitrous oxide, dezocine was associated with a high incidence of postoperative nausea and a delayed discharge time.<sup>577</sup> In adult patients who had arthroscopic surgery under general anesthesia, dezocine (5 mg IV) and morphine (5 mg IV) were similarly effective for postoperative analgesia and showed similar side effects.<sup>578</sup> A recent meta- analysis showed that dezocine significantly reduced the incidence and severity of opioidinduced cough in the induction of general anesthesia, but had no significant effect on vital signs.<sup>579</sup>

## Opioid Antagonists

### NALOXONE

Clinically, opioid antagonists are used to restore spontaneous ventilation in patients who breathe inadequately after opioid overdoses or opioid anesthesia. In addition, opioid antagonists can reduce or reverse opioid- induced nausea and vomiting, pruritus, urinary retention, rigidity, and biliary spasm associated with numerous therapies employing opioids.

Although naloxone is generally considered to be a pure opioid receptor antagonist, there have been reports on the effect of naloxone which cannot be explained by opioid antagonistic activity of naloxone. For example, in postoperative patients on morphine patient- controlled analgesia, low- dose naloxone (0.25 mcg/kg/h) caused the morphine consumption to reduce by 30%.<sup>580</sup> This apparent paradoxical antinociceptive effect of naloxone might involve release of endogenous opioids enhanced by inhibition of presynaptic autoinhibition by naloxone. There are no studies that describe the transition from low analgesia-promoting doses of naloxone to higher doses that antagonize analgesia, but it was suggested that naloxone doses &lt;25 mcg (administered in 1 minute) will enhance analgesia, whereas higher doses will counteract analgesia.<sup>581</sup> Regarding the opioid agonistic activity of naloxone, there have been several reports. High- dose naloxone possesses partial agonistic activity on the μ- and κ- opioid receptors in the cultured cells.<sup>582</sup>

### Reversal of Respiratory Depression by Naloxone

In the early 1950s, nalorphine and levallorphan were evaluated as opioid antagonists. They were often found

unacceptable because of a high incidence of side effects, as well as incomplete reversal of respiratory depression. Naloxone was introduced into clinical practice in the late 1960s. There have been reports of side effects (increases in heart rate and blood pressure) and more serious complications (e.g., pulmonary edema). Initial naloxone dose recommendations range from 0.4 to 0.8 mg. Onset of action of IV naloxone is rapid (1–2 minutes), and half- life and duration of effect are short, approximately 30 to 60 minutes. If IV access is not available, naloxone, in doses similar to those given IV, is effectively absorbed after intratracheal administration. Effect of antagonism with naloxone on opioidinduced respiratory depression under different conditions were simulated by the use of the pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic model.<sup>581</sup> When naloxone is administered for reversal of respiratory depression by buprenorphine, the effect of naloxone is limited by high affinity for and slow dissociation from the μ- opioid receptor of buprenorphine, and depends on the buprenorphine dose and the correct naloxone dose window<sup>583</sup> (Fig. 22.37). Because respiratory depression from buprenorphine may outlast the effects of naloxone boluses or short infusions, a continuous infusion of naloxone may be required to maintain reversal of respiratory depression.<sup>583</sup>

Several mechanisms produce increases in arterial blood pressure, heart rate, and other significant hemodynamic alterations after naloxone reversal of opioids. These include pain, rapid awakening, and sympathetic activation not necessarily due to pain. When patients receiving naloxone for opioid agonist reversal are hypothermic due to intraoperative heat loss, O2 consumption and minute ventilation can increase two- to threefold.<sup>584</sup> Such metabolic demands also stress the cardiovascular system, increasing cardiac output. In addition, greater degrees of hypercapnia at the time of opioid antagonism will result in greater degrees of cardiovascular stimulation because of associated sympathetic stimulation. Opioid reversal may be particularly hazardous in patients with pheochromocytoma or chromaffin tissue tumors. It was proposed that naloxone triggers a centrally mediated catecholamine surge, which results in an adrenergic crisis, leading to shifts in blood volume from the systemic to the pulmonary vascular bed, which increases permeability and, subsequently, pulmonary edema.<sup>585</sup> However, it was also reported that IV administration of 10 mg naloxone did not significantly change plasma catecholamine concentration and blood pressure in pheochromocytoma patients.<sup>586</sup>

Recurrence of respiratory depression after naloxone is due to the short half- life of naloxone. “Renarcotization” occurs more frequently after the use of naloxone to reverse longeracting opioids such as morphine. This is commonly not seen in clinical practice because opioid concentrations are often just above the threshold for respiratory depression, and treatment with a single or just a few effective bolus doses of naloxone is sufficient to reverse the respiratory depression induced by most opioids for the short time that the agonist concentration exceeds the respiratory depression threshold.<sup>235</sup> Short- acting opioids such as alfentanil rarely pose a danger of renarcotization, because of a rapid plasma decay curve and weak opioid receptor binding compared with fentanyl and sufentanil. Naloxone, although active at μ- , δ- , and κ- receptors, has greatest affinity for μ- receptors, which

### Treatment of Opioid Overdose with Naloxone

The opioid crisis came in three waves: increased prescription, usage, and opioid overdose deaths from 1999 to 2010; increased overdose deaths from heroin started in 2010; and the rise in the use of fentanyl and other synthetic opioids started in 2013.<sup>587</sup> Opioid overdose is frequently treated with naloxone in out- of- hospital settings by emergency medical services (EMS) personnel and laypersons. The Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration recommended coprescription of naloxone in patients with history of opioid or substance use disorder, those who took benzodiazepine with the opioid, and those who are no longer tolerant (after incarceration or after detoxification program). Naloxone prescribing was also emphasized in the 2016 CDC Guideline for Prescribing Opioids for Chronic Pain, which specifically recommends considering offering naloxone when factors that increase risk for opioid overdose, such as history of overdose, history of substance use disorder, opioid dosages ≥50 morphine milligram equivalents (MME)/day, or concurrent benzodiazepine use are present.<sup>588</sup> Naloxone is available as an injectable formulation and a nasal spray. Compared to the injectable formulation, the nasal spray is easier to use, and there is no risk of a needle stick, and a preparation capable of delivering a higher concentration is available. The 2- mg/mL intranasal naloxone appears to have efficacy similar to the 2- mg intramuscular naloxone for reversal of opioid overdose.<sup>589</sup>

### Other Applications of Naloxone

Low- dose naloxone not only has been shown to ameliorate undesired opioid- induced side effects but also to block the development of acute opioid tolerance. Naloxone infusion 0.25 mcg/kg/h prevented the acute opioid tolerance induced by large doses of remifentanil (0.30 mcg/kg/min)

in patients undergoing open colorectal surgery, and cumulative morphine consumption at 24 hours after surgery was significantly reduced. Furthermore, ultra- low- dose naloxone infusion provided a quicker recovery of bowel function, and reduced the length of hospital stay.<sup>590</sup>

Reversal of effects of nonopioid drugs such as alcohol, barbiturates, and benzodiazepines by naloxone has been reported. However, it was also reported that naloxone potentiates anxiolytic- like action of benzodiazepines and barbiturates in the rat.<sup>591</sup> Although it was suggested that the μ- opioid receptor is involved in the antinociceptive action of ketamine in mice,<sup>73</sup> naloxone did not affect the action of ketamine on secondary hyperalgesia induced by burn injury in humans.<sup>592</sup> Thus, it is not advisable to try to reverse the effects of overdose of nonopioid drugs by naloxone.

Evidence indicates that endogenous opioid peptides participate in the control of cardiovascular regulation during hemorrhagic shock. In patients with documented septic shock and resistance to a one- liter fluid challenge, administration of naloxone by initial bolus of 0.03 mg/kg followed by infusion at a rate of 0.2 mg/kg/h significantly increased mean arterial pressure, but did not affect survival.<sup>593</sup> This effect may be due to centrally mediated increases in sympathetic tone and decreases in parasympathetic output and/or antagonism of endogenous opioids. A meta- analysis involving 126 patients with septic, cardiogenic, hemorrhagic, or spinal shock showed that naloxone therapy was associated with statistically significant hemodynamic improvement in mean arterial pressure (weighted mean difference +9.33 mm Hg), but the effect of naloxone on the mortality was not clear.<sup>297</sup>

One study indicated that opioids can elicit excitatory as well as inhibitory modulation of the action potentials of sensory neurons, and ultra- low doses of naloxone could selectively block the excitatory effect of opioids in mice.<sup>594</sup> A prospective randomized double- blind study demonstrated that addition of an ultra- low dose of naloxone (100 ng) to 34 mL of 1.5% lidocaine solution with or without fentanyl

<!-- PDF page 58 -->

in an axillary brachial plexus block for forearm surgery lengthens the onset time of motor and sensory blockade, delays the first postoperative pain, and prolongs motor blockade.<sup>595</sup> This finding may suggest that the excitatory effect of endogenous opioid is involved in the postoperative pain that was blocked by the low dose of naloxone.

It was previously reported that combined use of cerebrospinal fluid drainage and naloxone reduces the risk of paraplegia in thoracoabdominal aneurysm repair.<sup>596</sup> Currently, naloxone infusion is often used as a component of multimodal therapy to treat symptomatic spinal cord ischemia after thoracic endovascular aortic repair,<sup>597</sup> but effectiveness of naloxone is not clearly shown. Naloxone may also be used as an antipruritic drug in the case of pruritus induced by causes other than opioid drugs.<sup>598</sup> Several randomized controlled studies have reported significant improvement of pruritus in cholestasis, chronic urticaria, and atopic dermatitis by opioid antagonists including naloxone, naltrexone, and nalmefene, but, in view of the side effects and costs, opioid antagonists can only be recommended as second- line treatment in these conditions.<sup>599</sup>

### NALTREXONE

Naltrexone is a μ- , δ- , and κ- opioid receptor antagonist. It is longer acting than naloxone (plasma half- life of 8–12 versus 0.5–1.5 hours) and can be administered orally. A double- blind, placebo- controlled study for patients undergoing cesarean section indicated that naltrexone (6 mg) was an effective oral prophylactic against the pruritus and vomiting associated with intrathecal morphine, but was associated with shorter duration of analgesia.<sup>600</sup>

As a prescribed treatment for opioid dependence, extended- release naltrexone hydrochloride was compared directly with opioid medication treatment, which includes daily buprenorphine hydrochloride with naloxone hydrochloride. It was shown that extended- release naltrexone, administered 380 mg as monthly intramuscular injections, was as effective as buprenorphine–naloxone in maintaining short- term abstinence from heroin and other illicit substances and should be considered as a treatment option for opioid- dependent individuals.<sup>214</sup> Importantly, naltrexone induction requires individuals to be completely off opioids due to the likelihood of severe opioid withdrawal. Randomized, placebo- controlled clinical trials that investigated the oral or intramuscular long- acting formulation of naltrexone in substance use disorders or behavioral addictions such as pathologic gambling, kleptomania, and trichotillomania showed efficacy and safety of naltrexone in treating these addictions.<sup>601</sup> Broad anti- addiction efficacy of naltrexone might suggest a shared role of the endogenous opioid system in the pathophysiology of a number of addictive disorders.

### NALMEFENE

Nalmefene has a greater preference for μ- than δ- or κ- receptors and is equipotent to naloxone. Nalmefene is long acting after oral (0.5–3.0 mg/kg) and parenteral (0.2– 2.0 mg/kg) administration. Bioavailability after oral administration is 40% to 50%, and peak plasma concentrations

are reached in 1 to 2 hours. The mean terminal elimination half- life of nalmefene is 8.5 hours, compared with 1 hour for naloxone. Prophylactic administration of lowdose nalmefene (15 or 25 mg IV) significantly decreased the need for antiemetics and antipruritic medications in patients receiving intravenous patient- controlled analgesia with morphine.<sup>602</sup> Similarly to naloxone and naltrexone, several studies have evaluated the efficacy of nalmefene as an antipruritic agent.<sup>599</sup>

### PERIPHERALLY ACTING μ- OPIOID RECEPTOR ANTAGONISTS

The American Gastroenterological Association (AGA) recommended the use of laxatives as the first line in patients with opioid- induced constipation (OIC). In cases of inadequate responses to laxatives, peripherally acting μ- opioid receptor antagonists (PAMORAs) are recommended.<sup>309</sup>

### Methylnaltrexone

Methylnaltrexone is the first quaternary ammonium opioid receptor antagonist that does not cross the blood–brain barrier.<sup>301</sup> It can reverse adverse effects of opioid medications mediated by peripheral opioid receptors, while the opioid effects mediated by opioid receptors in the central nervous system, such as analgesia, are not affected. Delayed gastric emptying induced by 0.09 mg/kg morphine could be attenuated by 0.3 mg/kg methylnaltrexone in healthy volunteers.<sup>307</sup>

Methylnaltrexone is subcutaneously or orally administered for treatment of OIC in patients with advanced medical illness.<sup>603</sup> It was reported that subcutaneous methylnaltrexone (0.15 mg/kg) rapidly induced laxation in patients who had received opioids for two or more weeks and who had received stable doses of opioids and laxatives for three or more days without relief of OIC.<sup>604</sup> On the other hand, methylnaltrexone (12 mg once daily) did not affect opioidmediated analgesia in patients with chronic non-cancer pain suffering from OIC who received more than 50 mg of oral morphine equivalents daily.<sup>605</sup> Randomized doubleblind placebo- controlled studies have shown effectiveness of methylnaltrexone and alvimopan, another peripherally acting opioid receptor antagonist, in management of postoperative ileus, a transient cessation of coordinate bowel function after surgery, in patients who received opioidbased IV PCA.<sup>301</sup>

### Naloxegol

Naloxegol, a PEGylated derivative of naloxone, is a peripherally acting μ- opioid antagonist which can be orally administered. PEGylation confers substrate properties for P- glycoprotein transporter and thus limits the ability of naloxegol to cross the blood–brain barrier. A double- blind study in outpatients with non-cancer pain and OIC demonstrated that daily dose of 12.5 mg or 25 mg naloxegol improved bowel movement, as compared with placebo, without changing pain scores and daily opioid dose.<sup>606</sup> A randomized trial evaluated the effect of naloxegol on opioidinduced postoperative bladder dysfunction by measurement of residual urine volume, but no significant effect was found, and did not support use of naloxegol for postoperative urinary retention.<sup>607</sup>

<!-- PDF page 59 -->

### Naldemedine

Naldemedine is structurally related to naltrexone, and, at an oral dose of 0.2 mg, appeared to benefit patients with OIC and cancer irrespective of baseline characteristics.<sup>608</sup> It was also reported that naldemedine, at a dose of 0.2 mg, did not appear to affect analgesia or withdrawal. P- glycoprotein inhibitors (e.g., amiodarone, carvedilol, and cyclosporine) and strong and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., cimetidine, erythromycin, and verapamil) might raise plasma concentration of naldemedine.<sup>609</sup>

opioid- induced antinociception at the spinal level but inhibited at the supraspinal level.<sup>614</sup> Many studies in humans revealed benzodiazepine–opioid interactions to be synergistic (supra- additive). Fentanyl was found to synergize with midazolam in a dose- dependent fashion, in which combination of 25% of the fentanyl ED50 and 23% of the midazolam ED50 yielded ED50 of the combination to obtain inability to open eyes upon command in healthy women.<sup>615</sup> Response surface analysis realized that hypnotic interaction between alfentanil and midazolam was significantly synergistic.<sup>616</sup>

### Thiopental

## Drug Interactions with Opioids

### GENERAL PRINCIPLES

Opioids can interact with other drugs concomitantly administered for anesthesia. Although some of these interactions are intentionally sought, others are unwanted and adverse. There are three general types of mechanisms of drug interactions: pharmaceutical, pharmacokinetic, and pharmacodynamic.<sup>610</sup>

First, pharmaceutical interactions are chemical in nature, as illustrated when an alkaline solution of thiopental and an acidic solution of nondepolarizing muscle relaxants precipitate when simultaneously administered IV.

Second, pharmacokinetic interactions occur when the administration of one agent alters the pharmacokinetics or disposition of another agent. Hemodynamic changes induced by one agent can affect pharmacokinetic behavior of the other agent. Sufentanil, which has a greater hepatic extraction ratio than alfentanil, is more likely to be affected by decreases in hepatic blood flow. Cimetidine can prolong opioid effects by decreasing hepatic blood flow and/or diminishing hepatic metabolism. The CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 isoforms of the cytochrome P450 enzyme are responsible for the oxidative metabolism of opioids. Thus, drugs and foods that can interact with the cytochrome P450 system and cause either an increased activity (enzyme induction) or enzyme inhibition may affect pharmacokinetics of opioids<sup>611</sup> (Table 22.13). For example, it was reported that, in patients undergoing methadone maintenance treatment, drinking grapefruit juice resulted in reduction of CYP3A activity with a modest increase in peak level and decrease in clearance of methadone.<sup>612</sup>

Third, pharmacodynamic interaction occurs when the pharmacologic effect of one drug is altered by that of another drug, and is classified as synergistic, additive, or antagonistic in nature. The mechanism of interaction may occur at the same target or through alternate pathways. Pharmacodynamic interaction between opioids and inhaled anesthetics or propofol is important in performing balanced anesthesia or total intravenous anesthesia.

### SEDATIVE–HYPNOTICS

### Midazolam

Interactions between midazolam and opioids regarding hypnotic and antinociceptive effects have been analyzed. In dogs, concerning the antinociceptive effect, interaction between midazolam and fentanyl was reported to be less than additive.<sup>613</sup> In rats, midazolam enhanced

Thiopental and opioids are commonly administered together for induction of anesthesia. The Cp50 (asleep) of thiopental was 7.32 mcg/mL in the absence of fentanyl and 7.22 mcg/mL in the presence of fentanyl (1.27 ± 0.5 ng/mL), indicating that fentanyl, at an analgesic concentration, has no effect on the hypnotic concentration of thiopental.<sup>617</sup> Administration of alfentanil 10 mcg/kg decreased the thiopental ED50 from 2.6 mg/kg to 1.9 mg/kg for eye opening to voice command, but the mode of interaction was not examined.<sup>618</sup> Thus, it has not been clear whether the hypnotic interaction between thiopental and opioids is synergistic or not. Barbiturates can potentiate or produce hypotension if a large dose is administered with opioids. Hypotension after barbiturate–opioid combination is due to venodilatation, decreased cardiac filling, myocardial depression, and decreased sympathetic nervous system activity. Reducing induction doses of barbiturates administered concomitantly with opioids is recommended.

### Etomidate

Etomidate can be combined in low doses with opioids with little loss of cardiovascular stability. In patients scheduled for coronary artery bypass grafting, etomidate (0.25 mg/ kg) plus fentanyl (6 mcg/kg) resulted in less hypotension after induction and intubation than propofol (1 mg/kg) plus fentanyl (6 mcg/kg).<sup>619</sup> It was reported that myoclonic movements, a common problem during the induction of general anesthesia with etomidate, can be suppressed by sufentanil (0.3 mcg/kg).<sup>620</sup> A meta- analysis showed that pretreatment with remifentanil (1–2 mcg/kg) could be one operative option to reduce both incidence and severity of etomidate- induced myoclonus.<sup>621</sup>

### Ketamine

Many studies on combinations of opioids and ketamine, an NMDA receptor antagonist, have been reported to optimize analgesic therapy to a variety of painful conditions. Opioidinduced hyperalgesia and opioid tolerance can be prevented by ketamine in rats, suggesting the usefulness of combination of ketamine and opioids for postoperative analgesia. A pharmacologic study in mice suggested that endogenous opioids and μ- and δ- opioid receptors are also involved in ketamine- induced central antinociception but the κ- opioid receptor is not involved in this effect.<sup>622</sup> In a study examining the interaction of ketamine and remifentanil on the MAC of sevoflurane in rats, ketamine and remifentanil dose- dependently reduced the MAC, but adding the low dose of remifentanil to ketamine did not improve the MAC reduction, whereas the high dose of remifentanil enhanced MAC reduction by ketamine in a subadditive fashion.<sup>623</sup>

In the acute perioperative setting, results on interaction between opioids and ketamine vary depending on the clinical context. Combination of ketamine (2.5 or 10 mg IV) and alfentanil (0.25 or 1 mg IV) provided no advantage over a larger dose of either drug alone in relieving pain caused by intradermal capsaicin injection in healthy volunteers.<sup>624</sup> Furthermore, combination of ketamine 1 mg/mL and morphine 1 mg/mL for PCA did not provide benefit to patients undergoing major abdominal surgery.<sup>625</sup> In contrast, Lauretti reported that oral ketamine and transdermal nitroglycerin effectively reduced daily consumption of oral morphine in patients with cancer pain.<sup>626</sup> A prospective randomized double- blind controlled study demonstrated that intraoperative and postoperative administration of ketamine for the first 48 hours after surgery (2 mcg/kg/min after a 0.5 mg/kg bolus) improved postoperative analgesia with a significant decrease of morphine consumption.<sup>627</sup> Webb _et al._ reported that small- dose ketamine could be a useful addition to perioperative tramadol administration in patients undergoing abdominal surgery.<sup>628</sup> Methadone and ketamine, which are NMDA receptor antagonists, have been documented to facilitate postoperative pain control. Intraoperative methadone (0.2 mg/kg) and ketamine infusion (0.3 mg/kg/h intraoperatively and then 0.1 mg/kg/h for 48 hours postoperatively) significantly reduced postoperative pain and use of opioid.<sup>629</sup>

### Dexmedetomidine

Dexmedetomidine is a sedative with anxiolytic and amnestic effects that acts through binding to the α2- adrenoceptor. Dexmedetomidine provides significant sedation without cardiorespiratory compromise, and patients sedated with dexmedetomidine can be readily aroused. These features make dexmedetomidine useful in many procedures in combination with opioids. Previously, premedication with dexmedetomidine was shown to reduce requirement of fentanyl in abdominal hysterectomy, cholecystectomy, and intraocular surgery under general anesthesia.<sup>630</sup> In contrast, it was recently reported that, although addition of remifentanil slightly increased the depth of sedation by dexmedetomidine, it did not increase the probability of no response to name called, tolerance of shake and shout, tolerance of trapezius squeeze, and tolerance of laryngoscopy, suggesting the absence of significant synergistic interaction regarding hypnotic effect between dexmedetomidine and remifentanil.<sup>631</sup>

### MUSCLE RELAXANTS

Many factors alter the impact of muscle relaxants on hemodynamics when combined with opioids: the dose, timing, and rate of administration of each relaxant as well as the premedication, intravascular volume, left ventricular function, and presence of other drugs with autonomic nervous system actions.



::: details 原始表格與符號（PDF 第 61 頁）

![第 22 章，原始 PDF 第 61 頁](assets/chapter-22/source-page-0061.png)

:::

<!-- PDF page 62 -->

Pancuronium bromide has been frequently used for muscle relaxation during high- dose opioid anesthesia. The vagolytic action of pancuronium can attenuate opioidinduced bradycardia and support blood pressure. In patients undergoing coronary artery bypass grafting administered with sufentanil (3–8 mcg/kg), pancuronium (0.12 mg/kg) caused significant increase in mean arterial pressure, heart rate, and cardiac output but did not induce myocardial ischemia.<sup>632</sup> In patients scheduled for coronary artery bypass grafting administered with high- dose fentanyl (100 mcg/kg) and pancuronium (0.10 mg/kg), increase in heart rate and cardiac index occurred after induction of anesthesia and tracheal intubation, whereas patients administered with vecuronium (0.09 mg/kg) instead of pancuronium did not show increase in heart rate and cardiac index.<sup>633</sup>

Combinations of vecuronium and high doses of opioids may produce negative chronotropic and inotropic effects resulting in decreases in heart rate, cardiac output, and blood pressure and increases in the need for vasopressor support. In patients scheduled for coronary artery surgery administered with 40 mcg/kg sufentanil in combination with vecuronium (0.1 mg/kg), heart rate, mean arterial pressure, and systemic vascular resistance decreased from baseline values after tracheal intubation with neither significant change in cardiac output nor evidence of new myocardial ischemia.<sup>634</sup>

In patients anesthetized with fentanyl 50 mcg/kg and scheduled to undergo elective coronary artery bypass grafting, rocuronium 0.6 mg/kg (approximately equivalent to 2 × ED95 doses) was associated with changes of only small magnitude in hemodynamic variables, including increases in stroke volume index (+15%) and cardiac index (+11%), and a decrease in pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (−25%).<sup>635</sup> In patients undergoing coronary artery bypass surgery under fentanyl anesthesia, mivacurium 0.15 or 0.2 mg/kg produced decreases in mean arterial pressure and systemic vascular resistance index possibly mediated by histamine release, whereas atracurium (0.5 mg/kg) produced no significant hemodynamic changes.<sup>636</sup>

### MONOAMINE OXIDASE INHIBITORS

Monoamine oxidase inhibitors (MAOIs) can underlie the most serious and potentially fatal drug interaction with opioids. Combination of meperidine and MAOI can induce the serotonin syndrome, which is caused by excess serotonin availability in the CNS at the 5- HT1A receptor and characterized by confusion, fever, shivering, diaphoresis, ataxia, hyperreflexia, myoclonus, or diarrhea. The phenylpiperidine series opioids, including meperidine, tramadol, and methadone, appear to be weak serotonin reuptake inhibitors and have all been involved in serotonin toxicity reactions with MAOIs, whereas morphine, codeine, oxycodone, and buprenorphine are known not to be serotonin reuptake inhibitors, and do not precipitate serotonin toxicity with MAOIs.<sup>637</sup> Alfentanil and remifentanil could be used with MAOIs without complications.<sup>638,639</sup>

### CALCIUM CHANNEL BLOCKERS

Because opioids can inhibit voltage- dependent Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel activity through the activation of G proteins, it may be possible that opioid action is potentiated by Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel

blockers. Numerous animal studies and a few clinical studies have documented that opioid- induced analgesia is potentiated by L- type Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel blockers.<sup>640</sup> However, there is also a report that L- type Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel blockers, diltiazem, nimodipine, and verapamil do not potentiate morphine analgesia at clinically relevant doses in healthy volunteers.<sup>641</sup> N- type Ca<sup>2+</sup> channels are involved in the release of neurotransmitters from sensory neurons in the spinal cord. It was reported that intrathecal administration of a blocker of this channel, ω- conotoxin GVIA, produced antinociception, and interacts synergistically with opioids at the spinal cord level.<sup>642</sup>

Because both Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel blockers and various opioids are substrates of cytochrome CYP3A4, metabolism of these drugs can affect each other and their action can be prolonged in the case of coadministration.<sup>611</sup>

### MAGNESIUM

Magnesium has been shown to have antinociceptive effects probably due to its antagonistic action on the NMDA receptor. Intravenous administration of magnesium sulfate 50 mg/kg preoperatively and 8 mg/kg/h intraoperatively significantly reduced intra- and postoperative fentanyl requirement.<sup>643</sup> Because passage of magnesium across the blood–brain barrier is limited, intrathecal administration of magnesium was also tested. Intrathecal administration of fentanyl 25 mcg plus magnesium sulfate 50 mg provided significantly prolonged analgesia compared with fentanyl alone, in patients requesting analgesia for labor.<sup>644</sup>

Effectiveness of magnesium for prevention of opioidinduced hyperalgesia has been tested. A randomized, double- blind, prospective study showed that a relatively high dose of remifentanil (0.2 mcg/kg/min) enhances periincisional hyperalgesia in patients undergoing thyroidectomy, and intraoperative magnesium sulfate (30 mg/ kg at induction followed by 10 mg/kg/h) can prevent remifentanil- induced hyperalgesia.<sup>192</sup>

### NONSTEROIDAL ANTI- INFLAMMATORY DRUGS

Nonsteroidal anti- inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), such as ibuprofen, diclofenac, or ketorolac, have been perioperatively administered to reduce opioid requirement and prevent opioid- induced hyperalgesia and/or acute opioid tolerance. A randomized double- blind placebo- controlled study has shown that 8 mg of lornoxicam prevented increase in postoperative morphine consumption induced by intraoperative three bolus administrations of 1 mcg/kg fentanyl in female patients undergoing abdominal hysterectomy with spinal anesthesia.<sup>645</sup> It was reported that perioperative administration of diclofenac (75 mg twice daily) reduced morphine consumption and the incidence of adverse effects such as sedation and nausea after total abdominal hysterectomy.<sup>646</sup> In a randomized double- blind trial, 0.1 mg/kg morphine was shown to be superior to 30 mg ketorolac as postoperative pain relief, but adding ketorolac to morphine reduces postoperative opioid requirement and opioid- related side effects in the early postoperative period.<sup>647</sup> Through the reduction of opioid requirements, NSAIDs are thought to help prevent opioid- induced hyperalgesia and/or the development of acute opioid tolerance, although the possibility

<!-- PDF page 63 -->

that NSAIDs directly suppress opioid- induced hyperalgesia and acute opioid tolerance cannot be excluded.

### ACETAMINOPHEN (PARACETAMOL)

Acetaminophen has central analgesic and antipyretic effects that share similarity to those of NSAIDs but has weak peripheral anti- inflammatory action. Acetaminophen (basal infusion rate of 1.5 mg/kg/h) has been shown to have significant fentanyl- sparing effects and reduces side effects when combined with fentanyl (basal infusion rate of 0.25 mcg/kg/h) in intravenous parent- or nurse- controlled analgesia for postoperative pediatric pain management.<sup>648</sup> On the other hand, a randomized, placebo- controlled, double- blind study in children who received standard propofol–remifentanil anesthesia undergoing spine surgery showed that intravenously administered acetaminophen 90 mg/kg/day did improve analgesia, but did not diminish oxycodone consumption during 24 hours.<sup>649</sup> Because both acetaminophen and fentanyl are substrates of CYP3A4, their metabolism can affect each other.<sup>611</sup> However, an _in vitro_ study using human and rat liver microsome showed that therapeutic concentration of acetaminophen did not inhibit fentanyl metabolism, suggesting that harmful pharmacokinetic interaction between acetaminophen and fentanyl is unlikely.<sup>650</sup>

was demonstrated that perioperative administration of two doses of oral duloxetine 60 mg (2 hours before surgery and on the first postoperative day), a potent selective serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor, could reduce morphine requirement after knee replacement surgery.<sup>656</sup>

### LOCAL ANESTHETICS

Systemic administration of local anesthetics can significantly reduce pain and perioperative systemic lidocaine can significantly reduce opioid requirements.<sup>657</sup> A meta- analysis demonstrated that the use of intravenous lidocaine was associated with a significant reduction in intraoperative remifentanil consumption compared with that in the placebo group.<sup>658</sup> On the other hand, a previous study showed that the duration of spinal block by 100 mg hyperbaric preservative- free 5% lidocaine was much shorter in the opium abusers (60 ± 7 min) than in the nonabusers (83 ± 10 min).<sup>659</sup> In a study examining the effect of systemic morphine (seven daily subcutaneous injections of morphine 10 mg/kg) on the potency of lidocaine- induced block of the compound action potential in isolated rat sciatic nerves, it was shown that lidocaine potency was decreased due to the intrinsic changes in the peripheral nerve, and the loss of lidocaine potency remained 35 days after the last morphine injection.<sup>660</sup> Thus, there may be a decrease in potency of local anesthetics in patients on opioids.

### GABAPENTINOIDS

Gabapentinoids, such as gabapentin and pregabalin, structural analogues of γ- aminobutyric acid, bind to the α2δ subunit of spinal voltage- gated Ca<sup>2+</sup> channel, and produce antihyperalgesic effects in neuropathic and other painful conditions. A volunteer study demonstrated that gabapentin (600 mg) enhanced the acute analgesic effect of morphine (60 mg, controlled release), and that the plasma concentration of gabapentin was increased when morphine was administered concomitantly.<sup>651</sup> This result suggested that both pharmacodynamic and pharmacokinetic interaction between morphine and gabapentin leads to increased analgesic effects. In rodents, it was shown that systemic and intrathecal application of gabapentin prevents the development of opioid- induced hyperalgesia.<sup>652</sup> Preoperative administration of pregabalin is proposed as a promising way of enhancing postoperative pain control. A randomized, triple- blinded, placebo- controlled study demonstrated that preoperative administration of 300 mg pregabalin in patients undergoing transperitoneal nephrectomy reduces postoperative opioid consumption and decreases the area of mechanical hyperalgesia.<sup>653</sup>

### β - ADRENORECEPTOR ANTAGONISTS

Previous studies suggested that esmolol may modulate the perioperative pain response and reduce anesthetic requirements. A recent meta- analysis demonstrates that intraoperative administration of esmolol (0.5 mg/kg, then 0.05–30 mcg/ kg/min) reduces both intraoperative and postoperative opioid consumption, with no change in postoperative pain scores.<sup>661</sup> The mechanism by which esmolol modulates the pain response and opioid consumption is not clear.

### ANTIDEPRESSANTS

Antidepressants are widely used to treat various chronic inflammatory and neuropathic pain conditions. Animal studies have suggested the presence of interaction between antidepressants and opioids. An isobolographic analysis demonstrated that systemic amitriptyline and morphine synergistically inhibit cutaneous orofacial inflammatory pain in rats.<sup>654</sup> Pretreatment with amitriptyline increases morphine- induced hypercarbia through pharmacodynamic processes in rabbits, suggesting that morphine doses should be reduced with careful titration, if patients are receiving concomitant treatment with tricyclic antidepressants.<sup>655</sup> It
